The Complete 2008 Honda Fit Book

Everything a new driver and a worried parent need to know to drive, own, fix, and even race a 2008 Honda Fit.

Disclaimer

This book is not affiliated with Honda. Working on cars can injure you. Follow your owner's manual when it disagrees with this book.

How to use this book

This book is about one specific car: the 2008 Honda Fit, US base trim, first-generation chassis code GD3. Honda sold a fancier Fit Sport trim that year too, and a completely different second-generation Fit started in 2009. Neither of those is this book. If your Fit is a Sport trim, or a 2009 model or newer, some of what is here still applies, but a lot of it does not. Check Chapter 2 to see exactly what your base trim does and does not have, and do not assume a part number, a torque value, or a step in this book is correct for any Fit other than the 2007–2008 GD3 base car.

This book covers both transmissions Honda offered on the 2008 Fit: the 5-speed manual and the 5-speed automatic. Wherever the two work differently, the book says so and walks through both, usually in side-by-side sections. If a chapter does not mention a transmission difference, the information applies to both.

How the sourcing works. Every fact, number, and procedure in this book was checked against a real source before it was written down: Honda's own owner's manual, government safety databases, published repair manuals, or (when nothing better existed) the Honda Fit owner community. Each sentence that carries a specific claim has an invisible tag behind it in the file this book was built from, a short code like F-WP01-003 for a fact or P-WP11-8 for a repair procedure. You will never see these codes on the printed page; they are hidden markers, not footnote numbers. What they mean for you is this: nothing in this book was made up. If you ever want to check exactly where a claim came from, the codes tie every sentence back to a real, dated source.

Two phrases in this book flag information that deserves a little extra caution:

  • "Owners report that..." This phrase means the information came from the Honda Fit owner community, not from Honda or a government agency. It is only used when at least two separate owners or forum sources said the same thing independently. It is usually reliable, but it has not been confirmed by Honda's own paperwork, so treat it as a strong lead rather than a guarantee.
  • UNVERIFIED. When this book could not find a trustworthy source for a specific number or claim, even after a real search, it says so plainly instead of guessing. You will see a note like "We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, a dealer, or a repair shop before you rely on this." Guessing about a car is how people get hurt or waste money; this book would rather tell you it does not know.

How the book is organized. The book has ten parts. Part I introduces the car and helps you confirm exactly which Fit you own. Part II covers the basics: keys, seats, starting the car, and every control and gauge. Part III teaches you to actually drive it, in good conditions and bad. Part IV covers the paperwork side of ownership. Part V is the maintenance you can do yourself, from an oil change on up. Part VI teaches you to diagnose a problem before you pay anyone to look at it. Part VII walks through bigger repairs at home. Part VIII is for readers who want to modify the car, and Part IX is for readers who want to take it to an autocross lot or a track day. Part X, the appendices, gathers the book's reference tables (specs, torque values, fuse maps, part numbers, a glossary, and more) into one place you can flip to quickly instead of hunting through a chapter.

Read the parts in order if this is your first car. Jump straight to the part you need if it is not.

Part IMeet Your Fit

Chapter 1What This Car Is

What you will learn

  • Why Honda built the Fit, and what kind of car it is.
  • Why the year and the generation of your Fit matter.
  • What a chassis code is, and which one your car has.
  • Where your 2008 Fit was built, and which trims Honda sold in the US that year.

The short version

Your car is a 2008 Honda Fit, base trim. Honda built it at its plant in Suzuka, Japan. In the US, Honda sold only two versions of the 2008 Fit: the base Fit and the Fit Sport. Your car belongs to the first generation of the Fit, a car with the internal code GD3. It is not the same car as the Fit that came after it.

The full explanation

A small car built to hold a lot

The Honda Fit is a small hatchback, a car with a rear door that swings up instead of a trunk lid. Honda built the Fit around a 1.5-liter engine, a size measurement that tells you how much air and fuel the engine's cylinders can hold. Later chapters cover the engine, the size of the car, and how far a tank of gas takes you. For now, the key point is this: the Fit was built to be small on the outside but roomy on the inside. It was also built to use less fuel than a bigger car.

Honda's engine for this car carries the code L15A1. An engine code is a short label a manufacturer uses to identify one specific engine design, separate from any other engine that company builds. You will see this code on parts boxes and repair information, so it is worth remembering.

Why the generation of your Fit matters

A chassis code is a short label Honda uses inside the company to identify one specific version of a car's structure and parts. Your 2008 Fit carries the chassis code GD3. Every part built for this car, from body panels to engine mounts, was designed around that GD3 structure.

The safety-testing group IIHS states that the Fit "was introduced in the 2007 model year" in the US. That means the GD3 Fit was sold new in the US only for the 2007 and 2008 model years. A model year is the year Honda assigns to a specific version of a car. It does not always match the calendar year the car was actually built or sold.

Honda later replaced this car with a new design. That newer Fit used a different chassis code and different parts throughout, even though it kept the same name. This book covers only the first-generation, 2008, GD3 Fit. If your car is a Fit built for model year 2009 or later, the two cars share a name, but they do not share a design. Do not use this book's specific numbers, part information, or procedures for that later car. Whenever this book gives you a number, a part, or a step, it is speaking about the 2008 GD3 Fit only.

Base trim and Fit Sport

Honda's 2008 US lineup for the Fit had exactly two trims, a trim being one specific equipment level of a car that a manufacturer sells under one name. Those two trims were the base Fit and the Fit Sport. This book is written for the base Fit. Chapter 2 walks through exactly what the base trim has, and what it does not have compared with the Fit Sport.

Where your car was built

Honda built the 2008 Fit at its plant in Suzuka, in Japan's Mie Prefecture. Every 2008 US-market Fit, base and Sport alike, came from that same plant.

Quick check

  1. What chassis code does a 2008 base Fit have?
  2. In what city and country was your 2008 Fit built?
  3. How many US trims did Honda sell for the 2008 Fit, and what were they called?
  4. Why does it matter that your Fit is a first-generation car, not the generation that came after it?

Chapter 2Identify Your Exact Car

What you will learn

  • How to read your car's VIN, the Vehicle Identification Number that names your exact car.
  • Where to find the metal plates and stickers that identify your Fit.
  • Exactly what your base Fit has, and what it does not have compared with a Fit Sport.
  • What Honda knows, and does not know for sure, about your car's paint color code.

The short version

Your car's VIN is a 17-character code that identifies your exact Fit. You can find that VIN on a plate at the top of your dashboard, on a label inside the driver's doorjamb, and stamped into the engine compartment. A base Fit and a Fit Sport look similar, but the base trim is missing several features the Sport has, such as alloy wheels, fog lights, and cruise control.

The full explanation

Reading your VIN

A VIN, short for Vehicle Identification Number, is a 17-character code that no two cars in the world share. Reading a VIN means breaking that code into groups of characters, where each group tells you something different about the car.

For your 2008 base Fit, here is what the groups mean:

VIN positions What they tell you Value for your car
1-3 WMI, the World Manufacturer Identifier JHM = Honda Motor Co., Ltd., Japan, passenger car
4-6 Platform or chassis code GD3, the first-generation Fit structure
10 Model year code The digit "8" marks a 2008 model
11 Assembly plant code "S" marks the Suzuka, Japan plant
12-17 Serial number A running production number unique to your car

If you want to decode your own real VIN with full confidence, use the official decoder at the NHTSA website. NHTSA stands for the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. Hand-counting the characters yourself is a helpful way to understand the pattern. But the government tool checks the whole code, including a check digit that confirms the VIN is valid.

Where to find the VIN on your car

Honda puts your VIN in three places. The easiest one to check is a metal plate fastened to the top of the dashboard. You can see this plate by looking through the windshield from outside, on the driver's side. The VIN also appears on the certification label inside the driver's doorjamb, printed both as text and as a barcode. It is also stamped into the engine compartment's bulkhead, the wall that separates the engine bay from the passenger cabin.

The sticker that tells you your tire information

That same doorjamb area holds a second important label. This one lists the tire sizes and cold tire pressures Honda recommends for your car. "Cold" means the tires have not been driven on recently, since driving heats up the air inside a tire and raises its pressure reading. Check this label, not a number you remember or a tire shop's default setting, when you set your tire pressure.

What base got, and what base did not get

Honda sold your 2008 Fit as one of two US trims: the plain "Fit," which this book calls base, and the "Fit Sport." Both trims came standard with air conditioning, power windows, power door locks, and power mirrors. Both also came with the 60/40 split "Magic Seat" rear seat system, which lets you fold or flip the rear seats into several different cargo configurations. The base Fit's factory tires were sized P175/65R14, mounted on 14-inch steel wheels with full wheel covers; the Fit Sport instead used a larger, wider tire, P195/55R15, on alloy wheels.

Compared with the Fit Sport, the base Fit did not come with any of the following:

  • Paddle shifters mounted on the steering wheel, available only on Sport models with the automatic transmission.
  • An underbody aero kit and a rear roofline spoiler.
  • Fog lights.
  • A remote keyless entry system with a security system.
  • Cruise control.
  • 15-inch aluminum alloy wheels.
  • The upgraded 200-watt, six-speaker audio system with an auxiliary audio jack for plugging in an outside music player; base cars instead came with a four-speaker AM/FM/CD system.

One research note worth passing along: a Canadian-market Honda specification page describes a similar "base" trim as including an auxiliary audio jack, which conflicts with the US information above. This may reflect a real difference between the US and Canadian versions of the same trim, but it could not be confirmed either way. If your factory radio has an auxiliary jack and everything else here matches your car, that is not necessarily a mistake in this book.

Paint colors

Honda's own announcement for the 2008 Fit states that two colors were new that year: Tidewater Blue Metallic and Blackberry Pearl. A parts and pricing website lists seven total exterior colors for the 2008 Fit. They are:

  • Milano Red
  • Storm Silver Metallic
  • Tidewater Blue Metallic
  • Vivid Blue Pearl
  • Blackberry Pearl
  • Blaze Orange Metallic
  • Nighthawk Black Pearl

A paint code is a short factory code that identifies one specific paint color, and body shops use it to mix a matching paint. Some paint codes were found for these colors, but they came from a general Honda color reference that was not confirmed as accurate specifically for the 2008 Fit. Do not order paint by a code from this book. Instead, check the paint code sticker on your own driver's doorjamb, the same area that holds your VIN and tire information, and match that code exactly.

The emissions label

We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual.

Quick check

  1. What does "VIN" stand for, and how many characters does it have?
  2. Where are the three places Honda put the VIN on your car?
  3. Name three features the Fit Sport had that the base Fit did not.
  4. Where should you check for your car's real, confirmed paint code, instead of trusting a general list?

Chapter 3Specifications at a Glance

What you will learn

  • The exact engine numbers for your 2008 base Fit.
  • The size and weight of your car, and how much it can carry.
  • How many miles per gallon the EPA (Environmental Protection Agency) rated your Fit for.
  • Your car's original price, and how it did in crash testing.

The short version

Your 2008 base Fit uses a 1.5-liter engine that makes 109 horsepower. It is a small car, under 158 inches long, that still holds over 21 cubic feet of cargo with the rear seats up. Honda sold it new for $13,950 with a manual transmission or $14,750 with an automatic.

The full explanation

Engine

Horsepower is a measurement of how much work an engine can do over time. Torque is a measurement of an engine's twisting force, the pulling power you feel when you accelerate. RPM stands for revolutions per minute, or how many full turns the engine makes every minute. Compression ratio compares the space inside a cylinder before and after the piston squeezes the air-fuel mixture; a higher number means a tighter squeeze. Bore and stroke describe the width of each cylinder and the distance its piston travels. Cc stands for cubic centimeters, a way of measuring the total volume of all the cylinders together. Redline is the highest RPM the engine is designed to reach safely.

Spec Value
Engine code L15A1
Displacement 1.5 L (1,497 cc)
Horsepower 109 hp @ 5,800 rpm
Torque 105 lb-ft @ 4,800 rpm
Compression ratio 10.4:1
Bore x stroke 73.0 mm x 89.4 mm
Required fuel Regular unleaded gasoline
Redline 6,500 rpm

Honda's own specification sheet names the required fuel as "Regular Unleaded" without giving an exact minimum octane number, a number that measures a gasoline's resistance to knocking. Standard US regular-grade gasoline is commonly labeled 87 octane. But no source specific to this car states that number, so this book does not claim it as confirmed for your Fit.

Transmission

A gear ratio is the number of times the engine turns for every one turn of the wheels in a given gear. A higher ratio gives more pulling power at low speed, and a lower ratio saves fuel at speed. Your base Fit could be ordered with a 5-speed manual transmission, which was standard, or an optional 5-speed automatic transmission.

Gear Manual ratio Automatic ratio
1st 3.462 2.996
2nd 1.870 1.679
3rd 1.321 1.067
4th 0.970 0.756
5th 0.757 0.550
Reverse 3.231 1.957
Final drive 4.29 4.56

Weight and size

Curb weight is how much the car weighs by itself, full of fuel and fluids, but with no passengers or cargo aboard. Wheelbase is the distance between the center of the front wheel and the center of the rear wheel. Ground clearance is the gap between the lowest part of the car and the ground.

Spec Value
Curb weight, manual 2,432 lb
Curb weight, automatic 2,514 lb
Length 157.4 in
Width 66.2 in
Height 60.0 in
Wheelbase 96.5 in
Ground clearance We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual.

Cargo and fuel

Cargo volume is the amount of space, measured in cubic feet, available for luggage and other items.

Spec Value
Cargo volume, rear seats up 21.3 cu ft
Cargo volume, rear seats folded down 56.8 cu ft
Fuel tank capacity 10.8 gal (41 L)

A few other sources gave different numbers for cargo volume with the seats folded down, ranging from about 42 to about 59 cubic feet. This likely happened because different companies measure cargo space using different methods. This book uses the number from Honda's own 2008 US specification sheet, 56.8 cubic feet, as the most trustworthy figure for your car.

Fuel economy

The EPA rates every new car's fuel economy in miles per gallon, written as MPG, so shoppers can compare cars on a level basis. City MPG estimates driving in stop-and-go traffic; highway MPG estimates steady-speed driving.

Transmission City MPG Highway MPG
5-speed manual 28 34
5-speed automatic 27 34

Tires and wheels

Your base Fit's factory tire size is P175/65R14 81S, mounted on a 14-inch steel wheel, 5.5 inches wide, with a full wheel cover.

Crash test ratings

NHTSA is the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, and IIHS is the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety. They are the two US groups that crash-test new cars. Both publish star or letter ratings so buyers can compare safety.

NHTSA rated the 2008 Fit this way, on a 1-to-5-star scale where 5 stars is the best score:

Test Rating
Frontal crash, driver side 5 stars
Frontal crash, passenger side 5 stars
Side crash, driver side 5 stars
Side crash, passenger side 3 stars
Rollover 4 stars (13.3% rollover risk)

Rollover risk is NHTSA's estimate of how likely a car is to roll over in a single-vehicle crash; a lower percentage is safer. NHTSA did not publish a single combined overall star rating for the 2008 Fit, because NHTSA did not start using one combined score until testing for the 2011 model year.

IIHS graded the same car on a scale of Good, Acceptable, Marginal, and Poor:

Test Rating
Moderate overlap front crash Good
Side crash Good
Head restraints and seats (whiplash protection) Poor

IIHS states that these results apply to both the 2007 and 2008 model years, since the two years share the same structure. IIHS had not yet created its small overlap front crash test when your Fit was built, so no small overlap rating exists for this car.

Original price

MSRP stands for Manufacturer's Suggested Retail Price, the price a manufacturer recommends a dealer charge before any negotiation, taxes, or fees.

Transmission MSRP
5-speed manual $13,950
5-speed automatic $14,750

These prices exclude tax, license, registration, and a $670 destination charge, the fee a manufacturer adds to cover shipping the car to the dealer. Actual dealer prices could vary.

Quick check

  1. How many horsepower does your Fit's engine make, and at what RPM?
  2. What is the difference between curb weight and cargo volume?
  3. Which transmission gets better city fuel economy, the manual or the automatic?
  4. What was your Fit's original MSRP with a manual transmission, and what does MSRP stand for?
  5. Why does this book not give you a confirmed ground clearance number?

Part IIThe Absolute Basics

Chapter 4Keys, locks, and getting in

What you will learn

  • How the key and the anti-theft chip inside it work
  • Why your Fit does not have a remote fob, and how to lock and unlock it instead
  • How to use the child safety lock on the back doors
  • How to find the fuel door and the tailgate (the rear hatch door) release
  • How to open the hood safely, step by step

The short version

Your base 2008 Fit uses a plain metal key, not a fob, and that key has a hidden chip that talks to the car's anti-theft system. You lock and unlock the car with the key, a small tab on each door, or a power switch on the driver's door. The hood, fuel door, and tailgate (the rear hatch, since the Fit is a hatchback and has no separate trunk) each open a different way. This chapter walks you through all of them.

The full explanation

The key and the immobilizer

Your car came with two metal keys and a small tag with a key number on it, which you use to order a new key later. Each key has a chip built into it. This chip talks to your car's immobilizer, a system that stops the engine from starting unless it senses the right chip. The key has no battery, but a damaged chip still stops the car from starting.

Some Fit models come with a remote transmitter, a small fob you press to lock or unlock the car from a distance. Your base Fit does not have one. That feature is built into the Sport trim only. On your car, you lock and unlock every door using the key or the switches inside the car.

Locking and unlocking the doors

Every door has a small tab on top. Push the tab in to lock that door, and pull it out to unlock it. Your Fit also has power door locks, which use an electric switch instead of your hand at each door. On the driver's door, a master switch locks every door and the tailgate at once. Press the top of the switch to lock them, and press the bottom to unlock them. Turning the key in the driver's door lock also works. The first turn locks or unlocks only the driver's door. The second turn locks or unlocks the rest of the car. One safety note: the master switch will not work while the driver's door is open with the key in the ignition. The ignition is the slot where you turn the key to start the engine.

The child safety lock

Each rear door has a small lock lever near its rear edge. Set that lever to LOCK, and the rear door cannot open from inside, no matter how the regular door lock tab is set. The door still opens normally from outside using the outer handle. Use this lock any time a child rides in the back seat.

The fuel door

Your base Fit's fuel door has no lever or cable inside the car. To open it, walk around to the side of the car and pull on the notched edge of the fuel door itself. The fuel cap, the small cap that seals the gas tank opening, stays attached to the car by a short tether. This means you cannot drop it or lose it while refueling.

The tailgate

The Fit is a hatchback, so its rear cargo door is called the tailgate, not a trunk. It opens only from the outside: pull its handle, then lift it up. It has no separate button or lever inside the car. Instead, it locks and unlocks together with the driver's door whenever you use the key, the door lock tab, or the master power-lock switch. On your base trim, you can also lock or unlock the tailgate directly with the key.

Step by step: Open the hood

Safety warnings

  • Always park on level, flat ground and set the parking brake before you open the hood.
  • Always let the engine cool before you open the hood if you recently finished driving.
  • Do not open the hood if you see steam or spray coming from underneath it. Wait until it stops.
  • Always check that the support rod, the metal rod that holds the hood open, is properly seated in its hole before you let go of the hood.

Steps

  1. Park on level ground, turn off the engine, and set the parking brake.
  2. Reach under the lower-left corner of the dashboard, near your left knee, and pull the hood release handle.
  3. The hood will pop up slightly but stay held by a second, hidden latch.
  4. Walk to the front of the car and slide your fingers under the hood's front edge, near the center.
  5. Feel for the second latch handle and push it to release the hood.
  6. Lift the hood the rest of the way open.
  7. Pull the metal support rod free from its clip under the hood.
  8. Fit the tip of the support rod into the matching hole on the underside of the hood.
  9. Let go of the hood slowly to make sure the rod is holding its weight.

To close the hood

  1. Lift the hood slightly to free the support rod from its hole, then clip the rod back in place.
  2. Lower the hood to about one foot above the front fenders, the body panels over the front wheels, then let it drop the rest of the way.
  3. Push down lightly on the hood to confirm it caught and is fully latched.

How you know it worked: The hood stays open on its own, held only by the rod, with no need for you to hold it up. When closed, it sits flush and does not move when you push down on it.

What can go wrong

  • You pull only the interior release and never find the second hood latch, so the hood will not lift.
  • You let go of an open hood before the support rod is properly seated in its hole.
  • You slam the hood shut from too low, instead of letting it drop the last foot on its own.

Quick check

  1. What part inside your key allows the car to start, even though the key has no battery?
  2. Does your base 2008 Fit come with a remote fob to lock and unlock the doors? Why or why not?
  3. How do you open the fuel door on your Fit?
  4. What is the correct name for the Fit's rear cargo door, and how many ways can you unlock it?
  5. Name one safety warning you must follow before you open the hood.

Chapter 5Sitting right

What you will learn

  • How to slide and recline the driver's seat correctly
  • How to tilt the steering wheel and adjust the head restraints (headrests)
  • How your power mirrors work, and why you set them before you drive
  • How to fasten your seat belt so it protects you correctly
  • The full setup routine to run every time you get in, before you start driving

The short version

Before you drive, you slide your seat, tilt the steering wheel, set your head restraints, aim your mirrors, and fasten your seat belt correctly. Your base Fit has power mirrors and a tilt-only steering wheel, so you use switches and a lever instead of pushing on things by hand. Doing all of this before you move keeps you safe, because these adjustments protect you in a crash and help you see the road.

The full explanation

The seat

The driver's seat slides forward and backward using a lever under the front edge of the seat cushion. Lift the lever, slide the seat to where you want it, then let go and rock the seat to confirm it caught. A second lever on the side of the seat-back, the part of the seat behind your back, tilts that backrest angle. Pull the lever out, lean the seat-back to the angle you want, and release the lever to lock it. The rear outer seat-backs recline too, using a release lever on the outside edge of each one. The owner's manual does not give an exact travel distance or a number of notches for either seat adjustment. Reclining your seat-back too far is dangerous, because a reclined seat-back stops your seat belt from holding you properly in a crash.

The steering wheel

Your base Fit's steering wheel tilts up and down, but it does not telescope, meaning it does not slide toward or away from you. To adjust it, push the lever under the steering column, the part of the steering assembly under the wheel, all the way down to unlock it. Move the wheel to an angle where you can still see all the gauges, the dials in front of you, then push the lever back up to lock it.

The head restraints

Every seating position has a head restraint, the padded part above the seat-back that supports your head. The two front head restraints slide up and down. Pull straight up to raise one, or press its release button sideways while you push it down to lower it. Always use both hands, and never do this while driving. Line up the middle of the back of your head with the center of the restraint. The rear outer head restraints do not adjust for height; they only lift out for cleaning after you press a release button.

The mirrors

Your base Fit has power mirrors on the outside, not manual ones you push by hand. With the ignition on, use a selector switch to pick the driver's or the passenger's mirror, then use a second switch to tilt that mirror up, down, left, or right. Return the selector switch to its center, off position when you finish, so the setting locks in place. The inside rear-view mirror also has a day and night flip tab that cuts glare from headlights behind you at night.

The seat belt

To buckle up, push the metal latch plate into the buckle and tug on it to confirm it caught. Press the red button on the buckle when you want to release it. For a correct fit, pull the lap portion down low across your hip bones first. Then pull the shoulder portion up to remove slack, so it crosses the middle of your chest and shoulder, not your neck or arm. If the belt still crosses your neck or arm, change the shoulder anchor height instead. Each front shoulder belt anchor, the fixed point where the belt attaches near your shoulder, slides up and down a track with four height positions. Squeeze the two small buttons on the anchor to unlock it and slide it. This height adjuster exists only on the front seats, not the rear ones.

Step by step: Adjust your seat and mirrors before driving

Safety warnings

  • Always make every seat, head restraint, mirror, and steering wheel adjustment before you start driving, never while the vehicle is moving.
  • Always sit as far back from the steering wheel as you safely can while still reaching the controls. Sitting too close raises your injury risk if the front airbag inflates.
  • Do not recline the seat-back too far. A reclined seat-back stops the seat belt from holding you properly in a crash.
  • Always confirm the seat and seat-back are locked in position by rocking them before you drive.

Steps

  1. Sit in the seat and pull up the lever under the front edge of the seat cushion.
  2. Slide the seat to a position where you can fully press the pedals, then release the lever.
  3. Rock the seat forward and back to make sure it is locked in place.
  4. Pull the lever on the side of the seat-back and lean to a comfortable, upright angle.
  5. Release the lever to lock the seat-back, then rock it to confirm it caught.
  6. Pull straight up on each front head restraint, or press its side button and push down, until it is level with the middle of your head.
  7. Push the lever under the steering column down to unlock the wheel.
  8. Move the wheel up or down until you can see all the gauges clearly, then push the lever back up to lock it.
  9. Try moving the wheel to confirm it is locked in place.
  10. Flip the tab under the inside mirror to day mode, then tilt it for a clear view out the back window.
  11. Turn the ignition to the ON position and move the mirror selector switch to the driver's side.
  12. Press the edges of the mirror adjustment switch until that mirror shows a clear view, then repeat for the passenger's side mirror.
  13. Return the mirror selector switch to its center, off position so the setting is not changed by accident.
  14. Fasten your seat belt, then pull the lap portion low and snug and the shoulder portion across your chest.
  15. If the belt crosses your neck or arm, squeeze the two buttons on the shoulder anchor and slide it to a better height.

How you know it worked: You can fully press the pedals and see every gauge. Both outside mirrors show a clear view without you leaning, and the shoulder belt rests across the middle of your chest, not your neck.

What can go wrong

  • You adjust the mirrors, seat, or steering wheel after you already started driving.
  • You leave the seat too close to the steering wheel.
  • You forget to lock the seat-back angle lever.
  • You leave the mirror selector switch turned on instead of centering it, which can let the setting drift.

Quick check

  1. What could happen in a crash if you recline your seat-back too far?
  2. What do you push under the steering column to unlock its tilt adjustment?
  3. Why does your base Fit use switches, not your hand, to aim the outside mirrors?
  4. How many height positions does the front shoulder belt anchor have?
  5. Name two adjustments you must always make before driving, never while driving.

Chapter 6Starting and stopping the car

What you will learn

  • The four positions of the ignition switch, the slot where you turn the key to start the car
  • How to start a manual transmission car, step by step
  • How to start an automatic transmission car, step by step
  • What the immobilizer light means and what to do if it blinks
  • How to release the shift lock on an automatic if the shifter will not move out of Park

The short version

Before you turn the key, you set the parking brake and check that the shifter is in the right spot. On a manual, you press the clutch pedal all the way down first. On an automatic, you press the brake pedal and confirm the shift lever is in Park. Once the engine starts, ease off the accelerator, the gas pedal, so the engine does not race.

The full explanation

The ignition switch

Your Fit's ignition switch has four positions. LOCK is where you insert or remove the key; on an automatic, the shift lever must also be in Park to turn the key to LOCK. ACCESSORY runs the audio system and the accessory power socket. ON is the normal driving position, and switching from ACCESSORY to ON lights up several dashboard indicators briefly as a test. START is spring-loaded and used only to start the engine; it springs back to ON when you let go.

A reminder beeper sounds if you open the driver's door while the key is left in LOCK or ACCESSORY, and removing the key stops it. On an automatic, the shift lever must be in Park before you can remove the key. Never remove the key while driving. Doing so locks the steering wheel and can cause you to lose control of the car.

Before you start driving, the owner's manual's own pre-drive checklist tells you to check the gauges and indicators on the instrument panel. The instrument panel is the panel of dials and lights in front of you. The same checklist also covers your mirrors, seat position, seat belts, and closed doors.

Safety warning (general knowledge, true of any gasoline engine, not a Honda-specific fact): Never start or run the engine in a closed garage or other enclosed space. A running engine produces carbon monoxide, a gas you cannot see or smell that can kill you in minutes. Always start the car outdoors or with the garage door fully open.

Starting a manual transmission Fit

  1. Apply the parking brake.
  2. In cold weather, turn off electrical accessories to reduce battery drain.
  3. Press the clutch pedal all the way to the floor. The START position will not work unless the clutch pedal is fully pressed.
  4. Without touching the accelerator, turn the key to START.
  5. Do not hold START for more than 15 seconds at a time. If the engine does not start, wait at least 10 seconds before you try again.
  6. Once the engine starts, ease off the accelerator so it does not race.

If the engine still does not start after 15 seconds, or it starts and stalls right away, repeat the START step with the accelerator pressed about halfway down. If it still will not start, the engine may be flooded, meaning too much fuel has reached it. Press the accelerator all the way down and hold it there while starting; if that does not work, go back to the halfway-down step. Cold weather and altitudes above 8,000 feet (2,400 m) make starting harder.

Starting an automatic transmission Fit

  1. Apply the parking brake.
  2. In cold weather, turn off electrical accessories to reduce battery drain.
  3. Make sure the shift lever is in Park, and press the brake pedal.
  4. Without touching the accelerator, turn the key to START.
  5. Do not hold START for more than 15 seconds at a time. If the engine does not start, wait at least 10 seconds before you try again.
  6. Once the engine starts, ease off the accelerator so it does not race.

The flooding and hard-starting notes above apply the same way on an automatic.

The automatic transmission's shift positions

Your base automatic Fit has these shift lever positions: P (Park), R (Reverse), N (Neutral), and D (Drive, which uses gears one through five on its own). It also has D3, which is like D but locks out 4th and 5th gear, useful for engine braking downhill or in stop-and-go traffic. Position 2 locks 2nd gear, for climbing power, engine braking, or slippery starts, and position 1 locks 1st gear. An "S" position and paddle shifters exist only on the Sport trim, not on your base car.

Releasing the shift lock

Normally, you move the automatic shift lever out of Park by pressing the brake pedal and pressing the lever's release button. If that does not work, your Fit has a shift lock release slot, a hidden covered slot in the shift lever console, next to the lever's release button. To use it, set the parking brake and remove the key from the ignition. Protect the notch of the slot's cover with a cloth, then carefully pry the cover off with a small flat-tip screwdriver or a metal fingernail file. Insert the key into the slot, push down on the key while you press the shift lever's release button, and move the lever out of Park to Neutral. Remove the key from the slot, reinstall the cover with its notch on the left side, press the brake pedal, and restart the engine. Needing this procedure means your car is developing a problem, so have it checked by a dealer.

The manual transmission's shift pattern

Your 5-speed manual is synchronized in all forward gears, meaning the gears mesh smoothly without extra effort from you. It has a mechanical lockout that prevents shifting directly from 5th gear into reverse. Always press the clutch pedal all the way down when you shift. Do not rest your foot on the clutch pedal when you are not shifting, since that wears the clutch faster. Come to a complete stop before you shift into reverse; shifting into reverse while moving can damage the transmission. Pause briefly with the clutch down, or pass through a forward gear for a moment, before you engage reverse, to stop the gears from grinding.

The immobilizer light

The key symbol on your dashboard is the immobilizer system indicator. At key-ON, it should light briefly, then go off, confirming the car recognized your key's chip. If it blinks instead, the system did not recognize your key. Turn the key to LOCK, remove it, reinsert it, and turn to ON again. Another key or metal object near the ignition switch can also cause this, so keep spare keys and fobs away from the switch while starting. If it keeps blinking, call the dealer. The light also blinks a few times when you turn the key from ON down to ACCESSORY or LOCK; that is normal, not a fault. Never alter the immobilizer system or add devices to it, and always take your key with you when you leave the car.

Quick check

  1. Name the four positions of the ignition switch, in order.
  2. What must you do with your foot before turning the key to START on a manual? What about on an automatic?
  3. What should you do if the engine has not started after holding START for 15 seconds?
  4. Where is the shift lock release slot, and when would you need to use it?
  5. If the immobilizer light blinks instead of going off, what are your first three steps?

Chapter 7The dashboard explained

What you will learn

  • What every warning light on your base 2008 Fit's dashboard means
  • What to do for each light, and whether it is safe to keep driving
  • Which lights described in the owner's manual you will never see on your base car
  • What each gauge and readout on your instrument panel, the panel of dials and lights in front of you, shows you

The short version

Your instrument panel has 20 warning lights and messages that apply to your base Fit, and this chapter lists every one. Some lights only give you information, some tell you to schedule a repair soon, and a few tell you to pull over right away. Learn this table before your first drive, because it is the page you will come back to most.

The full explanation

How many lights does your Fit have

The owner's manual describes 26 named indicators across US and Canadian Fit models. Six of those do not apply to a US base Fit, leaving 20 that you may actually see. The immobilizer system indicator, the key-shaped light that confirms your key's anti-theft chip was recognized, is covered in Chapter 6, since it comes up during starting.

The lights you will never see

Your base Fit does not have six indicators that the manual describes, because they belong to other trims or other countries. These are the fog light indicator (Sport only) and the security system indicator (Sport only, since base has no remote entry system). They also include the cruise main and cruise control indicators, found only on Sport and Canadian LX models, since base has no cruise control. The last two are the daytime running lights indicator and the washer level indicator, both Canadian models only. If your base car ever shows one of these six, something aftermarket, meaning added after the factory build, is probably involved.

The warning light table

Light What it means What you do Safe to keep driving?
Malfunction Indicator Lamp (MIL), also called the check engine light — an engine-block icon An emission-control part may have a problem, or the fuel cap is loose or missing Check the fuel cap first and tighten it until it clicks. If the light stays on or comes back, see a dealer soon Yes for now, but do not ignore it. Driving with it on can damage emissions parts and the engine
Low Oil Pressure Indicator — an oil can icon Engine oil pressure has dropped very low or is lost Pull off the road right away and shut off the engine. Check the oil level, add oil if it is low, then watch the light when you restart No. Stop the car and shut off the engine as soon as it is safe
Charging System Indicator — a battery icon The battery is not being charged while the engine runs Turn off accessories you do not need, keep the engine running, and drive to a service station for help Only for a short distance to get help. The battery will drain further if you stop the engine
Brake System Indicator (red) — an exclamation mark in a circle Either the parking brake is not fully released, or the brake fluid is low or there is a brake problem If the parking brake is on, release it fully. If the light stays on, check the brake fluid; if the pedal feels wrong, pull over and call for a tow Yes if it is only the parking-brake reminder. No if the brake pedal feels different than normal
Seat Belt Reminder Indicator — a person wearing a belt The driver or front passenger seat belt is not fastened Fasten your seat belt, and make sure your passenger does too Yes, but always buckle up before you drive
Supplemental Restraint System (SRS) Indicator — a person in front of an airbag shape A possible problem with the airbags, seat belt tensioners, or related sensors Have a dealer inspect the car as soon as possible Yes, but the airbags may not work correctly in a crash until it is checked
Side Airbag Off Indicator — reads "SIDE AIRBAG OFF" The front passenger's side airbag has automatically shut off No action needed unless it seems unexpected; if so, have it checked Yes, this light is informational only
Anti-lock Brake System (ABS) Indicator — reads "ABS" in a circle A problem with the anti-lock function, the system that keeps your wheels from locking up under hard braking Have a dealer inspect the car soon Yes. Normal braking still works, but drive extra carefully, especially on wet or icy roads
Electric Power Steering (EPS) Indicator — a steering wheel icon A problem in the electric power steering system, which helps you turn the wheel with less effort Stop in a safe place, turn off the engine, then restart it to reset the system. If it returns, have it checked The manual says to stop and shut off the engine right away. Treat this as a pull-over light
Low Fuel Indicator — a fuel pump icon About 1.7 US gallons (6.3 L) of fuel remain in the tank Refuel soon Yes, but plan to refuel before it runs out
Door and Tailgate Open Indicator — an open-door icon A door or the tailgate is not closed tightly Close all doors and the tailgate firmly Yes, but close it first for safety
High Temperature Indicator — a red thermometer icon Engine coolant, the liquid that keeps your engine from overheating, is running hot If it blinks, slow down. If it stays on, pull over safely and shut off the engine No if the light stays on. Pull over and shut off the engine to avoid engine damage
Low Temperature Indicator — a blue thermometer icon Normal when the engine is cold. A problem if it stays on once the engine has warmed up If it stays on after the engine warms up, have a dealer check it Yes, this is normal at startup in cold weather
Turn Signal Indicators — left and right green arrows Shows an active turn signal. A fast blink usually means a burned-out bulb Replace the bulb soon if it blinks fast or does not blink at all Yes, but fix a burned-out bulb soon so other drivers can see your signal
Hazard Warning (four-way flashers) — both arrows flashing together The hazard button was pressed, for use when you are parked in a dangerous spot or your car is disabled Use it when stopped in traffic danger or broken down Yes, this is a safety feature
Low Tire Pressure Indicator — a tire cross-section with an exclamation mark One or more tires is significantly underinflated Pull over when safe, check your tires, and inflate or replace the low one with the compact spare Drive slowly and briefly to a safe stop. If two or more tires are low, call for a tow
Tire Pressure Monitoring System (TPMS) Indicator — reads "TPMS" The tire-pressure monitoring system itself has a problem and can no longer watch your tire pressure Check tire pressure by hand at every fill-up, and have a dealer check the system Yes, but you lose the automatic low-tire warning until it is fixed
Lights On Indicator Your exterior lights are switched on Turn off exterior lights when you park, or they will drain the battery Yes, this light is informational only
High Beam Indicator — a blue headlight icon Your high-beam headlights are on Switch to low beams when other traffic is oncoming Yes, this light is informational only
Maintenance Minder Indicator — a wrench icon It is time for scheduled maintenance Check the information display for which service is due, and schedule it Yes, but do not ignore it for long
"D" Indicator (automatic transmission only) Shows the shift lever is in Drive. A flashing "D" while driving can mean a transmission problem If it flashes, avoid hard acceleration and have the transmission checked soon Yes for a short trip, but get it checked, especially if the check engine light is also on
"CHECK FUEL CAP" message (on the information display, not a separate light) The gas cap is loose or missing Turn off the engine, and check and retighten the cap until it clicks once Yes, but the message can take several days of driving to clear even after you fix the cap

The gauges

Your instrument panel shows a tachometer, a gauge that measures how fast your engine is spinning, along with a speedometer and a fuel gauge. It also has an odometer, which shows total distance driven, a trip meter with A and B settings you can reset separately, and a select/reset knob. An information display shows text messages like the Maintenance Minder and "CHECK FUEL CAP." Your Fit has no separate needle gauge for coolant temperature. Instead, the High and Low Temperature indicator lights tell you about coolant temperature.

Press the select/reset knob to switch between Trip A and Trip B, and press and hold it until the display reads "0.0" to reset one. The odometer shows total miles driven; tampering with it to change the reading is illegal under US federal law.

The fuel gauge shows approximately how much fuel is in the tank, and it may read slightly more or less than the actual amount. The needle drops to empty after you turn off the ignition and comes back up once the engine runs again. Do not drive with an extremely low fuel level. Running out of fuel can make the engine misfire and can damage the catalytic converter, a part of the exhaust system that reduces pollution.

The tachometer has a red zone, the danger zone the engine speed limiter protects against on upshifts and the reference point for safe engine braking on downshifts. The 2008 Fit's factory redline, the highest safe engine speed, is 6,500 rpm. We could not confirm the normal idle RPM for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual.

Your speedometer is a standard analog needle gauge, and US models read in mph.

Quick check

  1. If your Low Oil Pressure light comes on, what should you do first, and is it safe to keep driving?
  2. Name two lights that are informational only, meaning you do not need to change your driving.
  3. Which light tells you your car is close to running out of fuel, and about how much fuel is left when it comes on?
  4. Why does your base Fit's dashboard never show a cruise control indicator?
  5. What is the difference between your engine's redline and its normal idle speed, and which one could this book confirm?

Chapter 8Controls You Will Use Every Day

What you will learn

  • How to work the lights, turn signals, hazards, and horn
  • How to run the wipers, washers, and both defrosters
  • How the heating and air conditioning (HVAC, or heating, ventilation, and air conditioning) controls work
  • How the power windows, door locks, and child locks work
  • How the radio works, and why the base Fit has no aux jack (a port for plugging in a phone or music player)

The short version

Most controls sit on the two steering-column levers or on the dashboard near the gauges. The left lever runs your lights and turn signals. The right lever runs your wipers and washers. The radio plays AM, FM, and CDs, but the US base Fit has no aux jack, so you cannot plug in a phone or MP3 player directly .

The full explanation

Horn

Press the center pad of the steering wheel to sound the horn .

Headlights

The light switch sits on the left steering-column lever, the same lever you use for turn signals . Turn it one position on to light the parking lights, taillights, instrument panel, side markers, and rear license plate . Turn it to the next position to turn on the headlights . A "lights on" indicator on the instrument panel reminds you the lights are on. If you leave the lights on and turn the ignition to ACCESSORY or LOCK, that indicator stays lit, and a chime sounds when you open the driver's door .

High beams

Push the left lever forward to turn on the high beams; a high-beam indicator lights up . Pull the lever back to return to low beams . To flash your high beams without leaving them on, pull the lever back lightly and let go; the high beams stay on only while you hold the lever .

Turn signals

The same left lever signals your turns. Push it down to signal a left turn and up to signal a right turn . The lever returns to center on its own once the turn finishes, or when you release it . For a lane change, push the lever lightly and hold it; it will not latch, so it stops signaling when you let go .

Hazard warning lights

A dedicated hazard button flashes all four turn signals and both dashboard turn-signal indicators at once . It sits near the instrument panel, grouped with the brightness knob and the rear defogger button .

Windshield wipers and washer

The right steering-column lever runs your wipers . It has six positions. MIST runs the wipers at high speed only while you hold the lever up. OFF stops them. INT (intermittent) sweeps the wipers every few seconds. LO runs them at a steady low speed. HI runs them at a steady high speed. Pulling the lever toward you sprays washer fluid; the wipers run at low speed and make one more sweep after you let go . Two-speed, intermittent front wipers come standard on the base Fit, not only on the Sport trim .

Rear wiper and washer

A separate rotary switch runs the rear wiper and washer . OFF parks the wiper. Turning the switch clockwise turns the rear wiper on. Holding it past ON also sprays the rear washer while the wiper runs. Turning the switch counterclockwise sprays the rear washer alone, without running the wiper . Both washers share one fluid tank; there is no separate rear tank . The rear wiper and washer come standard on base trim .

Defrosting the front windshield

Turn the fan on; high speed clears the glass fastest. Slide the fresh air/recirculation lever to fresh air mode . Turn the mode control dial to the windshield-defroster setting . This turns on the air conditioner automatically, since dry air clears the glass faster; you cannot switch the A/C off in this mode . Set the temperature so the air feels warm. Closing the round dashboard corner vents sends more air to the defroster vents . Once the glass clears, switch back to fresh air mode so the windows do not fog again .

Defrosting the rear window

Press the rear window defogger button on the dash to turn its heating grid on and off; the button has its own indicator light . This button includes a built-in shutoff timer, so it turns itself off after a set time rather than running forever . Never scrape the inside of the rear glass up and down; always wipe it side to side . The defogger's heating wires run along the inside of the glass, and scraping can damage them .

Heating and air conditioning (HVAC)

Four main controls sit across the dash, left to right . The fan control dial raises or lowers airflow. The temperature control dial makes the air warmer as you turn it clockwise. The fresh air/recirculation lever picks where the air comes from. The mode control dial picks which vents the air flows from: dashboard vents, floor vents, a mix of the two, the windshield defroster, or a floor-and-defroster mix . A small amount of air always flows from the dashboard corner vents, no matter which mode you pick . A separate A/C button turns the air conditioning compressor on and off, with its own indicator light . Air conditioning comes standard on every 2008 US Fit, including base trim .

The fresh air/recirculation lever controls where intake air comes from . Slide it to fresh air, and outside air enters through intakes at the base of the windshield; keep that area clear of leaves and debris . Slide it to recirculation, and the system reuses air already inside the cabin . Leave the system in fresh air mode most of the time, since recirculation can fog your windows, especially with the A/C off . Switch to recirculation only for short stretches through dust or smoke, then switch back .

Power windows

The ignition must be in the ON position for any window to move . Push a window switch down and hold it to lower that window; release it to stop partway . Pull back and hold to raise a window . The driver's window has an AUTO feature: push its switch firmly down past the first light push, then let go, and the window lowers all the way by itself . Pulling back briefly while it lowers stops it partway . A MAIN switch on the driver's door disables the passenger window switches when pushed in; the driver's own window keeps working . Push the MAIN switch again to turn the passenger switches back on . Keep the MAIN switch off, disabling the passenger windows, whenever children ride in the car, so they cannot operate a window and hurt themselves . Always check that everyone is clear before closing any window; a closing power window can seriously injure a hand or fingers . Power windows, including auto-down on the driver's side, come standard on base trim .

Door locks

Each door has a lock tab on top; push it in to lock and pull it out to unlock . You can also lock or unlock the front doors with the ignition key . On the driver's door, pushing the top of the master power-lock switch locks all doors and the tailgate together; pushing the bottom unlocks them all . Each passenger door's own tab locks or unlocks only that door . Turning the key once in the driver's door from outside unlocks only that door; turning it again unlocks the rest . To lock a passenger door from outside, push in its tab and close the door . To lock the driver's door from outside, take the key out, push in the tab or press the top of the master switch, then close the door . A lockout-prevention feature exists too. With the driver's door open and the key still in the ignition, the master switch will not work, and pushing in the driver's tab does not lock the car . This design stops you from locking your only key inside by accident .

Child safety locks

Each rear door has a childproof lock lever near its edge . Set it to LOCK, and that rear door cannot open from the inside handle at all, no matter how the regular lock tab is set . It opens only from outside . To release it, pull the lock tab up and open the door from outside .

Dome light

The ceiling light has a three-position switch: ON, Door Activated, and OFF . In Door Activated mode, the light turns on when you open any door, or unlock the doors with the key . It fades out over about 30 seconds after every door closes tightly . It also turns on when you remove the key from the ignition, and fades the same way if you do not then open a door . If a door stays open with no key in the ignition, the light shuts off completely after 3 minutes to protect the battery .

12-volt power outlet

The accessory power socket sits in the dash's center pocket, directly above the aux jack pocket; pull up its rubber cover to use it . It powers 12-volt DC (direct current, the steady, one-direction electricity a car battery supplies) accessories rated 120 watts or less, about 10 amps; it will not power a heating element like an old cigarette lighter . The ignition must be in ACCESSORY or ON for it to have power; it goes dead in LOCK . This outlet comes standard on base trim .

Radio and audio

The base Fit comes with a 160-watt AM/FM/CD system and 4 speakers . With the ignition in ACCESSORY or ON, turn the system on with the PWR/VOL knob or the AM/FM button, and adjust volume on the same knob . Change bands with the AM/FM button . Five ways exist to find a station. TUNE steps through frequencies one at a time, and SEEK searches automatically for the next strong signal . SCAN samples every strong station for about 10 seconds each . Six preset buttons store one AM station and up to two FM stations each, for 6 AM and 12 FM presets total . AUTO SELECT finds and stores strong local stations, which helps on a road trip far from home . The system also plays CDs and raises volume on its own as road noise increases with speed .

Base trim has no aux jack. This is an important, real limit of the car. An aux jack lets you plug a phone or music player into the stereo with a cable. Two official Honda sources confirm the aux jack ships only on the US Fit Sport and on Canadian models, never on the US base Fit . Compared to the Sport's 200-watt, 6-speaker system, the base system also lacks MP3/WMA playback and a 5-mode equalizer (a tool for adjusting different sound frequencies) . Speed-sensitive volume, however, works on both trims . The stereo gives you no direct way to plug a phone in. If you want to play music from a phone in a base Fit, you will need a separate solution, such as an FM transmitter.

To set the clock on base trim's radio, press and hold the CLOCK button until you hear a beep; the displayed time starts blinking . Press the H button repeatedly to set the hour, then the M button repeatedly to set the minutes . Press CLOCK again to lock in the time . To round to the nearest hour instead, press CLOCK until it beeps, then press the R button .

Steering wheel

The steering wheel tilts up and down; this feature comes standard on base trim . Push the lever under the steering column all the way down to unlock the wheel . Move the wheel up or down while keeping the gauges visible, then push the lever back up to lock it in place . The wheel does not telescope, meaning it does not slide closer to or farther from you .

Step by step: Defrost a foggy windshield

  1. Turn the fan on; pick high speed to clear the glass faster .
  2. Slide the fresh air/recirculation lever to fresh air mode .
  3. Turn the mode control dial to the windshield-defroster setting .
  4. Set the temperature dial so the air feels warm.
  5. Close the round dashboard corner vents to send more air to the defroster vents .
  6. Once the windshield clears, switch back to fresh air mode .

What can go wrong

  • Closing a power window on someone's fingers can cause serious injury; always check that hands and fingers are clear first .
  • Leaving the passenger window switches active with a child in the car lets that child operate a window and get hurt; use the MAIN switch to disable them .
  • Scraping the inside of the rear window up and down can damage the defogger's heating wires; always wipe side to side .
  • Expecting an aux jack on a base-trim stereo will leave you stuck; the US base Fit does not have one .
  • Leaving a door open with the key in the ignition can drain your battery through the dome light and chimes. The light itself shuts off after 3 minutes to help protect the battery .

Quick check

  1. Which lever controls your turn signals, and which direction do you push it for a left turn?
  2. Name two of the six positions on the windshield wiper lever.
  3. Does the base 2008 Fit have an aux jack for a phone or music player?
  4. What should you do before closing a power window, and why?
  5. How do you stop a child in the back seat from opening a rear door from the inside?

Chapter 9The Magic Seat and Cargo Tricks

What you will learn

  • What the Magic Seat is and why the Fit's cargo space is unusual for such a small car
  • How to fold the rear seat flat for cargo (Utility Mode)
  • How to flip the rear seat cushions up for tall items (Tall Mode)
  • How to make one long flat surface for lumber or surfboards (Long Mode)
  • How to recline the front seats flat to rest (Refresh Mode)

The short version

The Magic Seat is Honda's name for the Fit's 60/40-split rear seat, split so each side folds on its own . It can fold four different ways beyond normal seating, called Utility, Tall, Long, and Refresh mode . Each mode takes a few minutes and no tools, but each one also has real safety rules you must follow before you start.

The full explanation

Honda built the Magic Seat as a 60/40-split rear seat, meaning the two sides split unevenly . This lets you fold one side and leave a passenger seated on the other . Honda describes five total seating and cargo setups: the standard five-passenger mode, plus refresh mode, tall object mode, long object mode, and utility mode . This chapter covers the four non-standard modes as step-by-step procedures.

With every seat up in standard mode, the Fit holds 90.1 cubic feet of passenger space and 21.3 cubic feet of cargo space behind the back seat . Fold the rear seat-backs down flat for Utility Mode, and cargo space grows to 56.8 cubic feet .

Two basic seat motions make up all four modes. First, a rear seat cushion can fold up and lock against the seat-back, which is the base move behind Tall Mode . Second, a rear seat-back can fold down flat, which is the base move behind Utility Mode and part of Long Mode . Long Mode and Refresh Mode add more steps on top of these two basic motions.

Step by step

Utility Mode: fold the rear seat down flat for cargo

Utility Mode gives you a flat load floor from the tailgate forward, for boxes, groceries, or bikes .

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always remove any items from the rear seat before you fold it. - Always make sure nothing sits on the floor before you fold the seat-back down. - Always keep cargo below the bottom of the windows, so it does not block the side curtain airbags (airbags built into the roof rail above the windows). - Always tie down loose cargo so it cannot fly forward during hard braking. - Do not put heavy items directly on a folded seat-back. - Do not let a passenger sit on a rear seat you have folded down; return each side fully upright, locked, and belted first.

Steps: 1. Decide which side of the 60/40 rear seat you are folding: driver's side, passenger's side, or both . 2. Remove everything from that rear seat. 3. Move the matching front seat as far forward as it will go . 4. Lock the rear outer head restraint (the padded support behind your head) on that side in its lowest position . 5. If you are folding the driver's side, also set the rear-center head restraint to its lowest position . 6. If you are folding the driver's side, pull the latch plate to release the center seat belt from its anchor, and let the belt retract into the ceiling holder . Store the buckle there too . 7. Pull up the release lever on the outside of that seat-back . 8. Fold the seat-back forward until it lies flat . 9. Repeat steps 2 through 8 on the other side if you need the full-width flat floor. 10. Load your cargo, keeping it below window height and tied down.

How you know it worked: The seat-back lies flat and level, forming a flat load floor from the tailgate forward. It does not spring back up when you let go.

Tall Mode: flip the rear cushions up for tall items

Tall Mode opens floor space in front of the seat-back, for items too tall to fit under a normal seat .

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always remove any items from the rear seat before you fold the cushion up. - Always make sure the cushion locks firmly against the seat-back before you load anything against it. - Do not stack items higher than the top of the seat-backs; they can block your rear view and be thrown around the car in a crash. - Do not place cargo where it blocks your view through the rear window.

Steps: 1. Remove everything from the rear seat cushion you are flipping up. 2. Pull up on the rear seat cushion . 3. Fold the seat leg down as the cushion comes up . 4. Push the seat cushion firmly against the seat-back until it locks in place . 5. Repeat for the other side if you want both cushions up. 6. Load your tall item into the open floor space in front of the seat-back. 7. To return to normal seating later, make sure the floor under the seat is empty first . 8. Pull the seat leg up fully . 9. Slowly push the seat cushion back down while holding it with both hands . 10. Set the seat leg into its floor guide; a latch appears when it seats correctly .

How you know it worked: The seat cushion stays locked upright against the seat-back on its own. It does not fall back down when you let go.

Long Mode: carry long items like lumber or surfboards

Owners report that Long Mode combines the Tall and Utility motions to create one flat path from the tailgate to the dashboard . We could not confirm an official Honda step list for this exact combined mode on the 2008 GD3; check your owner's manual before loading anything long or heavy .

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always keep at least one working seat belt and seating position free for every person riding in the car. Do not use more seats than are actually available once you reconfigure them. - Always tie down long cargo. An unsecured long item can fly forward with great force during hard braking, which can seriously injure or kill someone in the front seat. - Do not let cargo extend into the space where an airbag needs to open. - Do not drive with cargo blocking your view through the rear window or a side mirror. - We could not fully confirm these exact steps against the 2008 GD3 owner's manual. Follow your own owner's manual's cargo-loading warnings if they differ from this book .

Steps (community-reported): 1. Fold the passenger-side rear seat cushion up, as in Tall Mode steps 1 through 4, or fold that seat-back down flat, as in Utility Mode . Pick whichever gives you the flattest path to your item . 2. Move the front passenger seat as far forward as it will go . 3. Recline the front passenger seat-back forward until it rests against the folded rear seat, forming one flat surface from the tailgate to the dashboard on that side . 4. Leave the driver's-side rear seat upright if you still need to carry a rear passenger . 5. Load the long item, such as lumber, a surfboard, or a ladder, along the flattened path. 6. Tie the item down using the cargo area's tie-down anchors so it cannot slide or shift.

How you know it worked: The item lies flat and secured along one continuous surface from the tailgate to the dashboard. It does not shift when you push on it by hand.

Refresh Mode: recline the front seats flat to rest

Refresh Mode reclines the front seats flat, level with the rear cushions, for resting when the car is parked . This is not a driving position.

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always park the car, set the parking brake, and turn off the engine before you recline the seats this far. - Always reinstall both front head restraints and lock both seats securely upright again before anyone drives or rides in the car. - Do not drive the car, or let anyone ride in a moving car, with the front seats in this reclined position. Seat belts are not designed to protect a reclined occupant (a person lying back in the seat). - Do not let a child use this mode unsupervised; the fold-down lever and mechanism could pinch fingers.

Steps: 1. Park the car, set the parking brake, and turn off the engine. 2. Recline the rear seat-backs as far back as they will go . 3. Remove both front head restraints and store them somewhere secure so they do not roll around . 4. Using the seat slide lever, move both front seat cushions as far forward as they will go . 5. Pull up the seat-back angle adjustment lever on each front seat . 6. Pivot each front seat-back backward until it is level with the rear seat cushions, creating one large cushioned area . 7. To return to the normal driving position, hold each seat-back with your hand first. 8. Pivot each seat-back back upright, controlling the motion so it does not come up too fast. 9. Reinstall both head restraints . 10. Lock both seats securely before anyone drives or rides in the car.

How you know it worked: The front seat-backs lie flat and level with the rear seat cushions, forming one continuous cushioned surface. Each seat-back stays down on its own without you holding it.

What can go wrong

Utility Mode

  • Forgetting to move the front seat forward first stops the folded seat-back from lying flat.
  • Leaving the head restraint up blocks the seat-back from folding flush.
  • Skipping the center seat belt's anchor release on the driver's side can strain or damage that belt.
  • Not checking the passenger airbag-off indicator after you restore the seat can leave you unsure if the front passenger's weight sensor still reads correctly.

Tall Mode

  • Not pushing the cushion firmly enough against the seat-back means it is not truly locked, and it can fall during driving.
  • Forgetting to fold the seat leg down can leave the mechanism loose.
  • Loading an item so tall that it blocks the rear window can also interfere with the seat-backs later, when you want to return the seat to normal.

Long Mode

  • Forgetting to move the front passenger seat forward before reclining it leaves a gap instead of a flat surface.
  • Not tying the item down lets it slide during braking or turns.
  • Loading an item so long that it sticks out past the rear bumper without a proper reflective flag or marker is unsafe, and may be illegal in some states.

Refresh Mode

  • Forgetting to remove the head restraints first stops the seat-back from lying fully flat.
  • Letting go of the seat-back too quickly when you return it upright is risky; it can spring up faster than expected, so control it by hand.
  • Forgetting to relock the seats and reinstall the head restraints before driving again leaves the car unsafe for the next trip.

Quick check

  1. What are the names of the four non-standard Magic Seat modes?
  2. Before you fold the driver's-side rear seat-back down, what must you do with the center seat belt?
  3. Why should you never stack cargo higher than the tops of the seat-backs in Tall Mode?
  4. Why is Refresh Mode only safe when the car is parked with the engine off?
  5. Why does this book label the Long Mode steps as "community-reported" instead of manual-confirmed?

Chapter 10Fuel

What you will learn

  • What fuel grade your Fit needs
  • How big the fuel tank is, and roughly how far it will take you
  • What the low-fuel light means, and what happens if you ignore it
  • How to fill the tank correctly and safely
  • What the "CHECK FUEL CAP" message means and how to clear it

The short version

Your 2008 Fit needs 87 AKI (pump octane) regular unleaded gasoline or higher . The tank holds 10.8 US gallons (41 liters) . Fill it outdoors, away from any flame or spark, and never try to add fuel again after the pump nozzle clicks off .

The full explanation

Fuel grade

Use 87 AKI (pump octane, the number posted on the gas station pump) unleaded gasoline or higher . Using a lower-octane fuel can cause a heavy, persistent metallic knocking sound in the engine, and that knock can damage the engine over time . Honda also recommends gasoline with detergent additives (chemicals that help keep the engine clean) . Where you can find it, choose gasoline without manganese-based additives such as MMT . Those additives can trigger the malfunction indicator lamp, the dashboard warning light also called the "check engine" light . Your Fit is designed for gasoline blended with up to 10% ethanol or up to 15% MTBE (a fuel additive; short for methyl tertiary-butyl ether) by volume, but never gasoline containing methanol .

Tank size and range

The fuel tank holds 10.8 US gallons, or 41 liters . The owner's manual and this book do not give an exact miles-per-gallon figure in this chapter's sources. Your real-world range will depend on how and where you drive, so use your fuel gauge, not a mental estimate, to judge how far you can go.

The low-fuel light

The low fuel indicator (a dashboard light) turns on when about 1.7 US gallons (6.3 liters) remain in the tank, before the needle reaches E . A small extra reserve remains even once the needle reaches E, but treat that reserve as a safety margin, not fuel to drive on normally . Running the tank out of fuel can make the engine misfire and can damage the catalytic converter (a part that cleans your exhaust) . Avoid driving with an extremely low fuel level .

The fuel door and cap

The fuel fill door opens from outside the car; pull its notched edge to open it, since this trim has no separate release lever inside the car . The fuel fill cap stays tethered to the filler neck, so you cannot drop or lose it . You may hear a hissing sound as you remove the cap; that is normal tank pressure escaping . The cap's tether fits into a slit on the fuel fill door, so it can hang there while you refuel . Screw the cap back on until it clicks at least once; an improperly tightened cap can trigger the malfunction indicator lamp and a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message .

The "CHECK FUEL CAP" message

If the fuel fill cap is loose or missing, a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message scrolls across the instrument panel's information display the next time you start the engine . You can press the trip-meter select/reset knob to clear the message from the display early . It comes back every time you restart the engine until you fix the cap . If the system keeps detecting a loose or missing cap, the malfunction indicator lamp also turns on . To fix it, turn the engine off, check that the cap is installed, and tighten it until it clicks at least once . The malfunction indicator lamp will not turn off right away; it can take several days of normal driving after you fix the cap . If it stays on longer than that, have a dealer inspect the car .

Step by step: Refuel the car

Safety warnings, before you start: - Gasoline is highly flammable and explosive. You can be burned or seriously injured while handling it . - Always turn off the engine before you start fueling . - Always keep heat, sparks, and open flame away from the fuel fill area . - Always handle fuel outdoors only, never in an enclosed space . - Always wipe up any spilled fuel right away . - Do not try to "top off" the tank once the pump nozzle clicks off on its own; leave room for the fuel to expand as its temperature changes .

Steps: 1. Park with the driver's side of the car closest to the fuel pump. 2. Turn off the engine . 3. From outside the car, open the fuel fill door by pulling its notched edge . 4. Remove the fuel fill cap slowly; a hissing sound as tank pressure escapes is normal . 5. Fit the cap's tether into the slit on the fuel fill door, so the cap hangs there while you fuel . 6. Insert the pump nozzle and select 87 AKI regular unleaded fuel or higher . 7. Fill until the nozzle clicks off automatically, then stop; do not try to add more . 8. If the nozzle clicks off repeatedly before the tank feels full, try a different pump; this can mean a problem with the fuel vapor recovery system . A dealer visit may help if it keeps happening . 9. Screw the fuel fill cap back on until it clicks at least once . 10. Close the fuel fill door.

How you know it worked: The pump handles cleanly, the cap clicks at least once when you tighten it, and the fuel gauge needle moves up. No "CHECK FUEL CAP" message or malfunction indicator lamp appears on your next drive.

What can go wrong

  • Not tightening the fuel cap until it clicks can trigger a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message and the malfunction indicator lamp . This can take several days of driving to clear, even after you fix the cap .
  • Trying to "top off" the tank past the automatic click-off risks a spill, and leaves no room for the fuel to expand .
  • Forgetting to close the fuel fill door before you drive away leaves it open and exposed.
  • Driving with the fuel gauge near empty risks a misfire and can damage the catalytic converter .

Quick check

  1. What octane rating does your 2008 Fit need?
  2. About how many gallons remain in the tank when the low fuel indicator first turns on?
  3. Why should you never try to "top off" the tank after the pump clicks off?
  4. What does a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message mean, and how do you fix the underlying problem?
  5. Why must you turn off the engine before you start fueling?

Part IIIDriving It

Chapter 11First Drive for a New Driver

What you will learn

  • What is different about starting off in a manual Fit versus an automatic Fit
  • The gear positions on the base-trim automatic transmission, and what each one does
  • A short first-drive routine for each transmission type
  • Where to find the full manual-transmission practice routine in this book

The short version

A manual Fit needs your left foot on the clutch pedal before it will move. An automatic Fit only needs you to pick the right gear lever position and press the gas. Both transmissions reward a slow, gentle first drive in an empty parking lot.

The full explanation

This chapter gives you a first look at driving your Fit, split by transmission type. If you do not know which one your car has, look at the floor. A clutch pedal on the far left, next to the brake and gas, means you have a manual. Only two pedals means you have an automatic.

If your Fit has a manual transmission

A manual transmission asks you to pick every gear yourself using a lever and a clutch pedal. The clutch is the pedal that disconnects the engine from the wheels so you can change gears without grinding them.

The Fit's manual gearbox uses a standard five-speed "H" shift pattern. First and third gear sit in the top row of the pattern, and second, fourth, and reverse sit in the bottom row, with reverse next to fourth and fifth. The gearbox is synchronized in every forward gear, which means the gears are built to mesh smoothly as you shift.
