This chapter's first-drive routine for a manual is short on purpose. Chapter 12 walks through the full sequence, step by step, with the exact clutch and gas technique to use, so read that chapter before your first real practice session.

If your Fit has an automatic transmission

The base-trim automatic Fit has a full shift gate, meaning it offers more lever positions than only "Drive." The positions are Park, Reverse, Neutral, Drive, a position marked "D" with a small "3" (called D3), Second, and First. The Sport-model Fit has a shorter gate with paddle shifters instead, but the base trim you are learning on has all seven positions.

Here is what each position after Park, Reverse, and Neutral actually does:

  • Drive (D): your normal, everyday driving position. The transmission picks a suitable gear on its own, from first through fifth, based on your speed and how hard you are pressing the gas. When the engine is cold, it may shift up at a higher engine speed than usual, to help the engine warm up faster.
  • D3: works like Drive, but the transmission stays in first, second, or third gear and will not shift into fourth or fifth. Honda intends this position to give you engine braking on a steep downhill, and to stop the transmission from constantly shifting between third and fourth in stop-and-go traffic. Engine braking means using the drag of the engine, instead of only the brakes, to slow the car down.
  • Second (2): locks the transmission in second gear, so it will not drop to first when you stop. The manual recommends this position for climbing a grade with more power, and for extra engine braking on a steep downhill. It also helps for starting out on a slippery surface or in deep snow, and for reducing wheel spin.
  • First (1): locks the transmission in first gear. By moving the lever among First, Second, D3, and Drive as conditions call for it, you can shift the automatic somewhat like a manual, only without a clutch pedal.

For an ordinary first drive, you will spend almost all of your time in Drive. Save Second, First, and D3 for hills, snow, or heavy traffic, and remember to move the lever back to Drive once you no longer need them.

Note for parents and new drivers: this chapter's driving sources cover the gear lever positions themselves. They do not cover the exact steps for starting the engine and pulling out of a parking space on an automatic. Chapter 6 of this book, on starting and stopping the car, covers the engine-start sequence in full.

Step by step

Manual transmission — short version. Full safety warnings and the full 11-step routine are in Chapter 12. In brief: fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors, then press the clutch fully to the floor and start the engine. Shift into first gear, then slowly release the clutch while pressing the gas gently until the car moves. Go to Chapter 12 before you try this for the first time.

Automatic transmission — first drive. 1. Fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors before you shift out of Park. 2. Press the brake pedal, and move the shift lever from Park to Drive. 3. Release the parking brake. 4. Slowly release the brake pedal, then press the gas pedal gently. 5. Let the transmission pick its own gears as your speed changes; you do not need to shift it yourself in Drive. 6. If you are climbing a long, steep hill and want more power, or descending a steep hill and want more engine braking, move the lever to D3. 7. Move the lever back to Drive once you are back on a normal road.

What can go wrong

  • Manual: letting the clutch pedal up too fast causes the engine to stall, which means it shuts off because it cannot keep running against the load. Chapter 12 covers this in detail.
  • Automatic — forgetting to shift back to Drive: if you leave the lever in Second, First, or D3 after a hill or snow, the transmission stays stuck there. It will not use all five gears until you move the lever back. Get in the habit of checking the lever position once conditions are back to normal.
  • Automatic — confusing D3 with Drive: D3 feels almost identical to Drive at low speed, since both use first through third gear in that range. The difference only shows up above the speed where Drive would normally shift into fourth or fifth.

Quick check

  1. How can you tell, by looking at the pedals alone, whether your Fit is a manual or an automatic?
  2. Name the seven lever positions on the base-trim automatic Fit's shift gate.
  3. What is the difference between Drive and D3?
  4. Which position would you choose to help start out on deep snow or a slippery surface?
  5. Where in this book should you go to learn the full manual-transmission first-drive routine?

Chapter 12Driving a Manual Transmission from Zero

What you will learn

  • How the clutch pedal works and why letting it up too fast stalls the engine
  • The Fit's shift pattern, and how to get into reverse without grinding the gears
  • The recommended speeds for shifting up, for the best fuel economy
  • A full first-drive routine, plus a routine for starting off on a hill

The short version

The clutch pedal disconnects the engine from the wheels so you can change gears. Let it up slowly and smoothly, with a little gas, and the car moves off without stalling. Practice in an empty lot before you try traffic or hills.

The full explanation

A manual transmission gives you full control of which gear the engine is in, using a gear lever and a clutch pedal. The clutch pedal is the leftmost of the three pedals. Pressing it all the way down disconnects the engine from the wheels, so you can move the gear lever without grinding the gears.

The shift pattern

The Fit's manual gearbox uses a standard five-speed "H" pattern. First and third gear are in the top row of the pattern; second, fourth, and reverse are in the bottom row, with reverse next to fourth and fifth. Every forward gear is synchronized, meaning the gearbox is built to let the gears mesh smoothly as you shift.

Getting into reverse without grinding

The transmission has a built-in lockout that stops you from shifting straight from fifth gear into reverse. To shift into reverse without the gears grinding, come to a full stop and push the clutch pedal all the way down. Then either pause a few seconds before moving the lever into reverse, or shift briefly into a forward gear first. Either action gives the input shaft, the spinning part inside the gearbox that reverse gear must line up with, time to stop turning before reverse engages.

Shifting for good fuel economy

The owner's manual gives recommended shift points for normal acceleration. Shift from first to second around 15 mph (24 km/h), and second to third around 27 mph (43 km/h). Shift from third to fourth around 39 mph (62 km/h), and fourth to fifth around 53 mph (85 km/h). As a general rule, drive in the highest gear that still lets the engine run and speed up smoothly. That gives you the best fuel economy and the lowest emissions.

Starting off on a hill

Honda's owner's manual for this generation of Fit does not describe a dedicated hill-start procedure. This car also does not have a Hill Start Assist feature, which is a system that appears on some later Honda models. The manual's page on parking does cover parking on a hill. Turn your wheels, use first gear if you are facing uphill or reverse if you are facing downhill, and set the parking brake. It does not, however, describe how to pull away from a stop on an uphill grade.

Because of that gap, the hill-start routine below is a general three-pedal technique common to manual-transmission cars, built from this car's own documented clutch and parking-brake behavior. It is good general practice, not a Honda-published Fit procedure.

Step by step

Drive a manual transmission for the first time

Safety warnings, before you touch a pedal: - Always practice first in an empty parking lot or a very quiet street, not in traffic. - Always fasten your seatbelt and adjust your seat and mirrors before you start the engine. - Always press the clutch pedal all the way to the floor before turning the key to start the engine; the starter will not engage otherwise. - Do not rev the engine hard or ride the clutch pedal partly pressed. This wears the clutch out faster and can damage it. - Always come to a full stop before shifting into reverse. - Do not try to shift straight from fifth gear into reverse. The transmission has a lockout and needs a short pause, or a pass through a forward gear, first.

Steps: 1. Adjust your seat, mirrors, and seatbelt before starting the engine. 2. Push the clutch pedal all the way to the floor. The starter only works with the clutch down. 3. Turn the key to start the engine, then shift the lever into first gear. 4. Keep the brake pedal or parking brake applied while you slowly let the clutch pedal rise. 5. Feel for the "friction point," the moment the engine note changes and the car wants to creep forward. 6. Gently press the gas pedal while you keep easing the clutch pedal up. 7. Once the car is moving, finish releasing the clutch pedal and keep your foot off it. 8. Shift up around 15 mph into second, 27 mph into third, 39 mph into fourth, and 53 mph into fifth. 9. To shift up, press the clutch fully down, move the lever to the next gear, then ease the clutch back up. 10. To slow down, press the clutch down fully right before the engine would stall, then brake normally. 11. Once stopped, shift to neutral and take your foot off the clutch until you are ready to move again.

How you know it worked: you can pull away smoothly and shift up and down through the gears. You can also come to a full stop without the engine stalling or the car jerking.

Start on a hill (manual transmission)

Safety warnings, before you try this: - Always practice this first in an empty parking lot or a quiet street with a gentle slope, not on a busy or steep hill. - Always keep your right foot ready to move to the brake pedal in case the car starts to roll backward. - Do not practice on a hill with another car stopped close behind you until you are confident you will not roll back. - Always turn on your hazard lights if you stall on a hill and are blocking traffic, then try again. - Do not rest your foot on the clutch pedal once you are moving; this wears the clutch out faster.

Steps: 1. Come to a full stop on the hill using the brake pedal. 2. Pull the parking brake up firmly with your right hand. 3. Push the clutch pedal all the way to the floor. 4. Shift the lever into first gear. 5. Move your right foot from the brake pedal to the gas pedal. 6. Press the gas gently while slowly letting the clutch pedal rise, until you feel the engine pull against the car. 7. When you feel the car want to move forward, release the parking brake fully. 8. Keep easing the clutch pedal up while giving a little more gas, so the engine does not bog down or stall. 9. Once the car is moving smoothly forward, finish releasing the clutch pedal all the way.

How you know it worked: the car moves forward up the hill smoothly, does not roll backward at any point, and the engine does not stall.

What can go wrong

  • Letting the clutch pedal up too fast. This stalls the engine, meaning it shuts off because it cannot keep running against the sudden load.
  • Riding the clutch. Holding the clutch pedal partway up while driving wears the clutch out faster.
  • Giving too little gas while releasing the clutch. This also stalls the engine, especially on a hill.
  • Releasing the parking brake before the clutch has caught, on a hill start. This lets the car roll backward.
  • Forgetting to fully release the parking brake once moving. This can overheat the rear brakes.
  • Looking down at the shift lever instead of watching the road. Learn the shift pattern by feel.
  • Trying to shift straight from fifth gear to reverse. Pause first, or pass through a forward gear, instead.

Quick check

  1. What does the clutch pedal do, and where is it located?
  2. What are the recommended speeds for shifting from first to second, and from fourth to fifth, for good fuel economy?
  3. Why can you not shift straight from fifth gear into reverse, and what two things can you do instead?
  4. What should your right foot be ready to do during a hill start, in case the car rolls backward?
  5. Does the 2008 Fit have a Hill Start Assist feature?

Chapter 13Parking, Reversing, and Tight Spaces

What you will learn

  • Where reverse gear sits in the manual shift pattern, and how to get into it smoothly
  • What the owner's manual says about parking on a hill
  • Why this chapter is shorter than you might expect, and where to look for more

The short version

Reverse sits next to fourth and fifth gear in the Fit's shift pattern, and the transmission has a lockout so you cannot grind into it from fifth by mistake. On a hill, turn your wheels, leave the car in gear, and set the parking brake. Beyond that, the sources for this book do not cover detailed parking or reversing technique for this specific car.

The full explanation

Reverse gear, on a manual Fit

In the Fit's five-speed "H" shift pattern, reverse sits in the bottom row of the pattern, next to fourth gear. The transmission has a lockout that prevents you from shifting straight from fifth gear into reverse. To get into reverse smoothly, come to a full stop and push the clutch pedal all the way down. Then either pause a few seconds or shift briefly into a forward gear before moving the lever into reverse. Either action gives the gearbox's spinning input shaft time to stop turning before reverse engages. On an automatic Fit, reverse is one of the seven positions on the base-trim shift gate, alongside Park, Neutral, Drive, D3, Second, and First.

Parking on a hill

The owner's manual's page on parking covers what to do when you park on a hill. Turn your front wheels toward the curb or the edge of the road. Leave the transmission in first gear if you are facing uphill, or in reverse gear if you are facing downhill, and set the parking brake. This gives the parked car a second way to resist rolling, in addition to the parking brake, if it were to slip.

What this chapter does not cover, and why

The sources used for this book do not include specific technique for parallel parking or backing into a space. They also do not cover using mirrors while reversing, or judging distances in tight spaces, for the 2008 Fit. We could not confirm Honda-specific guidance on these points. Rather than invent tips that are not in the source material, this book points you to general safe-driving practice instead. Go slowly, and check your mirrors and look over your shoulder before and during any reversing move. If you are unsure a space is tight, get out and look before you try it. A driving instructor or your state's driver's manual is a good place to learn parking and reversing technique, step by step.

Quick check

  1. Where does reverse gear sit in the Fit's manual shift pattern?
  2. What must you do with the clutch pedal before shifting into reverse, and why?
  3. According to the owner's manual, what gear should you leave the car in when parked facing uphill? Facing downhill?
  4. Why does this chapter not include detailed parking or reversing tips?

Chapter 14Highway, Rain, Snow, Heat, and Night

What you will learn

  • Why driving fast on the highway costs you fuel economy
  • What kind of tires the owner's manual recommends for winter driving
  • The rules for using tire chains on a Fit
  • Which weather and lighting conditions this chapter's sources do not cover

The short version

Speed above about 45 to 50 mph costs you fuel economy fast, because wind resistance rises sharply. For winter, "M+S" or all-season tires cover most conditions, and real snow tires or approved cable-type chains, on the front only, help further. The sources for this book do not cover rain, extreme heat, or night driving for this specific car.

The full explanation

Highway speed

Aerodynamic drag, the resistance of the air pushing back against the car, rises sharply above 45 mph (75 km/h). The EPA's general driving guidance says gas mileage usually drops quickly above 50 mph. Each 5 mph you drive over 50 mph acts like paying about $0.29 more per gallon, based on EPA's national average fuel price at the time that figure was published. This EPA guidance is written for gasoline cars in general, not for the Fit specifically, but it applies to any gasoline car including the Fit. Roof racks, carriers, and trailers add extra drag on top of that.

Winter tires and traction

Tires marked "M+S," which stands for mud and snow, or marked "All Season" on the sidewall, are suitable for most winter driving. For the best traction in snow or ice, the owner's manual recommends installing dedicated snow tires or tire chains, and notes these may be legally required in some places. If you mount snow tires, they must be radial tires of the same size and load range as your originals. They must go on all four wheels, not only the drive wheels. Dry-road traction may be lower than with your original tires. Ask your tire dealer for a maximum speed recommendation for the snow tires you choose.

Tire chains

Tire chains are allowed on the Fit, but with limits specific to this car's tight tire clearance. Mount chains only on the front tires, never the rear. Only SAE Class "S" cable-type traction devices with rubber chain tensioners are allowed. The manual says not to use metal link-style chains. Install chains only when conditions or local law require them, and follow the chain maker's instructions. Mount them as tight as possible and drive slowly. Check that they are not contacting the brake lines or suspension, and stop right away if you hear contact. Remove the chains as soon as the road is clear.

Rain, heat, and night driving

This book's research did not find Honda-specific guidance for the 2008 Fit on driving in rain, in extreme heat, or at night. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual, and until you find more specific guidance, use general safe-driving practice instead. Slow down in rain, since wet roads reduce traction. Watch your temperature gauge and coolant level more closely in extreme heat. Chapter 16 covers overheating in detail. Use your headlights and reduce speed at night, when your view of the road is shorter.

Quick check

  1. About how fast can you drive before aerodynamic drag starts costing you noticeably more fuel?
  2. What two words does "M+S" stand for on a tire sidewall?
  3. Which wheels should carry tire chains on a Fit, and what type of chain does the manual allow?
  4. Does this chapter's source material cover night driving for the 2008 Fit? What should you do instead?

Chapter 15Getting Good Fuel Economy

What you will learn

  • The shift points that give the best fuel economy on a manual Fit
  • What the owner's manual and the EPA say hurts and helps your fuel economy
  • Why the break-in period matters for your car's long-term health
  • Why you should not tow a trailer with this car

The short version

Drive smoothly, keep your tires inflated, keep up with maintenance, and stay off the gas pedal in stop-and-go traffic. On a manual, shift up early. Above about 45 to 50 mph, wind resistance starts eating into your mileage fast.

The full explanation

Shifting for economy, on a manual

The owner's manual's recommended shift points, for normal acceleration, are: first to second around 15 mph (24 km/h), and second to third around 27 mph (43 km/h). Third to fourth comes around 39 mph (62 km/h), and fourth to fifth around 53 mph (85 km/h). The general rule is to drive in the highest gear that still lets the engine run and speed up smoothly.

What the owner's manual says hurts and helps your fuel economy

Factors that lower fuel economy, according to the owner's manual, include aggressive driving, meaning hard acceleration or hard braking, and excess idling and stop-and-go acceleration and braking. Running the engine cold, driving with a heavy load or the air conditioning on, and under-inflated tires also hurt it. Tips to improve it include keeping up with maintenance, using the correct oil viscosity, and keeping your tires properly inflated. Avoid excess weight in the car, and keep the underside clean of snow or mud buildup. Drive moderately, meaning you avoid rapid acceleration, hard cornering, and hard braking, and observe the speed limit. Aerodynamic drag, the resistance of the air pushing back against the car, rises sharply above 45 mph (75 km/h). Roof racks, carriers, and trailers add extra drag. On a manual transmission, use the highest gear that still lets the engine run smoothly.

What the EPA says, in general

The EPA's general driving-habit guidance applies to any gasoline car, including the Fit. It says aggressive driving, meaning speeding and hard acceleration or braking, can cut your gas mileage by roughly 15 to 30 percent at highway speeds. In stop-and-go traffic, aggressive driving can cut mileage by 10 to 40 percent. Gas mileage usually drops quickly above 50 mph. The EPA estimates that each 5 mph you drive over 50 mph acts like paying about $0.29 more per gallon. That figure is tied to national average fuel prices at the time it was published, and is not specific to the Fit. This is general EPA guidance for gasoline cars, not text written specifically about the Fit.

The break-in period

Honda asks you to pay extra attention to your driving style for the vehicle's first 600 miles (1,000 km). This is called the break-in period. During this time, avoid full-throttle starts and rapid acceleration for the whole 600 miles, and avoid hard braking for the first 200 miles (300 km). Do not change the engine oil early. Wait until the regular scheduled maintenance interval. The same advice applies again after an engine overhaul or exchange, or after the brakes are replaced. Driving gently during break-in protects the engine and brakes, which pays off in reliability and economy for the life of the car.

Towing

The owner's manual states that this vehicle is not built to tow a trailer at all, and that doing so can void the vehicle's warranties. No numeric trailer-towing weight rating is given anywhere in the manual, because the manual's position is that trailer towing should not be done with this car. A trailer adds weight and drag, and the manual already lists both as things that hurt fuel economy. So towing one would work against everything else in this chapter.

Quick check

  1. At what speed does the owner's manual recommend shifting from first to second gear, on a manual Fit?
  2. Name three things the owner's manual says will hurt your fuel economy.
  3. How long is the Fit's break-in period, and what should you avoid doing during the first part of it?
  4. Is towing a trailer recommended for this car? What does the owner's manual say about it?

Chapter 16When Something Goes Wrong on the Road

What you will learn

  • What each red or important warning light means, and what to do about it
  • How to change a flat tire safely, step by step
  • How to jump-start a dead battery, step by step
  • How to handle an overheating engine, step by step
  • The rules for towing this car, and what the front towing hook is really for
  • Why this chapter cannot give you a Honda-specific accident checklist, and what to do instead

The short version

Pull over safely, turn on your hazard lights, and read the warning light before you decide what to do next. A flashing or steady red brake light, a low oil pressure light, or a stuck-on high temperature light all mean stop and deal with it, not keep driving. Keep the compact spare's speed limit and the flat-tow distance and speed limits in mind, since both are lower than normal driving speed.

The full explanation

Warning lights you may see on the road

The owner's manual only gives an explicit color label, "(Red)," to one indicator: the Parking Brake and Brake System Indicator. It comes on whenever you turn the ignition to ON, as a parking-brake reminder, and stays on until the parking brake is fully released. A beeper also sounds if you drive with the parking brake partly applied. If it stays on after you release the parking brake, or comes on while you are driving, something is wrong. The brake fluid level is probably low, or there is a brake system problem. Press the brake pedal lightly to check if it feels normal. If it feels normal, check the brake fluid level at your next stop. If the pedal does not feel normal, meaning it goes down much farther or needs much harder pressure, slow down by downshifting and pull over immediately. Do not keep driving; have the vehicle towed and repaired. If the ABS indicator, the light for the anti-lock brake system, comes on together with this brake indicator, have the vehicle inspected by a dealer right away.

The Low Oil Pressure Indicator should never come on while the engine is running. If it flashes or stays on, oil pressure has dropped very low. Pull over safely, shut off the engine, and turn on your hazard lights. Let the vehicle sit a minute, then open the hood and check the oil level, adding oil to the full mark on the dipstick if needed. Then restart the engine and watch the indicator. If it does not go out within 10 seconds, shut the engine off again; there is a mechanical problem that needs repair before you keep driving. This indicator is commonly described as a red, oil-can-shaped light, but that color could not be confirmed for the 2008 Fit specifically.

The Charging System Indicator means, if it comes on brightly while the engine is running, that the battery is not being charged. Immediately turn off all electrical accessories, and avoid using power windows and similar loads. Keep the engine running, since starting it again would drain the battery further, and go to a service station or garage for help. Owners report that this light is a red battery-shaped symbol, though the owner's manual text itself does not name a color for it.

The Malfunction Indicator Lamp, also called the check-engine light, means one of the engine's emissions-control systems may have a problem. The car may feel unchanged, but fuel economy can drop and emissions can rise. Continued driving may cause serious damage, so have the vehicle checked by a dealer as soon as possible if it comes on repeatedly. If it comes on right after you refuel, check for a loose or missing fuel cap first. The manual may also show a "CHECK FUEL CAP" message. Tightening the cap can take several days of driving to clear the light. This indicator does not demand pulling over right away, the way the brake, oil-pressure, or overheating indicators do, but the manual still says not to keep driving on it indefinitely. We could not confirm this indicator's color for the 2008 Fit specifically; the manual does not label it at all. Do not assume the check-engine light is a red light. Based on the manual's own wording, only the brake system indicator is confirmed red; the check-engine light is never given a color in the manual.

Hazard lights and reflectors

The hazard warning button, also called the four-way flashers, is on the instrument panel. Pressing it flashes all four exterior turn signals and both instrument-panel turn indicators at the same time. Use the hazard lights if you need to park in a dangerous spot near heavy traffic, or if your vehicle is disabled. Every roadside procedure in this chapter, flat tire, overheating, and low oil pressure, tells you to turn on the hazard lights as an early step.

We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. A full read of the owner's manual found no mention anywhere of a warning triangle, reflective triangle, road flare, or roadside reflector. It is not listed as standard equipment or as a recommendation. Check your owner's manual, and consider carrying a reflective triangle or flares as extra roadside protection even though the Fit's manual does not require or mention one.

Towing this car, and the towing hook

Honda names two approved ways to tow this vehicle for ordinary, non-emergency situations. Flat-bed equipment, where the whole car is loaded onto the back of a truck, is what the manual calls the best way to transport the vehicle. Wheel-lift equipment, where the tow truck's two arms lift the front tires off the ground while the rear tires stay down, is called an acceptable way to tow the vehicle. The manual says to call a professional towing service, and never to tow the car with only a rope or chain, since that is very dangerous.

The owner's manual does not offer flat towing, meaning all four wheels rolling on the ground, as a supported method for either transmission. It only allows front-wheels-on-the-ground towing as a damage-only contingency, when flatbed or wheel-lift equipment cannot be used. This method is capped at 50 miles (80 km) and 35 mph (55 km/h) maximum. For a manual transmission, release the parking brake, shift to neutral, and leave the ignition switch in the ACCESSORY position so the steering wheel does not lock. For an automatic transmission, release the parking brake, start the engine, shift to D, and hold for 5 seconds. Then shift to N, let the engine idle for 3 minutes, and turn it off. Leave the ignition switch in ACCESSORY. If you cannot shift the transmission or cannot start the engine, the vehicle must be transported with the front wheels off the ground instead. The front-wheels-down method does not apply in that case. Do not lift the vehicle, or tow it, using the bumpers. They cannot support the vehicle's weight and will be seriously damaged. Always confirm the steering wheel turns freely, with the ignition in ACCESSORY, before towing begins.

The car also carries a single detachable towing hook and its extension tool, stored in the tool kit in the cargo area. It mounts into a bolt hole behind a cover panel in the front bumper. The manual frames this hook as a "get unstuck" tool. It is meant for freeing the car from sand, mud, or snow over very short distances, or for a towing service to use for straight, flat-ground pulling only. It is not for loading the car onto a flatbed, and must not be used as a cargo tie-down. Do not tow at an angle with it, and call a towing service for anything beyond a very short pull.

Accidents

We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. None of this book's research sources describe a Honda-specific accident checklist, meaning a list of what to do right after a collision. If you are ever in an accident, use general safe practice instead. Check for injuries first, and move to a safe spot off the road if the car can still move. Turn on your hazard lights, call emergency services if anyone is hurt, and exchange insurance and contact information with anyone else involved. Consult your state's driver's handbook or local authorities for the exact steps required where you live, since accident-reporting rules vary by state.

Step by step

Change a flat tire

Safety warnings, before you touch the jack: - Always stop far away from traffic lanes, on firm, level, non-slippery ground, before you start. - Always turn on the hazard warning lights before you get out of the car. - Always get every passenger out of the car before you change the tire. - Do not get under the car at any time. It can roll off the jack and crush you. - Always follow the jacking steps in order. Do not skip the jacking-point check. - Always handle wheel nuts and the brake hub carefully; they can be hot from driving. - Never exceed 50 mph (80 km/h) on the compact spare tire. It is a temporary tire only.

Steps: 1. Pull well off the road, onto firm, level, non-slippery ground, away from traffic. 2. Turn on the hazard warning lights. 3. Put the transmission in Park (automatic) or reverse (manual). Set the parking brake. 4. Turn the ignition off. Have everyone get out of the car and stand away from traffic. 5. Open the tailgate and lift the cargo floor lid using its strap. Hook the strap to the head restraint leg to hold it open. 6. Take the tool kit out of the center of the spare tire. 7. Turn the jack's end bracket counterclockwise to loosen it, then lift the jack straight out. 8. Unscrew the wing bolt and lift the spare tire out of its well. 9. Loosen each wheel nut on the flat tire one half turn with the wheel nut wrench. Do not remove them yet. 10. On base-trim cars, pry off the wheel cover using the flat tip of the extension. 11. Find the jacking point nearest the flat tire. Look for the mark molded into the body underside. 12. Set the jack under that jacking point. Turn the end bracket clockwise until the jack meets the point. 13. Check that the jacking point's tab is resting inside the jack's notch before you raise the car. 14. Use the extension with the wheel nut wrench to raise the car until the flat tire is off the ground. 15. Remove the loosened wheel nuts, then pull the flat tire off. Set it down with the outside face up. 16. Wipe dirt off the wheel hub and the spare's mounting surface with a clean cloth. 17. Put the spare tire onto the hub. Thread the wheel nuts on finger-tight in a crisscross pattern. 18. Lower the car to the ground with the jack, then remove the jack. 19. Tighten the wheel nuts the rest of the way in the same crisscross pattern, to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m). 20. Place the flat tire face down in the spare tire well and secure it with the wing bolt. 21. Store the jack and place the tool kit back in the center of the flat tire. 22. Store the wheel cover or center cap in the cargo area so it does not get scratched. 23. Unhook the strap, lower the cargo floor lid, and close the tailgate. 24. Drive to a service shop soon to have the wheel nut torque checked and the flat tire repaired.

How you know it worked: the spare tire sits flat on the ground, all wheel nuts are snug and torqued, and the car sits level. It drives straight with no pulling, wobble, or noise below 50 mph.

Jump start the car

Safety warnings, before you connect a cable: - Always keep sparks, open flames, and smoking materials away from the battery. It can explode. - Do not jump-start a battery with slushy or frozen electrolyte, the liquid inside the battery. Let it thaw first. - Do not connect the second jumper cable to the dead battery's negative terminal or to any other engine part. Use the grounding point, called "the stay" in the manual, instead. - Always turn off all electrical accessories on both vehicles before connecting cables. - Do not try to push- or pull-start a car with an automatic transmission. It will not work. - Always keep loose jumper cable ends away from each other and from metal until fully disconnected.

Steps: 1. Open the hood and check the physical condition of your battery before doing anything else. 2. In cold weather, check that the battery's electrolyte is not slushy or frozen. 3. Turn off the heater, A/C, audio system, lights, and every other electrical accessory. 4. Put the transmission in neutral (manual) or Park (automatic), and set the parking brake. 5. If you are using another vehicle, have someone start it and run it at a fast idle. 6. Connect one jumper cable to the positive (+) terminal on your dead battery. 7. Connect the other end of that cable to the positive (+) terminal on the booster battery. 8. Connect the second jumper cable to the negative (-) terminal on the booster battery. 9. Connect the other end of the second cable to the grounding stay on your car, not the battery. 10. Start your engine. If cranking is slow, recheck that every cable clamp has a solid metal contact. 11. Once your car is running, disconnect the negative cable from your car first, then from the booster battery. 12. Disconnect the positive cable from your car, then from the booster battery. 13. Keep the loose cable ends apart and away from any metal until both cables are fully removed.

How you know it worked: the engine starts and keeps running on its own once both cables are disconnected, and the charging system indicator is off.

The car is overheating

Safety warnings, before you open the hood: - Do not open the hood if you see steam or spray coming from under it. Wait until it stops. - Steam and spray from an overheated engine can scald you badly. Stay clear until things cool. - Always let the engine and radiator cool down before removing the radiator cap. - Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant, the liquid that keeps the engine's temperature down, can spray out and scald you. - Do not keep driving with the high temperature indicator on. It can cause serious engine damage.

Steps: 1. Safely pull to the side of the road, away from traffic. 2. Shift to neutral (manual) or Park (automatic), and set the parking brake. 3. Turn off all accessories and turn on the hazard warning lights. 4. If you see steam or spray from under the hood, turn off the engine right away. 5. Wait until no more steam or spray is visible before you open the hood. 6. If there is no steam or spray, you may leave the engine running and watch the indicator. 7. If the indicator goes off within a few minutes, wait for it, then continue driving. 8. If the indicator stays on, turn off the engine and let it sit. 9. Look for an obvious coolant leak, such as a split hose. Everything is still very hot. 10. If you find a leak, do not drive again until it is repaired. 11. If you see no leak, check the coolant level in the radiator reserve tank. 12. Add coolant to the reserve tank if the level is below the MIN mark. 13. If the reserve tank was empty, let the engine and radiator cool fully before going further. 14. Using gloves or a heavy cloth, turn the radiator cap counterclockwise to the first stop only. 15. Let the pressure release fully, then push down on the cap and turn it off. 16. Start the engine and set the temperature dial to maximum heat. 17. Add coolant to the radiator up to the base of the filler neck. 18. If you have no coolant mix on hand, plain water is an acceptable emergency substitute. 19. Put the radiator cap back on tightly, then run the engine and watch the indicator. 20. If the indicator blinks or comes on again, stop driving. The engine needs repair. 21. If the temperature stays normal, top the reserve tank to MAX and close its cap tightly. 22. Have the cooling system drained and refilled with the correct coolant mixture soon.

How you know it worked: the high temperature indicator stays off during normal driving, the reserve tank sits between MIN and MAX, and you see no new coolant leaks.

What can go wrong

  • Flat tire — loosening wheel nuts after the car is already jacked up. The wheel spins in place instead of loosening. Loosen them a half turn first, while the tire is still on the ground.
  • Flat tire — setting the jack on bodywork instead of the marked jacking point. Check for the molded mark before you raise the car.
  • Flat tire — driving the spare above 50 mph, or for a long distance, instead of repairing the flat promptly. The compact spare is a temporary tire only.
  • Flat tire — skipping the shop visit to have wheel nut torque re-checked. The manual calls for this after any fresh tightening.
  • Jump start — connecting the last cable to the dead battery's negative terminal instead of a ground point. This raises the risk of sparks near battery gases.
  • Jump start — leaving accessories on while connecting cables, or trying to push-start an automatic. Neither works safely.
  • Jump start — letting stray cable ends touch metal while still connected on one end. Keep the ends apart until both cables are fully off.
  • Overheating — opening the hood while steam is still visible, or removing the radiator cap on a hot engine. Both can cause serious scalding.
  • Overheating — continuing to drive after the indicator stays on, instead of pulling over. This can cause serious engine damage.
  • Overheating — forgetting to have the correct coolant mixture put back in, after adding plain water in an emergency. Plain water is a stopgap, not a permanent fix.
  • Towing — using the front towing hook for anything beyond a short, straight, flat-ground pull. It is a "get unstuck" tool, not a general towing point, and must not be used to load the car onto a flatbed.
  • Towing an automatic without the D-then-N cycle. Skipping the 5-second Drive step, the 3-minute idle in Neutral, and letting the engine cool before shifting can damage the transmission.

Quick check

  1. What color does the owner's manual explicitly confirm for a warning light, and which light is it?
  2. What should you do if the brake pedal does not feel normal when you press it?
  3. Name the maximum speed limit for the compact spare tire, and the distance and speed limit for front-wheels-on-the-ground towing.
  4. In what order do you disconnect jumper cables once the engine is running?
  5. Why should you never open the hood if you see steam coming from it?
  6. Does this book have a Honda-specific accident checklist for the 2008 Fit? What should you do if you are in an accident?

Part IVOwning It (the paperwork)

Chapter 17Title, Registration, Insurance, Inspection, and Emissions Basics

What you will learn

  • What a title is, and why you should never leave it in the glovebox.
  • What paperwork you should get when you buy from a dealer.
  • What paperwork you should get when you buy from a private person.
  • What the federal Buyers Guide sticker must tell you at a used car lot.
  • Why registration, insurance, and inspection rules are different in every state, and where to check yours.

The short version

A title is the legal paper that proves who owns a car, and it is the most important paper you will ever get with it. When you buy a car, you should also get a bill of sale and a written record of the mileage. Registration, insurance, and inspection rules are set by each state, so always check your state DMV's website for the exact steps.

The full explanation

Buying from a dealer

A title is the legal document that proves who owns a car . If you buy a used 2008 Fit from a licensed dealer, you should receive five things at the sale . First, the title, or proof the dealer can transfer it to you . Second, a bill of sale, which is the dealer's own sales contract . It shows the price, the taxes, and the fees . Third, a Buyers Guide window sticker, which becomes your copy once you buy the car . Fourth, an odometer disclosure statement, which is the mileage reading, signed by the seller . Federal law requires this statement for most cars under 20 model years old at the time of sale . Fifth, a temporary plate, or a finished application to title and register the car . This lets you drive it home legally while you wait for permanent plates .

Buying from a private person

Buying from a private seller works a little differently . You should still get the title, but the seller must sign it over to you . The seller's name on the title must match their name exactly . If it does not match, you will need extra paperwork. A chain of title is a paper trail showing each past owner in order. A power of attorney is a signed paper letting one person sign for another . You should also get a bill of sale with both people's names, addresses, and signatures . It should list the car's year, make, and VIN . A VIN is the 17-character code that identifies one specific vehicle; you will use it again in Chapter 18 . You need a written record of the mileage at the time of sale too . Some states also want a release-of-liability form . This form tells the DMV that the seller is no longer responsible for the car after the sale .

Why this book cannot give you exact state rules

Every state runs its own registration, insurance, and inspection system . Some states require a safety inspection or an emissions test . Some states require neither one . Some states want a notarized signature on the title, and some do not . Because these rules do not match from state to state, and because they can change, this book cannot list every one correctly and keep it current . Check your state DMV's website for the fees, deadlines, and inspection rules where you live .

The Buyers Guide sticker

The Federal Trade Commission's Used Car Rule forces dealers to post a Buyers Guide sticker on every used car . Private sellers do not have to post one . The dealer must put the sticker in the window before you are even allowed to inspect the car for buying . The sticker must say whether the dealer offers a warranty . If it does, the sticker must state how long the warranty lasts, what share of repair costs the dealer pays, and which systems it covers . It must list the car's 14 major systems and the kinds of defects that can happen in them . It must suggest asking the dealer if you can get the car inspected by an independent mechanic first . It must warn you not to trust spoken promises that are not written down . Since a 2018 update, it must also recommend getting a vehicle history report . The dealer must hand you this same sticker as your copy when you buy the car .

Quick check

  1. What is a title, and why should you never leave it in the glovebox?
  2. Name three of the five things you should get when buying from a dealer.
  3. What must the Buyers Guide sticker tell you about a warranty?
  4. Why can't this book list your state's exact registration and inspection rules?
  5. Where should you go to find your state's exact rules?

Chapter 18Recalls and Service Bulletins for This Exact Car, and How to Check for New Ones

What you will learn

  • What a recall is, and what a campaign number means.
  • What a TSB (Technical Service Bulletin) is, and how it differs from a recall.
  • All 8 confirmed NHTSA recalls for the 2007-2008 Honda Fit, in one table.
  • Two recalls that sound like they apply to your car but do not.
  • The top kinds of problems other 2008 Fit owners have reported to the government.
  • How to check your exact car's VIN for open recalls, right now.

The short version

A recall is a free, safety-related repair that the maker must offer because a part can fail in a dangerous way. Your 2008 Fit has 8 confirmed recalls, and you should check every one against your car's own VIN. You can check for free at nhtsa.gov/recalls using your car's 17-character VIN. Do this today, and check again every so often, not only once when you buy the car.

The full explanation

What a recall is

A recall is a safety fix that a car maker must offer for free, because a part on the car can fail in a way that could hurt someone . The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration, or NHTSA, is the US government agency that tracks recalls . Every recall gets a campaign number, which is a code that identifies that exact recall . A TSB, or Technical Service Bulletin, is different from a recall . A TSB is a repair instruction the maker sends to dealers for a known problem that is not considered a safety defect . A TSB is not free unless it is still covered by your warranty .

The 8 confirmed recalls for your 2008 Fit

NHTSA's own database lists 8 recall campaigns for the 2007-2008 Honda Fit . Before you drive a used 2008 Fit, or even your own car if you have not checked lately, confirm every one of these shows as completed, not open .

Campaign number Date recall began Component Remedy
10V033000 March 19, 2010 Power window master switch (driver's door) — water can enter through an open window and damage the switch, which can overheat or smoke Dealer inspects the switch and installs a water cover. Dealer replaces the switch if it is already damaged. Free
13V260000 Parts ready October 18, 2013 Power window master switch (driver's door) — a repeat of the same defect, because the 2010 fix did not fully solve it Dealer replaces the power window master switch with an updated part. Free
10V624000 January 24, 2011 Headlight low-beam wiring harness and combination switch — a bad connection can make low-beam headlights stop working Dealer inspects the low-beam terminal and repairs it as needed. Free
07V549000 December 12, 2007 Frontal airbag Occupant Detection System (ODS) wire harness — road salt tracked onto the floor can corrode this wire under the carpet. This one only applies if your car was ever sold or registered in one of 21 listed salt-belt states, or Washington, DC Dealer inspects the wire harness and replaces it if needed. Free
16V344000 June 27, 2016 Passenger frontal airbag inflator, made by a supplier named Takata — this covers both original and replacement inflators, and it can rupture and throw metal fragments Dealer replaces the passenger airbag inflator. Free
17V029000 March 2017 Passenger frontal airbag inflator — a later expansion of the same Takata defect as 16V344000 above Dealer replaces the front passenger airbag inflator. Free
19V501000 On or about August 12, 2019 Passenger frontal airbag inflator — this one only applies if the inflator had already been swapped once under an earlier Takata campaign Dealer replaces the passenger airbag inflator again. Free
20V770000 NHTSA notified December 10, 2020 Drive shaft — a coating defect can let road salt corrode the shaft until it breaks. This one only applies to manual-transmission 2008 Fits Dealer inspects the drive shaft and replaces it if necessary. Free

The ODS wire harness fix (07V549000) uses two related sensor systems, called ODS and OPDS . Together they detect a child seat or an out-of-position rider in the front passenger seat, so the airbag can be turned off for that seat when it should be . Takata was a company that made a type of airbag inflator that can explode over time as its propellant breaks down . That is why three separate campaigns above deal with a Takata passenger inflator . Salt-belt states are states that salt their roads heavily in winter, which speeds up rust on the underside of a car .

Two look-alike recalls that do NOT apply to your car

It is easy to search for "Honda Fit recall" and find a campaign that looks like a match but is not . The 2009 Fit is a different car underneath a similar name . Campaign 18V268000 covers a passenger airbag installation error, but its own list of affected years is 2007 and 2009 through 2013 — 2008 is not on that list . Campaign 19V182000 covers a driver-side airbag inflator, but it names only the 2007 Fit, not the 2008 Fit . Your car's passenger-side inflator is still covered, through the three campaigns in the table above . This is why you should always check your own car's VIN, instead of only searching by year and model .

One service bulletin worth knowing about

Electric power steering (EPS) is the electric motor that helps you turn the steering wheel . Honda Service Bulletin 14-058 extended the EPS warranty to 10 years or 150,000 miles, whichever came first . It applies to 2007-2008 Fits with a stored trouble code called a DTC, which stands for diagnostic trouble code . The specific codes are DTC 32-09 or DTC 61-04 . The symptom is steering that feels heavier than normal, especially when the car is not moving . By 2026 a typical 2008 Fit is past both limits of that warranty, so this repair is now an out-of-pocket cost, not a free warranty claim . A second bulletin, 13-021, covers a rocker arm oil pressure switch . It can trigger the MIL, or Malfunction Indicator Lamp, which is the dashboard light most people call the check engine light . It applies to 2007-2011 Fits and is fixed by replacing the switch and its seal .

What other 2008 Fit owners have reported to NHTSA

NHTSA keeps a public database of consumer complaints . As of this research, it held 217 total complaint records for the 2008 Fit . A single complaint can be tagged with more than one category, so the counts below add up to more than 217 . Here are the top categories, in order, with the number of complaints in each: air bags, 57 . Structure, 43 . Power train, 24 . Engine, 23 . Electrical system, 18 . Exterior lighting, 17 . Vehicle speed control, 14 . Brake-related complaints combined, 22 . Air bags being the top category matches the recall list above . Three of your car's eight recalls involve airbags .

How to check your own car's VIN, right now

Go to nhtsa.gov/recalls and use the "Search by YMM or VIN" option . Type in your car's full 17-character VIN and click Search . The VIN is printed on a small plate at the lower left corner of the windshield, visible from outside the car . It is also on your registration card and often your insurance card . NHTSA also lets you search by license plate and state instead . If your car has no open recalls, the tool says "0 unrepaired recalls associated with this VIN" . A search by year, make, and model instead of VIN gives you general recall and complaint information, not your specific car's repair status . Note that this VIN search shows unrepaired-recall status "for certain manufacturers," so it may not show every kind of record for every maker . American Honda's own owner site, owners.honda.com, also offers a VIN-based recall check, and Honda's customer service line, 1-800-999-1009, is another way to ask about recall or repair status . Using both the NHTSA tool and Honda's own site is a good habit, since a brand-new campaign can show up on one before the other . The exact page behavior on Honda's own site was not independently confirmed this pass, so treat it as a helpful backup, not your only check .

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between a recall and a TSB?
  2. Name any three of your car's 8 recall campaign numbers and what part they fix.
  3. Why doesn't campaign 19V182000 apply to your 2008 Fit, even though it covers a Fit?
  4. Where on the car do you find the VIN, and what website do you type it into?
  5. What is Takata, and why does it show up in three different recall campaigns?

Chapter 19Documents to Keep in the Glovebox and at Home

What you will learn

  • Which three or four papers belong in your car's glovebox.
  • Which papers belong at home instead, and why.
  • Why the title should never travel in the car.
  • How keeping good records now helps you later, including when you sell the car.

The short version

Keep your registration, your insurance card, and your inspection sticker in the glovebox, because you may need to show them at any time. Keep your title, your bill of sale, and your maintenance records at home instead, in a safe place. Never keep the title in the car, because a stolen title makes a stolen car much easier to resell.

The full explanation

What belongs in the glovebox

Keep your current registration in the glovebox . Registration is the state's record that your car is legally allowed on the road . Keep your proof of insurance card there too . If your state issues a safety or emissions inspection sticker or certificate, keep the current one in the car as well . Some owners also keep a copy of the Buyers Guide sticker from when they bought the car, described in Chapter 17 . A card listing an emergency contact, plus any medical conditions or allergies, is also worth keeping in the glovebox . Do not keep the title in the glovebox . A stolen title makes a stolen car much easier for a thief to resell .

What belongs at home

Keep your title at home, in a safe place . The title is the single most important paper for this car, and it is not something you need on a daily drive . Keep your original bill of sale and purchase paperwork with it . Keep your Buyers Guide sticker copy from the sale too . Keep the owner's manual at home, or write down where the digital copy is saved . Keep your maintenance and repair receipts together . They matter for warranty claims, and they help prove the car's service history if you ever sell it . Keep any recall notice letters you receive from Honda or NHTSA, covered in Chapter 18 . Keep your auto insurance policy documents at home too, separate from the insurance card in the car .

Why this matters later

Good records at home make it much easier to answer "what wears by then" questions if you ever sell the car . Chapter 20 covers this in detail for the 2008 Fit specifically .

Quick check

  1. Name three documents that belong in the glovebox.
  2. Name three documents that belong at home instead.
  3. Why should the title never stay in the car?
  4. Why are maintenance receipts worth keeping, even for routine work?

Chapter 20Buying a Used 2008 Fit: Inspection Checklist and Known Trouble Spots

What you will learn

  • What a general pre-purchase inspection (PPI) covers on any used car.
  • How to fold the 8 recalls from Chapter 18 into your buying checklist.
  • The one service bulletin worth asking about before you buy.
  • Eight trouble spots owners report on first-generation Fits, and how to check each one on a test drive.
  • What a used 2008 Fit tends to cost in today's market, in a clear price table.
  • Why two of the pricing sources disagree, and what a typical odometer reading looks like at this car's age.

The short version

Before you buy a used 2008 Fit, run its VIN through the recall check from Chapter 18 and get a mechanic's inspection. Owners report eight specific wear spots on this car, so check each one on the test drive. Expect a wide price range depending on the source and the car's condition. Expect the odometer to show anywhere from about 100,000 to 250,000 or more miles.

The full explanation

A general pre-purchase inspection

A pre-purchase inspection, or PPI, is a check of a used car before you buy it, done by you or by a paid independent mechanic . A general PPI covers five main areas on any car . First, the exterior and body: look for crash damage, mismatched paint, rust, and signs of flood damage . Also check whether the car sits level, and check for cracked or chipped glass . Second, tires and brakes: check tread depth, the tire's age code on the sidewall, uneven wear, and the condition of the pads, rotors, and calipers . Third, the engine bay and the underside: check for fluid leaks, worn belts and hoses, and rust on the frame . A puddle under a parked car is worth investigating, and a transmission leak is especially expensive to ignore . Fourth, the interior and electronics: test the heat and air conditioning, and check the instrument cluster and warning lights . Confirm every gauge and light actually works . Fifth, a test drive that checks braking, steering feel, suspension, and acceleration . Also ask the mechanic to scan the car's computer for stored diagnostic trouble codes (DTCs) . A code can be cleared right before a sale, only to come back after you have driven the car for a while . A professional PPI from an independent shop commonly costs about $100 to $200 .

Confirm every recall is closed

Run the car's VIN through the recall check described in Chapter 18 before you buy . Confirm all 8 recalls from that chapter's table show as completed, not open . Pay special attention to two of them . The drive shaft recall (20V770000) only applies if the car has a manual transmission . The ODS wire harness recall (07V549000) only applies if the car was ever sold or registered in a salt-belt state . Do not be alarmed if a general web search turns up campaign 19V182000, since that recall names only the 2007 Fit, not your 2008 Fit . Trust what the VIN search itself shows for your specific car, not a general search .

Ask about the steering bulletin too

Ask the seller whether the check engine light has ever come on with a steering-related code . Also ask whether the steering has ever felt unusually heavy, especially when the car is stopped . This relates to Honda bulletin 14-058, covered in Chapter 18, which once extended the steering system's warranty to 10 years or 150,000 miles . By now that warranty window has passed on any 2008 Fit, so a repair would come out of your own pocket . Still, knowing the car's history helps you judge its condition .

Eight trouble spots owners report on first-generation Fits

These eight items come from real owner reports on first-generation Fit forums . They are not from Honda or the government, so they are labeled as owner reports rather than confirmed defects .

  1. Owners report that motor mounts wear out over time, causing a clunk when shifting into or out of gear, or vibration at idle .
  2. Owners report that the timing chain tensioner can develop a ticking or rattling noise under load, though a healthy engine on this car is naturally a little "ticky" too .
  3. The clutch master and slave cylinder are the small hydraulic parts that move the clutch when you press the pedal . On manual-transmission cars, owners report that one of these can suddenly feel spongy or stop working, sometimes with no visible fluid leak .
  4. On automatic-transmission cars, owners report a shudder or hesitation holding a steady 55 to 65 miles per hour . More than one part can cause this, including worn spark plugs or ignition coils, not only the transmission .
  5. Owners report air conditioning problems in two forms: a compressor that rattles when it engages, and cooling that starts strong but does not stay cold . Also check the condenser, which sits ahead of the radiator, for rock-strike damage .
  6. Owners report a metallic clunk or knock from the front suspension over bumps, most often traced to worn sway-bar end links or strut top-mount bushings .
  7. Owners report a dashboard or glovebox rattle, most often from a loose panel under the windshield or a part behind the factory radio . It tends to get worse in cold weather .
  8. Owners report unexplained battery drain . Ask if the car has ever gone dead after sitting for a few days . A healthy 2008 Fit should draw roughly 15 to 17 milliamps (mA) of power with everything off . A milliamp (mA) is a small unit of electrical current .

Owners also report a specific rust spot worth checking, especially on a car that has spent winters in a state that salts its roads . Look where the rear quarter panel meets the rear bumper cover . Also check the seam where a plastic cover hides the joint between the quarter panel, the rocker panel, and the underside of the car . This pocket collects grit and moisture, and it is reported as the first place rust shows up on this car . Left unchecked, it can progress to holes in the driver's rear wheel well .

One test drive, eight checks

You can check most of these trouble spots on a single test drive . Shift firmly into and out of gear and listen for a clunk, which points to worn motor mounts . Accelerate under moderate load and listen for a new ticking or rattling beyond the engine's normal light idle sound . On a manual car, push the clutch pedal fully and feel for sponginess or a change in pedal travel . On an automatic car, hold a steady 55 to 65 miles per hour and feel for shudder or hesitation . Run the air conditioning on max for several minutes and confirm it stays cold the whole time, not only for the first minute . Drive over a few bumps and rough pavement, listening for suspension clunks and dashboard rattles .

What a used 2008 Fit costs in today's market

Different pricing sources measure different things, so their numbers do not match, and you should not read them as contradicting each other .

Source What it measures Base-trim range
Kelley Blue Book (KBB) Dealer retail price for the base "Hatchback 4D" trim $4,575 to $5,850
KBB Estimated trade-in value (what a dealer might offer you for your own car) About $2,550 to $2,825
KBB Estimated private-party value (selling car to car, not through a dealer) About $3,925 to $5,125
Edmunds Appraisal-tool trade-in value About $1,022
Edmunds Appraisal-tool private-party value About $1,446
CarGurus Average price on cars actually listed for sale right now About $6,521, with real listings ranging from roughly $2,600 to over $15,000 depending on the city

Note: KBB's own figures for 2026 show the Sport trim pricing lower than the base "Hatchback 4D" trim . This book uses the Hatchback 4D numbers above for base-trim buying guidance, not the Sport numbers .

KBB and Edmunds are appraisal-tool estimates, meaning they assume an average, typical-mileage, typical-condition car, and both run lower than real asking prices . CarGurus reflects actual asking prices on real listings . That is why its number runs higher, and why it varies so much by city, mileage, and condition . This price gap between the two kinds of sources is a known, logged disagreement between them, not a mistake in one source or the other . A fair way to read all this: expect a rough floor around $1,000 to $3,000 for a high-mileage, rough-condition example . Expect a realistic asking price of roughly $3,000 to $10,000 or more for a clean, lower-mileage example . Use these numbers as a starting point, since used-car prices move over time and by region .

What the odometer is likely to show

At the commonly cited average of about 12,000 miles driven per year, an 18-year-old car would show roughly 216,000 miles by simple math . The US Federal Highway Administration reports drivers actually average 13,400 to 14,300 miles per year . That would put the same math closer to 241,000 to 257,000 miles . Real 2008 Fit listings show much wider variation than either estimate. Two examples showed 206,169 miles and 128,493 miles . Many owners drive less than the national average, especially as a car gets older . Treat any reading from roughly 100,000 to 250,000 or more miles as plausible and normal for a car this age .

Routine maintenance, such as oil, filters, brakes, and tires, is covered elsewhere in this book . Beyond that, expect at least some of the eight trouble spots above to show wear, or to have already been fixed, on any real 100,000-plus-mile example . Clearcoat is the clear protective top layer over the paint. Also check the paint on horizontal surfaces like the roof and hood for clearcoat peeling, another item owners report on this car . Use what you find as a talking point for negotiating the price . Do not treat it as an automatic reason to walk away from an otherwise well cared for car .

Quick check

  1. Name the five or six areas a general pre-purchase inspection covers.
  2. Which two recalls from Chapter 18 only apply under certain conditions, and what are those conditions?
  3. Name three of the eight owner-reported trouble spots, and how you would check each one on a test drive.
  4. Why do KBB's and CarGurus' prices for the same car look so different?
  5. What odometer range should you consider normal for an 18-year-old Fit, and why is it so wide?

Part VMaintenance

Chapter 21The maintenance schedule

What you will learn

  • What the Maintenance Minder is, and why your Fit has no printed mileage chart for service
  • How the Maintenance Minder decides when your car needs service
  • What the codes "Item A," "Item B," and the numbered sub items mean
  • Five fixed rules that apply no matter what the Maintenance Minder says
  • What you, the owner, should check on your own, and how often
  • An honest note on cost, since this chapter's sources do not cover shop prices

The short version

Your 2008 Fit does not use a printed table of miles for oil changes and other service. Instead, a computer called the Maintenance Minder watches how you drive and shows a code on the dashboard display when something is due. A handful of items still follow a fixed schedule no matter what that display says, and this chapter lists every one of them.

The full explanation

What the Maintenance Minder is

The Maintenance Minder is a computer system built into your Fit. It tracks your driving and tells you when service is due, instead of relying on a fixed mileage chart. Many older Honda models, and some other cars, print a table with two columns: one for normal driving and one for severe driving, such as short trips or dusty roads. The driver has to decide which column applies. Your Fit's owner's manual has no such table. The Maintenance Minder computer factors driving severity into its own math, so you do not have to guess which column you fall into.

The system tracks something called oil life. Oil life is a percentage the computer calculates from your driving conditions and how many times your engine has turned over. As oil life drops, the display eventually shows a maintenance code telling you it is time for service. Chapter 7 shows you exactly what this looks like on your instrument panel and covers every other dashboard light and icon.

Reading the codes: Item A, Item B, and sub items

When the Maintenance Minder decides service is due, it shows one main code and, sometimes, one or more numbered sub items.

Main Item A means it is time to replace your engine oil. Main Item B means more. It calls for a new engine oil and filter, plus a shop inspection. That inspection covers your front and rear brakes, your parking brake adjustment, and your tie rod ends, steering gear box, and boots. It also covers your suspension parts, driveshaft boots, and brake hoses and lines. Last, it covers all your fluid levels, your exhaust system, and your fuel lines.

Alongside Item A or B, a sub item number can appear for other jobs the Maintenance Minder tracks. These include rotating your tires, replacing your engine air filter, and replacing your cabin air filter, the filter that cleans the air coming through your heater and air conditioner. They also include inspecting and adjusting your drive belt, replacing your transmission fluid, replacing your spark plugs, and replacing your engine coolant, the liquid that keeps your engine from overheating. None of these sub items has a fixed mileage number printed in your owner's manual. The Maintenance Minder decides the timing for each one based on how you drive.

One more item rides along with services A, B, 1, 2, or 3: your valve clearance. Valve clearance is the small gap inside the engine that lets the valves open and close correctly. It gets checked at those visits, but it only gets adjusted if the engine sounds noisy. There is no separate mileage number for this adjustment. It is tied to whichever service visit comes up next.

Five fixed rules that beat the Maintenance Minder

Most service on your Fit runs on the Maintenance Minder's own timing. But five items follow a fixed rule that applies no matter what the dashboard display says. These are the only true "fixed schedule" items in the whole book for this car, and every new driver and every parent should know all five.

  1. Brake fluid, every 3 years. Change it every 3 years regardless of any message on the display.
  2. Idle speed, every 160,000 miles (256,000 km). Have it inspected at this mileage, independent of the Maintenance Minder.
  3. Engine oil, at least once a year. If a full 12 months pass since your last reset without the display asking for an oil change, change the oil anyway.
  4. Air filter and cabin filter, every 15,000 miles (24,000 km) if conditions are dusty or sooty. If you drive in dusty conditions, replace your engine air filter at this interval instead of waiting for the display. If you drive mainly in urban areas with heavy soot from industry or diesel traffic, replace your cabin filter at this same interval.
  5. Automatic transmission fluid, on two special conditions. Say you drive an automatic-transmission Fit regularly at low speed in mountains. At 60,000 miles (100,000 km), have the fluid changed. Repeat that change every 30,000 miles (48,000 km) from then on. Say instead that you tow a Fit behind a motorhome. Change the automatic transmission fluid every 2 years, or every 30,000 miles (48,000 km) — whichever happens first. Both of these rules apply to the automatic transmission only.

Outside of these five, your owner's manual names no other severe-condition trigger with a fixed number. If you are shopping for parts or fluid, note that the Fit's automatic transmission fluid, Honda ATF-Z1, has since been discontinued industry-wide. Ask your parts store for its replacement, Honda ATF DW-1, if ATF-Z1 is not on the shelf.

What you check yourself, and how often

Between service visits, a few checks are yours to do, and none of them need a mechanic.

  • Engine oil level and engine coolant level: check both every time you stop for fuel.
  • Automatic transmission fluid level and brake fluid level: check monthly.
  • Tire pressure and tread: check monthly.
  • Headlights, parking lights, taillights, high-mount brake light, and license plate lights: check monthly that they all work.
  • Battery condition: check monthly using the test indicator window on top of the battery.

A note on cost

This chapter's sources are Honda's own owner's manual and parts-fitment pages. None of them give a dollar figure for what any of this costs, whether you do it yourself or pay a shop. We could not confirm DIY-versus-shop costs for the maintenance schedule from this work package's sources. Check current prices with a local shop or parts store, or look for cost information in Chapters 22 through 33, which cover each maintenance job on its own.

Step by step: Making sense of your maintenance display

  1. Look at your information display for an oil life percentage or a maintenance code.
  2. If the code is Item A, plan to change your engine oil.
  3. If the code is Item B, plan to change your engine oil and filter, and have the longer inspection list checked too.
  4. Check for any sub item number next to the main code, and match it to the job list above.
  5. Even with no code showing, change your oil once a year, change your brake fluid every 3 years, and check idle speed at 160,000 miles.
  6. If you drive in dusty or sooty conditions, or tow, or drive slowly in mountains with an automatic, apply the matching fixed rule above instead of waiting on the display.

What can go wrong

Because most of your Fit's maintenance runs on a computer instead of a printed chart, it is easy to assume the dashboard will catch everything. It will not. The five fixed rules in this chapter run on their own clock, separate from the Maintenance Minder, and the display will not necessarily remind you about them. A parent or new driver who only reacts to dashboard codes could miss a 3-year brake fluid change or a dusty-conditions air filter swap for years. Write these five rules down, or set your own calendar reminders, instead of relying on the display alone.

Quick check

  1. What does the Maintenance Minder do differently from an older car's printed service chart?
  2. What is the difference between Main Item A and Main Item B?
  3. Name all five fixed rules that apply no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows.
  4. How often should you check your tire pressure and your automatic transmission fluid level on your own?
  5. Why can't this chapter tell you exactly how much a maintenance visit will cost?

Chapter 22Tools and Supplies for a Starter Garage

What you will learn

  • Every tool this book's maintenance chapters actually call for
  • What each tool does, in plain words, the first time it comes up
  • Which tools you need for almost every job, and which ones are for bigger jobs only
  • A sensible order to buy tools in, so you are not buying everything at once
  • Safety gear and shop supplies to keep on hand besides tools

The short version

This chapter lists only the tools named in this book's own maintenance procedures, nothing extra. You do not need a full mechanic's toolbox to start. A handful of tools cover most jobs, and a few more only matter once you tackle brakes or oil changes.

The full explanation

Turning and tightening tools

Most jobs in this book start with turning a bolt. A ratchet is a handle that turns a socket, a metal cap that fits over a bolt head, in one direction without you lifting it off between turns. A breaker bar is a plain, longer handle with no ratcheting action, used when a bolt is stuck and needs extra force. You need a ratchet or breaker bar with sockets sized to your car's bolts for the oil drain plug and for many other jobs.

A torque wrench is a tool that tells you exactly how hard you are tightening a bolt, by feel or by a dial. It measures in pound-feet (lbf·ft) or newton-meters (N·m). You need one that reaches at least 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m), the torque for your wheel nuts. The same torque wrench should also reach down to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m), the torque for your oil drain bolt. Guessing at torque by feel alone risks a leak or a stripped bolt, so this tool is worth buying early.

An oil filter wrench is a tool shaped to grip the round oil filter so you can twist it loose. The owner's manual itself calls this "a special wrench, available from your dealer." You also need a lug wrench, or a socket that fits your wheel nuts plus a breaker bar, any time you take a wheel off. A flat-tip (flathead) screwdriver and a Phillips screwdriver cover the clips and screws on your air filter housing.

Lifting and support tools

A floor jack is a tool on wheels that lifts one corner or one end of the car using a hand pump or lever. Buy one rated for at least your car's weight. The small scissor jack that lives in your trunk is only rated for a roadside flat tire, not for working underneath the car.

Jack stands are metal supports you slide under the car once it is lifted, so the car's weight rests on solid steel instead of the jack. Buy at least two, and ideally four, each rated for at least your car's weight. Chapter 23 covers how to use a jack and jack stands safely, and that chapter's warnings apply every time you use these tools.

Wheel chocks are wedges you place against a tire that stays on the ground, so the car cannot roll while another corner is lifted. You need at least two.

Tire and brake tools

A tire pressure gauge measures the air pressure inside a tire, in pounds per square inch (psi). The owner's manual recommends using your own gauge every time, instead of a different shop gauge each visit, so your readings stay consistent. You will also want a portable inflator, or access to a gas station air pump, to add air.

A flashlight lets you see the brake pad through the wheel spokes without removing the wheel; a ruler or a dedicated pad-thickness gauge is a helpful, optional extra.

Once you move on to replacing brake pads, a C-clamp, or a dedicated brake piston tool, pushes the caliper piston back into its housing to make room for new pads. Rear drum brakes need their own set of tools. Use large C-clamps or brake-spring pliers to compress the brake shoes, plus a drum brake spring and hold-down tool kit to handle the springs and pins safely. A wire brush and brake-parts cleaner, a solvent spray made for cleaning brake dust, are needed for both front pad and rear drum jobs.

Bleeding your brakes means pushing old fluid and trapped air out of the brake lines. For that job, you need a wrench sized to the small bleeder screw on each brake. You also need clear plastic tubing that fits snugly over it, and a clean catch bottle for the old fluid. A turkey baster or a fluid syringe helps remove old fluid from the reservoir before you start.

Safety gear and shop supplies

Nitrile gloves and safety glasses protect your hands and eyes any time you work with oil, fresh or used. Keep a stack of shop rags on hand for wiping up drips and cleaning parts. You will also want a sealed container to carry used oil to a recycling center, since used oil cannot go in the trash or on the ground. For brake work, keep high-temperature brake grease on hand for pad backing and slide pins. For the parking-brake lever pivot on drum brakes, use a specific product called Molykote 44MA, or an equivalent high-temperature brake grease.

Chapters 24 through 27 name the exact oil, filters, pads, shoes, and fluid your car needs. This chapter only covers the tools that install them.

Step by step: Building your toolkit in a sensible order
