You do not need every tool on day one. Buy in this order, based on which jobs come first in this book.

  1. Buy a tire pressure gauge first. It is cheap, and Chapter 26 asks you to check your tires often.
  2. Add nitrile gloves, safety glasses, and shop rags. Every job after this uses at least one of them.
  3. Add a ratchet or breaker bar with a socket set, a lug wrench, and a flashlight. These cover inspection jobs and light repairs.
  4. Before your first oil change, add a torque wrench, an oil filter wrench, a drain pan, and a funnel.
  5. Before you lift the car for any reason, add a floor jack, jack stands, and wheel chocks, and read Chapter 23 first.
  6. Before a brake job, add the C-clamp or piston tool, wire brush, brake-parts cleaner, and, for rear drums, the spring and hold-down tool kit.
  7. Before bleeding brakes, add the bleeder wrench, clear tubing, a catch bottle, and a turkey baster or syringe.

What can go wrong

Several procedure cards behind this book flag one bolt size or another as unconfirmed, telling you to check your own car before buying a socket rather than guess. Buying the wrong socket size wastes a trip to the store, so measure or check the bolt on your own car first when a size is not given. A torque wrench is not optional if a job names a torque number. Tightening by feel alone can leave a bolt too loose, which can fail later, or too tight, which can strip threads or crack a part. Do not swap a floor jack alone for jack stands. The floor jack lifts the car, but only the jack stands are meant to hold its weight while you work underneath.

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between a ratchet and a breaker bar?
  2. Why does this book recommend a torque wrench instead of tightening bolts by feel?
  3. Name two tools you need before you can safely lift the car and work underneath it.
  4. Why should you use your own tire pressure gauge every time instead of a different gauge at each stop?
  5. What should you do if a procedure card does not give you an exact bolt size?

Chapter 23Safety First — Jacking, Jack Stands, Wheel Chocks, Hot Parts, Fluids

What you will learn

  • The one rule that matters most in this whole chapter: never trust a jack alone
  • How to jack up your Fit and support it safely on jack stands
  • Why hot parts under the hood and under the car can burn you
  • How to handle engine oil, brake fluid, and other fluids without hurting yourself or your car
  • What can go wrong when someone skips a safety step

The short version

A jack lifts your car, but a jack can slip or roll off its point without warning. Jack stands, wedges you place under the car once it is up, are what actually holds the car's weight while you work underneath it. Every job in Chapters 24 through 27 that lifts the car depends on you doing this one step correctly.

The full explanation

Why a jack alone is not enough

A jack is a tool that lifts one corner or end of a car. A jack stand is a separate metal support you slide underneath once the car is up, so its weight rests on solid steel instead of the jack. The owner's manual itself warns that a car can easily roll off a jack and seriously injure anyone underneath it. That is why every maintenance job in this book that needs the car off the ground also needs jack stands, not the jack by itself.

Your Fit's trunk holds a small scissor jack, meant only for changing a flat tire on the side of the road. A floor jack is a separate, larger tool on wheels, rated to hold much more weight, and it is what you use for jack-stand work in the garage. Buy a floor jack and jack stands that are each rated for at least your car's weight. Do this before you start any job in Chapters 24 through 27 that needs the car raised.

Where to lift, and where never to lift

Your owner's manual shows a molded mark on the underbody near each wheel. This is the lift point built for the small scissor jack. For jack-stand work, there are also reinforced seams along each rocker panel (the body panel below the doors) and along the front subframe, set well back from the bumper. These reinforced seam points come from community reports, not from the owner's manual itself, so check them against your own car and a second source before you rely on them.

Never set a jack or a jack stand under the floor pan, the exhaust, a plastic under-cover, or a random point on the frame. Any of these can bend, crush, or puncture under the car's weight.

Hot parts, fluids, and other everyday hazards

Beyond jacking, a few hazards come up again and again in Chapters 24 through 27. An engine, its exhaust, and its brakes all get hot enough to burn skin, so let them cool before you touch anything nearby. Used and fresh engine oil can irritate your skin and eyes, so wear gloves and eye protection whenever you handle it. Brake fluid splashes easily, damages paint on contact, and hurts eyes, so eye protection matters there too. Brake dust should never be blown around with compressed air or brushed dry; use a brake-parts cleaner spray instead, and never breathe it in.

Used oil is hazardous to the environment. Never pour it in the trash or on the ground; take it to a recycling center in a sealed container.

Step by step: Jack up the car and set it on jack stands

Safety warnings, before you touch the jack: - Do not ever get any part of your body under a car supported only by a jack. The car can roll off a jack and seriously injure anyone underneath. - Always work on a flat, level, hard surface. Never jack up a car on a slope, on soft ground, or on an incline. - Always set the parking brake and chock the wheels that will stay on the ground before you start jacking. - Always use the vehicle's marked or reinforced jacking points, never the floor pan, exhaust, plastic under-covers, or a random point on the frame. - Do not exceed a jack's or a jack stand's rated weight capacity. Check the rating sticker before you buy or borrow one. - Always give the car a firm shake test on the stands before working underneath, to confirm it will not slip.

Steps: 1. Park on a flat, level, solid surface and set the parking brake. 2. Chock the wheels that will remain on the ground. 3. Locate the lift point: the owner's manual shows a molded mark on the underbody near each wheel, for the factory scissor jack. 4. For jack-stand work, also find the reinforced pinch-weld seams on each rocker panel and the front subframe, well back from the bumper. 5. Treat step 4's points with caution: they are community-reported, not from the owner's manual, so double-check against your own car and a second reference. 6. Position the floor jack under the chosen point and raise the car slowly. 7. Stop as soon as the tire lifts a couple of inches and check that the jack is still centered on the point. 8. Continue raising to the height you need. 9. Slide a jack stand under a solid point near the jack and adjust it to the right height. 10. Slowly lower the floor jack until the car's weight rests on the jack stand. 11. Repeat steps 6 through 10 for the other end or side, if you are lifting the whole car. 12. Give the car a firm push and shake test before you get near or under it. 13. Leave the floor jack nearby as a backup, but do not rely on it to hold weight once the stands are set. 14. When done, reverse the process: raise slightly with the floor jack, remove the stands, then lower slowly and fully.

How you know it worked: the car sits solidly on the stands with no rocking when pushed. All four (or two) contact points look evenly loaded. The wheels you did not lift stay chocked and on the ground the whole time.

What can go wrong

The most common mistake is placing a jack or a jack stand on the floor pan, a plastic cover, or the exhaust instead of a reinforced point. That mistake can bend the body or crush the part. The second most common, and most dangerous, mistake is working under a car held up by the floor jack alone, with no stands underneath. Jacking on a slope or soft ground can let the car shift, or let the jack sink into the ground while you work. Forgetting to chock the wheels that stay on the ground can let the whole car roll while one corner is in the air.

Quick check

  1. Why is a jack alone not enough to support the car while you work underneath it?
  2. What is the difference between the trunk's scissor jack and a floor jack?
  3. Name two places you should never set a jack or a jack stand.
  4. What should you do before you fully trust the car's weight to the jack stands?
  5. Why should you never blow brake dust around with compressed air?

Chapter 24Oil and Filter Change

What you will learn

  • Why your Fit needs an oil and filter change, and what oil actually does
  • The exact oil type and amount your Fit needs
  • The full step-by-step process, from warming the engine to disposing of the old oil
  • The one torque number that matters most in this job
  • Common mistakes that cause leaks or engine damage

The short version

Engine oil lubricates the moving parts inside your engine and carries away heat and dirt. Your Fit needs 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of 5W-20 oil and a new filter each time you change it. This is rated as a job that needs the car raised on jack stands, so read Chapter 23 first if you have not already.

The full explanation

Why oil matters, and what your Fit needs

Oil coats the metal parts inside your engine so they can move against each other without wearing out. Over time, oil picks up heat, dirt, and tiny bits of metal, and it needs to be drained out and replaced.

Your owner's manual calls for API Premium-grade 5W-20 detergent oil, carrying the API Certification Seal marked "For Gasoline Engines." Honda's own motor oil is the preferred choice, though a synthetic oil that meets the same seal and viscosity is acceptable too. No oil additive is needed or recommended.

A full oil change, with a new filter, takes 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of oil. Without a filter change, it takes slightly less, 3.6 US quarts (3.4 L). After a full engine rebuild, when the engine is completely dry, it takes 4.4 US quarts (4.2 L). You will not need that last figure for a routine oil change. It is here so you recognize it if you ever see it on a parts receipt or in a shop's paperwork.

When to change it

Your Fit uses a computer called the Maintenance Minder instead of a printed mileage chart, as Chapter 21 explains in full. Even so, one fixed rule always applies: if a full 12 months pass without the display asking for an oil change, change the oil anyway.

The one number to memorize: drain bolt torque

The bolt at the bottom of your oil pan that lets old oil drain out is called the drain bolt. Tighten it to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m, 4.0 kgf·m) with a torque wrench, no more and no less. Honda gives no specific torque number for the oil filter itself. Instead, install a new filter according to the instructions printed on it. This usually means turning it by hand until it is snug against the engine, plus a small extra turn.

Step by step: Change the engine oil and filter

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always let the engine run first, then shut it off. The manual says to run the engine to normal temperature before draining so the oil flows and carries out contaminants. - Always let the engine and exhaust cool before you touch anything underneath the car, once you shut it off. Hot oil and hot metal can burn you. - Do not get under a vehicle held up only by a jack. Use jack stands, as Chapter 23 explains. - Always wear eye protection and gloves. Used oil and fresh oil can irritate skin and eyes. - Do not dump used oil in the trash or on the ground. It is hazardous to the environment. - Always clean up any spilled oil right away. Spilled oil can damage engine parts and creates a slip hazard.

Steps: 1. Warm up the engine by running it, then shut it off and set the parking brake. 2. Raise the front of the car and support it on jack stands, following Chapter 23. 3. Open the hood and remove the engine oil fill cap so air can enter as oil drains. 4. Slide the drain pan under the drain bolt at the bottom of the engine. 5. Loosen and remove the drain bolt and its washer with your socket. 6. Let all the oil drain fully into the pan; this can take several minutes. 7. Wipe the drain area, then reinstall the drain bolt with a brand-new washer. 8. Tighten the drain bolt to 29 lbf·ft (39 N·m) with a torque wrench. 9. Move the drain pan under the oil filter and remove the old filter with the filter wrench. 10. Check that the old filter's rubber gasket did not stick to the engine; remove it if it did. 11. Wipe a thin film of new oil on the new filter's gasket, then thread it on by hand until snug. 12. Lower the car back to the ground and remove the jack stands. 13. Pour in 3.8 US quarts (3.6 L) of the recommended 5W-20 oil through the funnel. 14. Reinstall the oil fill cap and tighten it securely. 15. Start the engine and watch the oil pressure light; it should go out within 5 seconds. 16. Let the engine run a few minutes, then shut it off and check the drain bolt and filter for leaks. 17. Wait several more minutes, then check the level on the dipstick, the thin metal rod you pull out to see the oil level, and top up if needed. 18. Seal the used oil in a container and take it to a recycling center.

How you know it worked: the oil pressure warning light goes out within 5 seconds of starting the engine. There are no drips under the car after it has sat overnight. The dipstick reads between the marks.

What can go wrong

Overfilling the engine past the dipstick's upper mark can damage the engine; the manual warns against this directly. Forgetting the new drain-bolt washer, called a crush washer, causes a slow leak. Over-tightening the oil filter by hand past snug can make it very hard to remove next time and can crush its gasket. Not fully re-torquing the drain bolt, or over-torquing it, can strip the pan's threads. And working under a car held up by only a jack, instead of jack stands, is the most dangerous mistake on this list.

Quick check

  1. What kind and amount of oil does your Fit need for a full oil change with a new filter?
  2. What torque should you use on the oil drain bolt, and with what tool?
  3. Why should you run the engine before draining the oil, and let it cool before you go underneath?
  4. What is a crush washer, and what happens if you forget to replace it?
  5. What should the oil pressure light do within 5 seconds of starting the engine, and what does that tell you?

Chapter 25Air Filter and Cabin Filter

What you will learn

  • What the engine air filter and the cabin air filter each do, and why they are different
  • The exact part numbers Honda used for your Fit
  • When to replace each filter
  • How to replace both filters, step by step, with no jacking or fluids involved
  • Common mistakes that reduce airflow instead of improving it

The short version

Your Fit has two separate air filters. The engine air filter cleans air before it reaches the engine, and the cabin air filter cleans air coming through your heater and air conditioner. Both jobs are simple, need no tools beyond a screwdriver, and take well under 20 minutes combined.

The full explanation

Two filters, two jobs

The engine air filter sits in a black plastic housing in the engine bay and cleans the air the engine breathes before that air reaches the fuel injection system. Its part number is Honda 17220-PWA-505, and it fits the 2007-2008 Fit; this number replaced an older part, 17220-PWA-J10.

The cabin air filter, also called the dust and pollen filter, cleans the air that comes through your heater and air conditioner vents before it reaches you. Its part number is Honda 80291-SAA-J01, and it fits the 2007-2008 Fit; this number has since been superseded by 80291-SAA-316. This filter is fitted on models with air conditioning, per the owner's manual.

When to replace each one

Both filters follow the Maintenance Minder's own timing under normal driving, with no fixed mileage number printed in the owner's manual. Two exceptions apply. If you drive mostly in dusty conditions, replace the engine air filter every 15,000 miles (24,000 km). If you drive mostly in urban areas with heavy soot from industry or diesel traffic, replace the cabin filter every 15,000 miles (24,000 km). Replace it sooner if airflow from your heater or air conditioner feels weaker than usual.

Step by step

Replace the engine air filter

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always let the engine cool before opening the hood if it has just been running. Parts near the filter box can be hot. - Do not start the engine with the air filter housing open or the element removed.

Steps: 1. Open the hood and find the air filter housing, a black plastic box on the driver's side of the engine bay. 2. Release the clips holding the two halves of the housing together (there are usually four). 3. Lift the top half of the housing up and out of the way. 4. Note which way the old filter element sits before you remove it. 5. Lift out the old, dirty filter element. 6. Wipe out any dust or leaves from inside the housing with a dry rag. 7. Place the new filter element in the same orientation as the old one. 8. Close the top half of the housing and press down until every clip snaps shut. 9. Check that all clips are fully closed before starting the engine.

How you know it worked: the housing halves sit flush together, every clip is snapped closed, and the engine starts and idles normally.

Replace the cabin (dust and pollen) filter

Safety warnings, before you start: - Do not force the glove box past its stops. You can break the hinge or the stop tabs.

Steps: 1. Open the glove box fully. 2. Squeeze both sides of the glove box inward to release the stop tabs. 3. Let the glove box swing down out of the way. 4. Find the filter tray in the housing behind the glove box opening. 5. Pinch the two clips on the tray's left and right sides and pull the tray out. 6. Slide the old filter out of the tray. 7. Look at the arrow printed on the new filter's frame. 8. Slide the new filter into the tray with the arrow pointing down. 9. Push the tray back into the housing until both clips click. 10. Swing the glove box back up and press it closed.

How you know it worked: the glove box closes and latches normally, and air from the vents feels stronger than before if the old filter was clogged.

What can go wrong

Forcing a stiff clip on the engine air filter housing can crack the plastic. Installing the new engine air filter upside down or backward, or leaving one clip unlatched, lets unfiltered air leak past the filter instead of through it. On the cabin filter, installing it with the arrow pointing the wrong way can reduce airflow instead of improving it. Not seating both tray clips lets the tray rattle or fall loose later.

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between the engine air filter and the cabin air filter?
  2. What is the part number for your Fit's engine air filter, and what older number did it replace?
  3. Under what driving condition should you replace the engine air filter every 15,000 miles instead of waiting on the Maintenance Minder?
  4. Which way should the arrow point when you install a new cabin filter?
  5. What can happen if you force a stiff clip on the air filter housing instead of releasing it properly?

Chapter 26Tires — Pressure, Rotation, Wear, Replacement, the Spare

What you will learn

  • Your Fit's correct tire pressure, tire size, and wheel size
  • How to check and set tire pressure the right way
  • How to rotate your tires safely, and the torque your wheel nuts need
  • What to look for when checking tires for wear
  • Where to find the flat-tire and spare-tire change procedure in this book

The short version

Your Fit's base trim uses 32 psi (220 kPa) in every tire, front and rear, and the compact spare needs 60 psi (420 kPa). Checking pressure takes about 10 minutes and needs only a gauge. Rotating all four tires needs jack stands and a torque wrench, and takes about 45 minutes.

The full explanation

Your Fit's tire and wheel numbers

Your base-trim Fit's factory tire size is P175/65R14 81S, mounted on a 14-inch by 5.5J steel wheel with a full wheel cover. The wheel bolts on a 4x100 mm pattern, meaning four lug nuts arranged around a circle 100 mm across. The wheel's center bore, the hole in the middle that centers the wheel on the hub, measures 56.1 mm. Offset means how far the wheel's mounting face sits from its centerline. The factory wheel's offset is ET45, within a reported range of 43 to 47 mm for this tire and wheel combination.

Recommended pressure

Both base and Sport trims use the same cold tire pressure: 32 psi (220 kPa, 2.2 kgf/cm²), front and rear. The compact spare tire needs a higher pressure, 60 psi (420 kPa, 4.2 kgf/cm²), since it is a smaller, temporary tire. "Cold" means the tire has been parked for at least 3 hours, or driven less than 1 mile (1.6 km). A hot tire reads 4 to 6 psi higher than its true pressure, and this can fool you into underfilling it.

Rotating your tires

Rotating tires means moving each wheel to a new position on the car, so all four tires wear more evenly. This is a Maintenance Minder sub item, with no fixed mileage number printed in the owner's manual; the Minder decides the timing. Whichever pattern you use, torque every wheel nut to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) once the car is back on the ground.

Checking for wear

While you check pressure or rotate tires, look each tire over for bulges, cuts, or objects stuck in the tread. These sources do not give a specific tread-depth number for deciding when a tire needs replacing. We could not confirm a tread-wear replacement number for the 2008 Fit from this chapter's sources. Check your tire's own wear indicators, small raised bars molded into the tread, or ask a tire shop.

The spare tire

Your Fit carries a compact spare tire for roadside use. Chapter 16 covers the full step-by-step procedure for changing a flat tire and using that spare, including the jacking steps, so this chapter does not repeat it. Come back to Chapter 16 if you have a flat on the road; use this chapter for routine pressure checks and rotations instead.

Step by step

Check and set your tire pressure

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always check pressure when the tires are cold: parked 3+ hours, or driven less than 1 mile (1.6 km). Hot tires read 4-6 psi high and will fool you into underfilling them. - Do not let air out of a hot tire to try to match the cold-pressure number; you will underinflate it. - Always check the spare tire too, not just the four road tires.

Steps: 1. Find the recommended pressures on the driver's doorjamb sticker, or use 32 psi front and rear as a cold-pressure fallback. 2. Remove the valve cap from the first tire and set it somewhere you won't lose it. 3. Press the tire gauge firmly onto the valve stem until the hiss stops and read the number. 4. Compare the reading to the recommended pressure for that tire. 5. If the pressure is low, add air in short bursts, checking often, until it matches the number. 6. If the pressure is too high, press the small pin inside the valve to release a little air, then recheck. 7. Reinstall the valve cap. 8. Repeat steps 2 through 7 for the other three tires. 9. Check the compact spare the same way; it should be 60 psi (420 kPa). 10. While you are down there, look each tire over for bulges, cuts, or objects stuck in the tread.

How you know it worked: every tire, including the spare, reads within about 1 psi of its recommended cold pressure, and no tire shows a bulge, cut, or embedded object.

Rotate the tires

Safety warnings, before you start: - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use four jack stands. - Always loosen every wheel nut a bit before you raise that corner off the ground. - Always chock the wheels that stay on the ground before you start jacking. - Do not fully tighten wheel nuts with the wheel hanging free; snug them, lower the car, then torque them on the ground.

Steps: 1. Park on level, solid ground and set the parking brake. 2. Chock the wheels you are not lifting. 3. Loosen each wheel nut about a half turn while all four tires are still on the ground. 4. Raise one corner and support it on a jack stand; remove that wheel. 5. Repeat step 4 for the other three corners so all four wheels are off. 6. Move each wheel to its new position, front-to-back and side-to-side per the rotation chart, unless your tires are directional. A directional tire has a tread pattern designed to roll only one way, marked with an arrow on the sidewall. 7. If your tires are marked directional, only swap front-to-back on the same side, never side-to-side. 8. Put each wheel back on its new hub and thread the nuts on by hand. 9. Snug the nuts in a crisscross (star) pattern with the wrench, one corner at a time. 10. Lower the car until the tire touches the ground but still has some jack support. 11. Torque each wheel nut to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in the crisscross pattern. 12. Lower the car fully and remove the jack stands. 13. Check and set the pressure in all four tires once they are all back on the ground.

How you know it worked: every wheel nut is torqued to 80 lbf·ft. No wheel wobbles when you drive at low speed. The tires are now in their new rotation positions.

What can go wrong

Checking pressure right after driving gives a falsely high reading. Using a different gauge each time, without noticing it reads differently from your own gauge, causes confusion over time. Forgetting the spare tire is easy, and it slowly loses pressure over months even if it is never used. Ignoring a low-tire-pressure warning light and skipping the physical check anyway defeats the point of having the light.

During rotation, trying to fully torque a wheel nut while the wheel still hangs free in the air can leave it improperly seated. Skipping the crisscross tightening pattern can seat the wheel unevenly. Rotating directional tires side-to-side puts the tread pattern running backward. Forgetting to re-torque wheel nuts after the first 50 to 100 miles of driving on a re-used wheel is another common miss.

Quick check

  1. What pressure should every tire on your base-trim Fit hold, and what pressure does the spare need?
  2. Why should you check tire pressure only when the tires are cold?
  3. What torque should you use on your wheel nuts, and when in the rotation process should you apply it?
  4. What is a directional tire, and how does rotating one differ from rotating a regular tire?
  5. Where in this book should you look for the full flat-tire change procedure?

Chapter 27Brakes — Inspection, Pads, Rotors, Fluid

What you will learn

  • Why your Fit's front and rear brakes are not the same type
  • How to inspect your front brake pads without removing the wheel
  • How to replace front brake pads, and how to replace rear brake shoes
  • How to bleed your brakes and what fluid to use
  • Safety warnings that apply to every brake job in this chapter

The short version

Your 2008 Fit's front brakes are disc brakes, and its rear brakes are drum brakes, not disc. This matters because the rear job uses shoes and springs instead of pads and a caliper, and it is a different repair. Every brake job in this chapter needs jack stands, eye protection, and patience with springs under tension.

The full explanation

Front disc brakes, rear drum brakes

A disc brake uses a flat metal disc, called a rotor, that spins with the wheel. A caliper is a clamp that squeezes flat pads against both sides of the rotor to slow it down. A drum brake works differently. A hollow metal drum spins with the wheel, and curved brake shoes press outward against the inside of the drum to slow it down. Inside the drum, a small threaded part called the self-adjuster (or just the adjuster) takes up slack as the shoes wear down. This keeps the brake working correctly without you adjusting it by hand every time.

Honda's own owner's manual states plainly that your Fit's front brakes are disc, and its rear brakes are drum. This applies across the 2008 Fit lineup, with no separate statement carving out a different design for base trim. Do not assume your rear brakes have pads and a caliper like the front; they do not.

Brake fluid

Your brake system, and your clutch system if you drive a manual, both use Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3. A DOT 3 or DOT 4 fluid from a sealed container works as a temporary substitute. Never use DOT 5 brake fluid; it is not compatible with your Fit's braking system and can cause serious damage. On a manual-transmission Fit, the clutch pedal is operated by hydraulic fluid from the same specification, held in its own reservoir with a gray cap. Replace your brake fluid every 3 years, no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows.

Torque and bolt sizes: what is confirmed, and what is not

Your wheel nuts, front or rear, torque to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) whenever a wheel comes off for brake work. Owners report a front caliper bracket-to-knuckle bolt torque of around 80 lbf·ft. This number comes from two community sources discussing a Fit Sport, not a confirmed base-trim source, so treat it as a starting point, not a certainty. No confirmed number exists yet for a rear caliper bracket bolt on this car. Since your Fit's rear brakes are drum, not disc, there is no rear caliper to begin with.

Step by step

Inspect the front brake pads through the wheel

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always let the brakes cool before you get close to the wheel if you just drove. The caliper and rotor can be very hot. - Do not touch the wheel or brake parts with bare hands right after driving.

Steps: 1. Park on level ground and set the parking brake. 2. Turn the steering wheel toward you so you can see through the wheel spokes at that corner. 3. Shine the flashlight through the spokes at the caliper, the metal bracket that squeezes the pads onto the rotor. 4. Look for the pad material pressed against the flat, shiny rotor surface on each side of the caliper. 5. Estimate the pad's thickness; a healthy pad is clearly thicker than a thin metal backing plate. 6. Compare the inside and outside pad if you can see both; they should wear about evenly. 7. Repeat steps 2 through 6 on the other front wheel. 8. Listen for a metallic screeching sound the next time you brake gently; the manual says this warns you the pads need replacing.

How you know it worked: you can see both pads on each front wheel and estimate their thickness. Or, you learn you cannot see enough, and you need to remove the wheel or have a shop check them.

Replace the front brake pads

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always wear eye protection. Brake dust and springs under tension can hurt your eyes. - Do not breathe brake dust; never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts, per the manual's own maintenance-safety caution. Use brake-parts cleaner instead. - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. - Always work on one side at a time and leave the other wheel on the ground so you have a reference for how parts go back together. - Do not let the caliper hang by its brake hose; support or hang it so the hose has no weight on it. - Always pump the brake pedal several times before driving, until it feels firm, before you rely on the brakes again.

Steps: 1. Loosen the front wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands. 2. Remove the wheel and set it aside. 3. Look at how the old pads and springs sit before you remove anything, or take a photo. 4. Remove the caliper slide-pin bolts and lift the caliper off the bracket. 5. Support the caliper with a hook or bungee so it does not hang by the hose. 6. Pull the old pads out of the bracket. 7. Use the C-clamp against the old pad to push the caliper piston back into its bore. 8. Clean the bracket and slide pins with a wire brush and brake-parts cleaner. 9. Apply a thin coat of brake grease to the pad backing and slide pins. 10. Place the new pads into the bracket. 11. Slide the caliper back over the new pads and reinstall the slide-pin bolts. 12. Reinstall the wheel and snug the nuts by hand. 13. Lower the car, then torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in a crisscross pattern. 14. Repeat steps 1 through 13 on the other front wheel. 15. Pump the brake pedal several times with the engine off until it feels firm before driving. 16. Check the brake fluid reservoir level and top it off if needed. 17. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently before returning to normal driving.

How you know it worked: the brake pedal feels firm, not soft or spongy. The car stops evenly with no pulling to one side. There is no grinding or squealing under light braking.

Replace the rear brake shoes (drum brakes)

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always wear eye protection. Springs are under strong tension and can snap free unexpectedly. - Do not breathe brake dust; never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts. Use brake-parts cleaner instead. - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. - Always work on one side at a time, using the untouched side as your reference for how everything goes back together. - Do not get your fingers between a compressed spring and the backing plate. - Always keep grease off the brake shoe linings and the drum's inner surface; grease on the linings ruins braking. - Always press the brake pedal several times after reassembly, before driving, to seat the shoes and set the self-adjuster.

Steps: 1. Loosen the rear wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands. 2. Remove the wheel and set it aside. 3. Release the parking brake, then pull the drum straight off the hub (tap gently if it is stuck). 4. Take a photo of the spring and lever layout before touching anything. 5. Unhook the lower return spring with your spring tool or pliers. 6. Unhook the upper return spring the same way. 7. Remove the hold-down springs and pins that pin each shoe to the backing plate. 8. Lift out the old shoes together with the adjuster and parking-brake link still attached. 9. Transfer the adjuster, parking-brake lever, and springs onto the new shoes in the same layout as the photo. 10. Apply a thin dab of high-temperature grease to the backing plate's contact pads and the parking-brake lever pivot, keeping grease off the linings. 11. Set the new shoes onto the backing plate and reinstall the hold-down pins and springs. 12. Hook the lower return spring back into place. 13. Use your C-clamps or spring pliers to compress the shoes enough to hook the upper return spring. 14. Check that the shoes sit fully in their bosses (the raised mounting pads that hold each shoe in place) and the adjuster turns freely. 15. Slide the drum back onto the hub. 16. Reinstall the wheel and snug the nuts by hand. 17. Lower the car, then torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf·ft (108 N·m) in a crisscross pattern. 18. Repeat steps 1 through 17 on the other rear wheel. 19. Press the brake pedal firmly several times with the engine off until the pedal feels normal. 20. Adjust the parking brake if it now feels loose. 21. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently before returning to normal driving.

How you know it worked: the brake pedal feels firm, and the parking brake holds the car on a hill. Both rear wheels turn freely with light drag when the car is off the ground. There is no grinding or dragging noise while driving.

Bleed the brakes

Safety warnings, before you start: - Always wear eye protection. Brake fluid can splash and it damages paint and eyes. - Do not let the brake fluid reservoir run dry during bleeding, or you will draw air back into the lines and have to start over. - Do not reuse fluid that comes out of the bleeder; only use fresh fluid from a sealed container. - Always keep brake fluid off painted surfaces; wipe up any spill immediately. - Do not drive the car until the pedal feels firm and consistent, not soft or sinking.

Steps: 1. Remove old fluid from the reservoir with the baster, leaving a little so it does not run dry. 2. Fill the reservoir with fresh Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3. 3. Slide clear tubing over the bleeder screw at the first wheel and route the other end into your catch bottle. 4. Have your helper press the brake pedal down and hold it. 5. Open the bleeder screw briefly to let fluid and air escape, then close it before the pedal is released. 6. Have your helper release the pedal only after the bleeder screw is closed. 7. Repeat steps 4 through 6 until no more air bubbles appear in the tubing. 8. Check the reservoir level and top it off before moving to the next wheel. 9. Move to the next wheel and repeat steps 3 through 8. 10. Recheck the reservoir level after all four wheels and fill to the MAX mark. 11. Press the brake pedal several times and confirm it feels firm and consistent, not soft.

How you know it worked: the pedal feels firm on the first press, not soft or sinking to the floor. No air bubbles appear in the tubing on a final check at each wheel.

What can go wrong

On front pads, pushing the piston back without first opening the brake fluid reservoir cap slightly can push fluid out or damage a seal. Forgetting to support the caliper can stretch or kink the brake hose. Skipping the pedal-pumping step before your first drive can mean the pedal goes to the floor instead of feeling firm.

On rear drum shoes, losing track of how the springs and adjuster were arranged is exactly why a before photo matters so much. Getting grease on the shoe linings can cause the brakes to grab or fade. Forcing the return spring on without a tool can make it snap loose unexpectedly. Not resetting the self-adjuster can leave the parking brake or pedal feeling loose. Several independent do-it-yourself writeups single out the main return spring as the one step that trips up first-timers. If you are not comfortable with springs under tension, this is a reasonable job to hand to a shop.

On bleeding, letting the reservoir run empty partway through draws in fresh air, undoing your work. Opening the bleeder screw too far can let it come out, and over-tightening it can snap it. Skipping the final firm-pedal check and driving with air still in a line is the most dangerous mistake in this whole chapter.

Quick check

  1. Are your Fit's rear brakes disc or drum? How does that change the parts and tools you need?
  2. What brake fluid type does your Fit use, and which type must you never use?
  3. How often should you replace your brake fluid, no matter what the Maintenance Minder shows?
  4. Name two safety warnings that apply to both the front pad job and the rear shoe job.
  5. How do you know your brake bleeding worked?

Chapter 28Battery: Testing, Cleaning, Replacing, Jump Starting

What you will learn

  • What size battery your 2008 Fit uses
  • How to test your battery's voltage with a multimeter, a tool that reads electrical charge
  • How to clean corrosion, the crusty buildup that forms on battery terminals
  • How to replace the battery yourself, and why you need your radio code first
  • Where to find the full jump-start steps if the battery goes dead on the road

The short version

Your Fit uses a 12-volt battery, size group 151R. You should check its condition every month. A cheap multimeter can tell you if the battery is healthy, half-charged, or failing. Corrosion on the terminals is easy to clean with baking soda and water, and replacing the whole battery takes basic tools and about 20 minutes. If the battery is already dead and you need to get moving right now, turn to Chapter 16. It covers jump starting in full.

The full explanation

Your battery, and why you check it monthly

A battery stores electrical power and delivers a big burst of it to start your engine. Your Fit's owner's manual tells you to check the battery's condition once a month. Most batteries have a small test indicator window on top. A label on the battery explains what each color in that window means. The window's exact location can vary between battery brands, so check the label on your own battery.

The battery that came with your Fit is a group size 151R. A "group size" is a standard code for a battery's physical dimensions and terminal layout, so the right one fits your battery tray and connects correctly. Some owners report swapping in a group 51R battery instead, since it offers more cold-cranking amps, a rating of how much starting power a battery can deliver in cold weather. Owners report that a 51R is a noticeably tighter physical fit, and some say they had to modify the battery tray to make it work. For a straightforward replacement with no modification, stick with the original 151R size.

A battery does one thing you should always respect: it gives off hydrogen gas, an explosive gas, during normal operation. This is true every time you work near the battery, for any reason. Keep flames, sparks, and cigarettes away from it, and always wear eye protection when you work close to it.

Testing the battery with a multimeter

A multimeter is a small handheld tool that reads electrical voltage, among other things. To test your battery, you touch its two probes to the battery's two posts and read the number on the screen. A rested battery, meaning the engine has been off at least an hour, should read about 12.6 volts or higher if it is fully charged. A reading near 12.2 volts means the battery is only about half charged, and anything below 12.0 volts usually points to a weak or failing battery. You can also have a helper crank the engine while you watch the meter; a healthy battery should not drop below about 9.6 volts while doing that. Full steps are below.

Cleaning corroded terminals

Corrosion looks like a white or yellowish, crusty powder around the battery posts and cable clamps. It forms slowly over time as the battery works, and it can weaken your electrical connection or even stop the car from starting. Baking soda mixed with water reacts with this corrosion and breaks it apart, letting you rinse it away. A thin coat of grease afterward slows corrosion from coming right back.

Replacing the battery

Before you disconnect any cable, write down your radio's 5-digit anti-theft code. Many Honda audio systems lock themselves as soon as the battery loses power, and you need that code to unlock the radio again afterward. Always disconnect the negative cable first and reconnect it last; this order lowers the risk of a spark shorting something in the car's electrical system. The new battery needs to be the same group size, 151R, so it fits the tray and hold-down bracket, the metal piece that clamps the battery in place, without modification.

Jump starting: where to find it

This chapter does not repeat the jump-start steps. Chapter 16 already covers the full procedure, from checking your battery's condition to the exact order for connecting and disconnecting cables. If your battery is dead right now, go there.

Step by step

Test the battery with a multimeter

Safety warnings, before you touch a probe to the battery: - Always wear eye protection when working near the battery. It gives off explosive hydrogen gas during normal operation. - Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery while testing it. - Do not let the multimeter's metal probes touch both terminals, or any metal, at the same time by accident. This can cause a spark. - Always wash your hands after handling the battery or its terminals.

Steps: 1. Turn off the engine and every accessory, such as lights, radio, and fan, and let the car sit at least 1 hour. 2. Set the multimeter dial to 20 volts DC. 3. Touch the red probe to the positive (+) battery post. 4. Touch the black probe to the negative (-) battery post. 5. Read the number on the multimeter's screen. This is the resting voltage. 6. Compare the reading to this chart: 12.6 volts or higher means a full, healthy charge. 7. A reading near 12.2 volts means the battery is only about half charged. 8. A reading below 12.0 volts usually means a weak or failing battery. 9. Optional: have a helper crank the engine while you watch the meter. A healthy battery should not drop below about 9.6 volts while cranking. 10. If the reading is low, or the battery struggles to crank the engine, plan to charge or replace it.

How you know it worked: you have a clear resting-voltage number and, if you did the cranking check, a cranking-voltage number, and you know which category the battery falls into.

Replace the battery

Safety warnings, before you loosen a cable clamp: - Always wear eye protection and protective clothing, or have a skilled technician do this. The battery gives off explosive hydrogen gas. - Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery. - Always disconnect the negative (-) cable first, and reconnect it last, to reduce the risk of a spark shorting the vehicle's electrical system. - Always wash your hands after handling the battery, its posts, or its terminals. They contain lead and lead compounds. - Do not drop the battery. It is heavy and can crack, spilling acid.

Steps: 1. Turn off the engine and every accessory. 2. Write down or confirm you have your radio's 5-digit anti-theft code. 3. Loosen and remove the negative (-) cable clamp first, and set it aside so it cannot touch the terminal. 4. Loosen and remove the positive (+) cable clamp next. 5. Remove the bolt or clamp holding the battery hold-down bracket in place. 6. Lift the old battery straight out, using a strap or careful grip. It is heavy. 7. Set the new battery in the tray, in the same orientation as the old one. 8. Reinstall the hold-down bracket and its bolt. 9. Reconnect the positive (+) cable clamp first this time, and tighten it. 10. Reconnect the negative (-) cable clamp last, and tighten it. 11. Start the engine to confirm it runs normally. 12. Turn on the radio and enter your 5-digit code if "ENTER CODE" appears. 13. Reset the clock if it lost its setting.

How you know it worked: the engine starts normally, the radio accepts your code, or never asked for one, and the clock is reset if needed.

Clean the battery terminals

Safety warnings, before you mix any solution: - Always wear eye protection and protective clothing, or have a skilled technician do this. The battery gives off explosive hydrogen gas. - Do not smoke or bring sparks or flames near the battery. - Always wash your hands after handling the battery or its terminals. They contain lead and lead compounds. - Always disconnect the negative (-) cable first if you need to remove a cable, and reconnect it last.

Steps: 1. Look at each terminal for a white or yellowish powder. This is corrosion. 2. Mix baking soda with a little water into a thick paste. 3. Cover the corroded terminal with the baking soda and water solution. 4. Watch it bubble up and turn brown as it reacts with the corrosion. 5. Once the bubbling stops, wash the terminal off with plain water. 6. Dry the battery and terminals with a cloth or paper towel. 7. Coat the clean terminals with a thin layer of grease to help prevent future corrosion. 8. Check that your radio still works. If it shows "ENTER CODE," enter your 5-digit code.

How you know it worked: the white or yellow powder is gone, the terminals look clean and shiny, and the cable clamps sit tight on the posts with no wobble.

What can go wrong

  • Testing right after driving, before the battery has rested. This gives a falsely high reading. Wait at least an hour with the engine off.
  • Assuming a battery that reads 12.6 volts resting is automatically fine. A battery can pass a resting-voltage test and still fail under starter load. The cranking check catches that.
  • Disconnecting the positive cable first. This raises the risk of a short if a tool accidentally touches metal. Always take the negative cable off first.
  • Forgetting the radio code before disconnecting the battery. You may have to dig through old paperwork afterward to find it.
  • Not securing the hold-down bracket. A loose battery can shift while driving.
  • Buying a non-original battery size without checking the physical fit first. A group 51R battery is a tighter squeeze than the factory 151R.
  • Skipping the grease coating after cleaning terminals. Corrosion often comes right back within months.
  • Not washing off all the baking soda paste. This leaves a residue behind on the terminal.

Quick check

  1. What group size battery does your 2008 Fit use from the factory?
  2. What voltage reading on a rested battery means a full, healthy charge?
  3. Why do you always disconnect the negative cable first and reconnect it last?
  4. What should you write down before you disconnect the battery, and why?
  5. Where in this book do you go for the full jump-start procedure?

Chapter 29Spark Plugs

What you will learn

  • What a spark plug does, and which plugs fit your 2008 Fit's engine
  • The correct gap and torque for a new spark plug
  • How to replace all four spark plugs yourself, step by step
  • Why valve clearance, a small engine gap checked at the same visits, is worth knowing about
  • What can go wrong during a plug swap, and how to avoid it

The short version

A spark plug ignites the fuel and air mixture inside your engine's cylinder. Your Fit takes an NGK IZFR6K-13 plug, or the equivalent Denso SKJ20DR-M13. The gap, the small space the spark jumps across, should be 1.2 to 1.3 millimeters (0.042 to 0.051 inches) on a new plug. Each plug tightens to about 13 pound-feet (18 newton-meters) of torque, a measure of twisting force. Replacing all four takes about 40 minutes the first time.

The full explanation

What spark plugs do, and when yours need changing

Every time a cylinder in your engine fires, a spark plug creates the spark that ignites the fuel and air inside it. Your Fit's owner's manual treats spark plug replacement as a Maintenance Minder sub item. This means the dashboard's Maintenance Minder computer decides when it is due, rather than a fixed mileage printed in the manual. No exact number is printed for your specific car. Your Fit's plugs are marketed as iridium-style plugs, plugs with a hard metal tip that lasts longer than a standard plug. Some general guidance for this kind of plug points to roughly 100,000 miles. This figure is not specific to the 2008 Fit. We could not confirm it as an exact number for your car, so check your owner's manual and dashboard display instead.

Which plugs, what gap, what torque

Your Fit's Specifications table lists two matching plug part numbers: NGK IZFR6K-13 and Denso SKJ20DR-M13. Either one is correct; they are made to the same specification.

A new plug's gap, the small air space between the plug's electrodes where the spark jumps across, should measure 1.2 to 1.3 millimeters, or 0.042 to 0.051 inches. Most new plugs ship pre-gapped correctly from the factory. Use a gap gauge to check this measurement, but do not force-adjust a pre-gapped plug unless it is clearly wrong.

Torque means how tight a bolt or plug is turned, measured in force applied at a distance. Each spark plug tightens to about 18 newton-meters, which is 1.8 kilogram-force-meters or 13 pound-feet. A torque wrench, a tool that stops or signals once you reach a set tightness, keeps you from cracking the cylinder head's soft aluminum threads by overtightening.

Valve clearance: a related check at the same visits

Valve clearance is the small gap that lets your engine's valves open and close correctly as they heat up and cool down. Your Fit's manual has the technician check this clearance at the same Maintenance Minder visits, labeled A, B, 1, 2, or 3, where other service happens. It only gets adjusted if the engine sounds noisy; there is no separate fixed mileage number for adjusting it. Check this on a cold engine, meaning the cylinder head is below 100 degrees Fahrenheit (38 degrees Celsius). The correct clearance is 0.15 to 0.19 millimeters (0.006 to 0.007 inches) on the intake valves. The correct clearance is 0.26 to 0.30 millimeters (0.010 to 0.012 inches) on the exhaust valves. We could not confirm a do-it-yourself procedure for adjusting valve clearance on this engine from this book's sources. This job is usually left to a technician, so check your owner's manual or a qualified shop if your engine is noisy.

Step by step

Replace the spark plugs

Safety warnings, before you touch a coil or a plug: - Always let the engine cool completely before starting. The cylinder head and exhaust stay hot long after the engine is off. - Do not drop anything into an open spark plug hole. A dropped bolt or tool can badly damage the engine. - Always work on one plug at a time so you never mix up which coil and boot go where. - Do not overtighten a plug. A torque wrench prevents cracking the cylinder head threads.

Steps: 1. Let the engine cool completely. 2. Open the hood and remove the plastic under-cowl or engine cover panel to reach the coils. 3. Unplug the electrical connector from the first ignition coil. 4. Remove the bolt holding that ignition coil, and lift the coil straight up and out. 5. Look inside the plug hole and clear away any loose dirt with a rag or brush. 6. Fit the spark plug socket over the old plug and turn it counterclockwise to remove it. 7. Inspect the old plug's tip for unusual color or heavy wear. 8. Check the new plug's gap against the specification on its box, but do not force-adjust a pre-gapped plug. 9. Apply a small amount of anti-seize compound, a paste that keeps metal threads from sticking together over time, to the new plug's threads. 10. Thread the new plug in by hand until it stops, to avoid cross-threading. 11. Tighten the plug the rest of the way with a torque wrench to about 13 pound-feet (18 newton-meters). 12. Reinstall that ignition coil, its bolt, and its electrical connector. 13. Repeat steps 3 through 12 for each remaining cylinder. 14. Reinstall the under-cowl or engine cover panel. 15. Start the engine and listen for smooth, even running with no misfire.

How you know it worked: the engine starts easily, idles smoothly with no shaking or misfire, and the check engine light does not come on after a short drive.

What can go wrong

  • Threading a plug in with the socket instead of starting it by hand. This risks cross-threading the soft aluminum cylinder head.
  • Skipping the torque wrench and guessing "snug." This can under-tighten or over-tighten the plug.
  • Mixing up which coil connector plugs into which coil. Work on one cylinder at a time to avoid this.
  • Dropping a bolt into the plug hole while the coil is off. Keep loose tools and hardware well clear of open plug holes.
  • Assuming your engine's plug interval matches general online guidance. Your Fit's own Maintenance Minder display is the only source that actually tracks your car's driving.

Quick check

  1. What two spark plug part numbers fit your 2008 Fit, and are they interchangeable?
  2. What gap should a new spark plug have?
  3. Why do you thread a new spark plug in by hand before using a torque wrench?
  4. How tight should each spark plug be torqued to?
  5. What is valve clearance, and when is it adjusted on your engine?

Chapter 30Coolant, Transmission Fluid, Brake Fluid, Power Steering, and Washer Fluid

What you will learn

  • What coolant type your 2008 Fit needs, and how much it holds
  • How to check the coolant level and top it up, and how to do a full drain and fill
  • What transmission fluid your car needs, and how to check and change it for your transmission type
  • What brake and clutch fluid your Fit uses, and the one fixed rule that always applies
  • Why this car has no power steering fluid to check at all
  • What washer fluid to use, and what never to put in that reservoir

The short version

Your Fit uses five separate fluids under the hood, besides engine oil: coolant, transmission fluid, brake fluid, clutch fluid on manual-transmission cars, and washer fluid. There is no power steering fluid on this car at all, because your Fit steers with an electric motor instead of hydraulic pressure. Each fluid has its own correct type; using the wrong one can cause real damage, so this chapter tells you exactly which one goes where.

The full explanation

Engine coolant

Coolant is the liquid that flows through your engine and radiator to carry heat away and keep the engine from overheating. Your Fit's owner's manual calls for Honda Long-Life Antifreeze/Coolant Type 2, which comes pre-mixed at 50 percent antifreeze and 50 percent water. Never add straight, undiluted antifreeze or plain water on its own. If Honda's coolant is not available, another major-brand coolant made for aluminum engines, and free of silicates, can be used temporarily. Flush the system and refill it with Honda coolant as soon as you reasonably can.

Replacing the coolant entirely is a Maintenance Minder sub item, so the dashboard display decides when it is due; no fixed mileage number is printed. The system's capacity depends on which transmission your car has. After a coolant change, a manual-transmission Fit holds 3.8 liters (1.00 US gallon), and an automatic-transmission Fit holds 3.7 liters (0.98 US gallon). After a full engine overhaul, the total capacity is 5.4 liters (1.43 US gallons) for a manual, or 5.3 liters (1.40 US gallons) for an automatic. The separate reserve tank, the see-through plastic tank you check day to day, holds 0.4 liters (0.11 US gallon) on its own.

Transmission fluid

Transmission fluid keeps your gearbox's internal parts lubricated and, on an automatic, also carries the hydraulic pressure that shifts gears. Replacing it is a Maintenance Minder sub item on both transmission types, so the dashboard decides normal timing. Two fixed exceptions apply only to automatic-transmission Fits. If you regularly drive at low speed in mountains, change the fluid at 60,000 miles (100,000 kilometers), then every 30,000 miles (48,000 kilometers) after that. If you tow the car behind a motorhome, change it every 2 years or 30,000 miles (48,000 kilometers), whichever comes first.

If you have a manual transmission, your Fit uses Honda Manual Transmission Fluid (MTF). If it is not available, an SAE 10W-30 or 10W-40 motor oil carrying the API Certification Seal for gasoline engines works as a temporary substitute. Switch back to Honda MTF soon, since plain motor oil lacks the additives your gearbox needs and can make shifting stiffer over time. The differential is the gearbox part that splits engine power between your front wheels. A change takes about 1.6 US quarts (1.5 liters); the total amount, including the differential, is 1.7 US quarts (1.6 liters).

If you have an automatic transmission, your Fit uses Honda Genuine ATF-Z1 only. Mixing in another brand's automatic transmission fluid (ATF) can wear down your transmission, and Honda's own warranty does not cover damage caused that way. A drain-and-refill takes about 2.5 US quarts (2.4 liters); the total system capacity, only reached during a full teardown, is about 6.3 US quarts (6.0 liters). One note for buying fluid today: Honda's ATF-Z1 has since been discontinued across the industry and replaced by Honda ATF DW-1 for service fills. Ask your parts store for the replacement if the original is not on the shelf.

Brake fluid and clutch fluid

Brake fluid transmits the force from your foot on the brake pedal to the brakes at each wheel. Your Fit's manual calls for Honda Heavy Duty Brake Fluid DOT 3, or a DOT 3 or DOT 4 fluid from a sealed container as a temporary replacement. Never use fluid marked DOT 5; it is not compatible with this car's brake system and can cause serious damage.

One rule for brake fluid always applies, no matter what the Maintenance Minder display shows: replace it every 3 years. This is one of the small set of fixed-schedule items in your whole maintenance schedule, alongside the annual oil change and the 160,000-mile idle speed check covered in Chapter 21.

If your Fit has a manual transmission, it also uses a hydraulic clutch, meaning fluid pressure, not a cable, disengages the clutch when you press the pedal. This clutch fluid shares the exact same specification as the brake fluid above. Check that its level sits between the MIN and MAX marks on the reservoir, which has a gray cap. A low level can point to a leak in the clutch hydraulic system.

We could not confirm a step-by-step, do-it-yourself procedure for replacing brake or clutch fluid from this book's sources at this pass. Check Chapter 27 for more on your braking system, and Chapter 40 for a full brake job, or have a qualified shop replace this fluid on schedule.

Power steering: this car does not have hydraulic fluid to check

Power steering is the system that reduces how much effort it takes to turn your steering wheel. Many older cars use a hydraulic pump and a reservoir of power steering fluid for this. Your 2008 Fit does not. It uses Electric Power-Assisted Rack-and-Pinion Steering (EPS) instead, on every trim, which means an electric motor assists your steering rather than pressurized fluid. Your owner's manual's Fluid Locations diagram has no power steering fluid reservoir at all. It lists only engine oil, automatic transmission fluid, brake fluid, clutch fluid on manual-transmission cars, washer fluid, and the coolant reservoir. Your dashboard has its own dedicated electric power steering indicator instead, confirming this is an electrical system, not a hydraulic one. This also means there is no power steering fluid to check, top up, or ever change on your Fit.

Washer fluid

Washer fluid sprays onto your windshield when you use the washers, so the wipers have something to clean with instead of dry glass. Your Fit's manual calls for any commercially available windshield washer fluid. Never put engine antifreeze, or a vinegar-and-water solution, into this reservoir. We could not confirm the exact reservoir capacity for the 2008 Fit from this book's sources. Check your owner's manual for that figure, and top the reservoir up when the level runs low.

Step by step

Check the coolant level and top it up

Safety warnings, before you touch the radiator cap: - Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant can spray out and scald you badly. Wait until the engine and radiator are cool. - Do not add plain water alone, or straight, undiluted antifreeze, to the reserve tank. - Do not add rust inhibitors or other additives. They may not be compatible with the coolant or engine parts.

Steps: 1. Park on level ground and let the engine and radiator cool completely. 2. Open the hood and find the coolant reserve tank, a translucent plastic tank, not the radiator itself. 3. Look at the coolant level against the MIN and MAX marks on the side of the tank. 4. If the level is at or below MIN, remove the reserve tank cap. 5. Pour Honda Long-Life Antifreeze/Coolant Type 2 in slowly, watching the level. 6. Fill until the level reaches the MAX mark. Do not overfill. 7. Put the reserve tank cap back on, and make sure it seats fully. 8. Wipe up any spilled coolant right away. It can damage engine bay parts. 9. Look around the reserve tank and radiator hoses for any obvious wet spots or drips that would explain a low level.

How you know it worked: the coolant level sits at or near the MAX mark on the reserve tank, the cap is seated, and you find no active leak underneath.

Drain and fill the coolant

Safety warnings, before you raise the car or open the radiator: - Do not remove the radiator cap while the engine is hot. Hot coolant can spray out and scald you. Wait until the engine and radiator are cool. - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps rated for the weight. - Always relieve pressure slowly. Turn the radiator cap counterclockwise to the first stop and let it hiss before pressing down and removing it. - Do not add plain water alone as a permanent fix, and do not mix Honda's concentrated coolant with plain water beyond what the label describes. - Always clean up spilled coolant immediately. It is toxic to pets and wildlife if left in a puddle.

Steps: 1. Let the engine and radiator cool completely. 2. Raise the front of the car and support it on jack stands, then remove the splash shield if one is fitted. 3. Relieve pressure at the radiator cap slowly, then remove the cap fully. 4. Place a drain pan under the radiator's drain plug, and loosen it to drain the radiator. 5. Place the drain pan under the drain bolt at the rear of the engine block, and remove it to drain the block. 6. Let all the coolant drain fully from both points. 7. Reinstall the block drain bolt with a new washer, and tighten it securely. 8. Tighten the radiator drain plug securely. 9. Remove, empty, and rinse the coolant reserve tank, then reinstall it. 10. Pour fresh Honda coolant into the radiator, up to the base of the filler neck. 11. Fill the reserve tank to the MAX mark. 12. Install the radiator cap loosely, not fully tight yet. 13. Start the engine and let it run until the radiator fan cycles on and off at least twice, to work air out of the system. 14. Turn off the engine, let it cool, and recheck the radiator and reserve tank levels. 15. Top off as needed, then put the radiator cap on fully and tightly. 16. Start the engine again and check underneath for leaks at both drain points. 17. Lower the car and remove the jack stands.

How you know it worked: the reserve tank sits at the MAX mark after a full warm-up-and-cool-down cycle, and there are no leaks at either drain point. The temperature gauge or warning light behaves normally on a test drive.

Check and change the manual transmission fluid

Safety warnings, before you raise the car: - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps. - Always check and change the fluid with the transmission at normal operating temperature, and the car on level ground. - Do not overfill. Fluid should only reach the edge of the bolt hole, not spill out under pressure. - Always clean up any spilled fluid immediately.

Steps (level check only): 1. Drive the car until the transmission reaches normal operating temperature. 2. Park on level ground and shut off the engine. 3. Remove the transmission filler bolt. 4. Carefully feel inside the bolt hole with a clean finger for the fluid level. 5. If fluid does not reach the edge of the hole, add Honda MTF until it starts to run back out. 6. Put a new washer on the filler bolt, and reinstall it, tightened securely.

Steps (full drain and refill, continuing from a cool transmission): 7. Raise the car and support it on jack stands. 8. Place the drain pan under the transmission's drain bolt. 9. Remove the drain bolt and let the old fluid fully drain. 10. Reinstall the drain bolt with a new washer, tightened securely. 11. Lower the car back to level ground. 12. Remove the filler bolt and add fresh Honda MTF through a pump or long funnel, until it reaches the edge of the hole. 13. Reinstall the filler bolt with a new washer, tightened securely. 14. Check for leaks at both bolts after a short drive.

How you know it worked: fluid sits right at the edge of the filler bolt hole, neither dripping out under pressure nor clearly low. There are no leaks at either bolt after driving, and shifting feels normal, not stiff or grindy.

Check and change the automatic transmission fluid

Safety warnings, before you raise the car: - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands or ramps. - Always check and fill the level with the engine at normal operating temperature, and the car on level ground. - Do not fill above the dipstick's upper mark. Overfilling can damage the transmission. - Always clean up any spilled fluid immediately.

Steps (level check only): 1. Park on level ground and start the engine, letting it run until the radiator fan comes on, then shut it off. 2. Wait about 60 seconds, but no longer than 90 seconds, before checking. 3. Remove the dipstick, the yellow-looped fluid gauge, and wipe it clean with a rag. 4. Insert the dipstick fully back into the transmission. 5. Remove it again, and read the fluid level against the upper and lower marks. 6. If the level is below the lower mark, add Honda ATF-Z1 through the dipstick tube until it is between the marks. 7. Reinsert the dipstick fully and securely.

Steps (full drain and refill, continuing after the level check): 8. Raise the car and support it on jack stands. 9. Place the drain pan under the transmission's drain plug. 10. Remove the drain plug and let the old fluid fully drain. 11. Reinstall the drain plug with a new washer, tightened securely. 12. Lower the car back to level ground. 13. Add fresh Honda ATF-Z1 through the dipstick tube using a pump or long funnel. 14. Recheck the level using steps 1 through 5 above, topping off as needed. 15. Check for leaks at the drain plug after a short drive.

How you know it worked: the dipstick reads between the upper and lower marks after the correct warm-up-and-wait routine, and there are no leaks at the drain plug. The car shifts smoothly, with no delay or harsh engagement.

What can go wrong

  • Topping off coolant with plain water instead of the correct pre-mixed type. This dilutes the mixture and can reduce its protection against freezing or overheating.
  • Opening a hot radiator cap and getting scalded by escaping steam and coolant. Always let the system cool first.
  • Not letting the engine run through a full warm-up cycle after a coolant drain and fill. This can leave a trapped air pocket that causes overheating later.
  • Using plain motor oil in a manual transmission long-term instead of switching back to Honda MTF. Motor oil lacks the additives your gearbox needs, and shifting can get stiffer over time.
  • Using any automatic transmission fluid other than Honda ATF-Z1, or its replacement, ATF DW-1. Mixing fluids can wear down your transmission, and the damage may not be covered under warranty.
  • Checking automatic transmission fluid cold, or without waiting the 60- to 90-second window after shutdown. This gives an inaccurate reading.
  • Assuming your Fit has power steering fluid to check because an older car you know did. This car steers electrically. There is no fluid at all.
  • Skipping the 3-year brake fluid change because the dashboard display never mentioned it. This is a fixed rule, separate from the Maintenance Minder.
  • Pouring antifreeze or a vinegar solution into the washer fluid reservoir. Use only commercial washer fluid.

Quick check

  1. What coolant type does your Fit require, and what should you never add to the system on its own?
  2. Name the two fixed-mileage exceptions for automatic transmission fluid, and who they apply to.
  3. What fluid does a manual-transmission Fit's clutch use, and where do you check its level?
  4. Does your 2008 Fit have hydraulic power steering fluid? Why or why not?
  5. What fixed rule applies to brake fluid no matter what the Maintenance Minder display says?

Chapter 31Belts, Hoses, and the Timing Chain

What you will learn

  • Why your 2008 Fit has a timing chain instead of a timing belt, and what that means for you
  • What the drive belt is, and how your car's maintenance schedule handles it
  • Why this book cannot give you a hose-replacement schedule for this chapter
  • Why there is no do-it-yourself procedure in this chapter, and where to take the car instead

The short version

Your Fit's engine uses a timing chain, not a timing belt. A timing chain is a metal chain, not rubber, and Honda treats it as a lifetime part with no scheduled replacement. Your car also has one drive belt, which the Maintenance Minder tracks for inspection and adjustment on its own schedule. This chapter has no do-it-yourself procedure, because none of this book's sources include one for these parts; a technician checks them for you.

The full explanation

Timing chain, not timing belt

A timing belt is a rubber belt. It keeps the engine's valves and pistons moving together. Many cars need this belt replaced on a strict mileage schedule. A skipped or broken timing belt can be a serious, sudden problem. Your Fit's engine does not have one of these belts at all. It uses a timing chain instead, a metal chain that does the same job. Your Fit's own maintenance item table lists two scheduled jobs: spark plug replacement and valve clearance inspection. It has no line at all for replacing a timing belt or timing chain. Honda treats the timing chain as a lifetime part under normal use. It has no scheduled replacement interval. It only needs service if a specific problem comes up. That problem could be unusual noise. It could be chain stretch, meaning the chain has worn and lengthened slightly. Or it could be the engine's timing actually skipping.

This is good news for your maintenance budget. Unlike a timing belt on many other cars, there is no expensive, scheduled chain replacement waiting for you at some fixed mileage. It is also a reason to pay attention if your engine ever sounds different or runs roughly. A timing chain problem is not something your maintenance schedule will warn you about ahead of time.

The drive belt

Your Fit has one drive belt. It spins accessories such as the alternator, the part that recharges your battery while the engine runs. Inspecting and adjusting this belt is a Maintenance Minder sub item. The dashboard's own maintenance computer decides when it is due. There is no fixed mileage printed in your manual for this job. This book's sources do not include a do-it-yourself procedure card for inspecting or adjusting this belt. A technician should check it during your regular Maintenance Minder service visits.

Hoses: what this chapter can honestly tell you

We could not confirm a specific inspection interval for coolant hoses from this book's sources at this pass. Your Fit's Maintenance Minder Item B inspection list does name some hoses. It lists brake hoses and lines, plus fuel lines and connections, as things a technician checks. It does not separately name engine coolant hoses in that list. Check your owner's manual for that item. Or ask your technician to look over your coolant hoses at your next service visit. This book does not have a confirmed, Fit-specific answer for that particular item.

Step by step

This chapter has no do-it-yourself procedure. Your Fit's engine does not have a timing belt to replace, so this book's research did not turn up a timing-belt-style replacement job. It also did not turn up a home procedure card for inspecting or adjusting the drive belt, or for checking coolant hoses. A technician handles all three of these jobs. This usually happens at your regular Maintenance Minder service visits, described in Chapter 21. If your engine ever sounds unusual, or you notice a belt squeal, have it looked at promptly. Do not guess at a fix yourself.

What can go wrong

  • Assuming your Fit needs a timing belt change at some mileage, the way many other cars do. Your engine has a timing chain, not a belt, and Honda gives it no scheduled replacement interval.
  • Ignoring an unusual engine noise because "the timing chain doesn't need service." A timing chain is only serviced when a problem shows up, such as noise, stretch, or a timing skip. Unusual noise is exactly the sign to have it checked.
  • Never having the drive belt inspected because the dashboard has not flagged it. The Maintenance Minder decides the timing, but it still relies on your regular service visits to actually happen.
  • Assuming this chapter covers a hose-replacement schedule that this book could not confirm. Check your owner's manual, or ask your technician, since this chapter is honest about that gap rather than guessing at a number.

Quick check

  1. Does your 2008 Fit have a timing belt or a timing chain? What is the difference between the two?
  2. Does your Fit's timing chain have a fixed replacement mileage? If not, when does it get serviced?
  3. What does the drive belt do, and how does your Fit's maintenance schedule decide when to inspect it?
  4. Why does this chapter not include a step-by-step procedure?

Chapter 32Lights and Bulbs, Wipers, Fuses

What you will learn

  • Every bulb size and type in your 2008 Fit, from headlights to the dome light
  • How to replace a headlight bulb, and tail, brake, or turn signal bulbs
  • What size wiper blades your Fit uses, and how to replace them
  • How to find and replace a blown fuse, and why you should never use the wrong amperage
  • What can go wrong during each of these jobs

The short version

Your Fit uses a mix of halogen bulbs, meaning bulbs with a heated filament inside a gas-filled glass bulb. It also uses small wedge-style bulbs for everything from turn signals to the dome light. Replacing most exterior bulbs takes about 15 minutes and basic tools. Wiper blades should be checked every 6 months and replaced at the first sign of trouble. A blown fuse, a small safety device that breaks an electrical circuit, is one of the easiest and safest repairs in your whole car.

The full explanation

Every light on your Fit, and its bulb

Your Fit's owner's manual and its Specifications table describe the bulb for nearly every light on the car. The table below lists them.

Light Bulb type Fact ID
Headlight, high and low beam HB2 (also cross-sold as 9003 or H4), halogen, 12 volt, 60/55 watt F-WP09-048
Fog light (Sport model or accessory) H11, halogen F-WP09-049
Front turn signal Amber, 12 volt, 21 watt F-WP09-050
Rear turn signal 7443 F-WP09-051
Stop/taillight 7443 F-WP09-052
Back-up light 7443 F-WP09-053
License plate light T10 F-WP09-054
High-mount brake light UNVERIFIED trade number F-WP09-055
Front side marker/parking light T10 F-WP09-056
Rear side marker T10 F-WP09-057
Dome/ceiling light C5W, 31 millimeter festoon, a tube-shaped bulb with metal caps on each end F-WP09-058
Cargo/luggage compartment light T10 F-WP09-059

Your headlight bulb carries the trade name HB2 in Honda's own Specifications table. Parts stores commonly sell this same physical bulb under the name H4 or 9003 as well; confirm the cross-reference against your own bulb's markings before you buy a replacement. If your Fit has fog lights, either from the Sport trim or added as an accessory, they use an H11 halogen bulb.

Your front turn signals use an amber, 21-watt bulb. Several of your rear lights share the same 7443 trade number: the turn signal, the stop/taillight, and the back-up light. Each one still plugs into its own socket in the assembly. Your license plate light, front and rear side marker lights, and cargo compartment light all use a small T10 wedge bulb. We could not confirm the exact trade number for your high-mount brake light bulb from this book's sources. The manual confirms it is a separate, owner-replaceable bulb, but does not print its part number. Check your owner's manual or a parts store for that specific number. Your dome light uses a festoon-style bulb, a small tube-shaped bulb with a metal cap on each end, listed as C5W in a 31-millimeter length.

Wiper blades

Check your wiper blades' condition at least every 6 months, per your owner's manual, and replace any blade at the first sign of cracking, hardening, or streaking. Your front wiper blades are two different sizes: 24 inches on the driver's side, and 16 inches on the passenger's side. Your rear wiper blade, on the tailgate, is 14 inches.

Fuses

A fuse is a small safety device that breaks an electrical circuit on purpose if too much current tries to flow through it. This protects the wiring behind it from damage or fire.
