Your Fit stores some spare fuses. Amperage rating, meaning the current level a fuse position is designed to carry, is printed on the fuse box's own lid diagram and in your owner's manual. Never substitute a fuse with a higher amperage rating than the one that was there originally. This raises the risk of damaging your electrical system.

Step by step

Replace a headlight bulb

Safety warnings, before you touch a bulb: - Do not touch the glass part of a halogen bulb with bare skin. Oil from your fingers can make it overheat and shatter when lit. Handle it by its base only. - Always clean the glass with denatured alcohol and a clean cloth before installing it, if you accidentally touch it. - Always let the engine cool if it was recently running, since you will reach in near the engine bay.

Steps: 1. To reach the driver's side bulb, start the engine, turn the wheel fully right, then turn the engine off. 2. To reach the passenger's side bulb, instead turn the wheel fully left. 3. Use a flat-tip screwdriver to remove the holding clip from the inner fender. 4. Pull the inner fender cover back to expose the back of the headlight. 5. Remove the electrical connector from the bulb by pulling it straight back. 6. Remove the rubber weather seal by pulling on its tab. 7. Unclip the end of the hold-down wire from its slot. 8. Pivot the hold-down wire out of the way and remove the old bulb. 9. Install the new bulb, holding it by its base, making sure its tabs sit in their slots. 10. Pivot the hold-down wire back into place and clip its end into the slot. 11. Check the wire's installation through the inspection window on the headlight assembly. 12. Install the rubber weather seal back over the back of the headlight assembly. 13. Push the electrical connector onto the new bulb until it is securely connected. 14. Reinstall the inner fender cover and the holding clip, pressing the clip's center to lock it. 15. Turn on the headlights to confirm the new bulb works.

How you know it worked: the headlight comes on and looks similarly bright and evenly aimed as the other side. The inner fender cover and clip are back in place.

Replace tail light and brake light bulbs

Safety warnings, before you pry the cover: - Do not force the trim tool or screwdriver against painted body panels without a cloth underneath. You can scratch the paint. - Always confirm which of the bulbs in the assembly is actually burned out before you disturb the others.

Steps for the rear light assembly, on the tailgate side: 1. Open the tailgate. 2. Place a cloth on the edge of the light assembly cover to protect the paint. 3. Pry the cover off carefully at the notch on its middle edge with a flat-tip screwdriver. 4. Identify which bulb is out: stop/taillight, back-up light, turn signal, or taillight. 5. Turn that bulb's socket one-quarter turn counterclockwise and remove it from the assembly. 6. Pull the burned-out bulb straight out of its socket. 7. Push the new bulb straight into the socket until it bottoms out. 8. Turn on the lights, or press the brake pedal for a stop/taillight bulb, to confirm it works. 9. Turn the socket clockwise back into the light assembly until it locks. 10. Reinstall the light assembly cover.

Steps for the high-mount brake light, on the rear window: 11. Open the tailgate. 12. Remove the light assembly cover by pushing in the tabs on both sides and pulling it off. 13. Turn the socket one-quarter turn counterclockwise and remove it from the assembly. 14. Pull the old bulb straight out of its socket. 15. Push the new bulb straight into the socket until it bottoms out. 16. Put the socket back into the light assembly and turn it clockwise to lock it. 17. Press the brake pedal to confirm the new bulb lights up. 18. Push the cover back onto the light assembly until it locks in place.

How you know it worked: the specific light you replaced turns on when tested, and both covers are fully seated and locked.

Replace the wiper blades

Safety warnings, before you raise a wiper arm: - Do not open the hood while the wiper arms are raised off the glass. The hood can hit and damage the arms. - Always handle a raised wiper arm carefully. Do not let it snap down onto bare glass without a blade attached.

Steps for the front wiper blades: 1. Raise the wiper arms off the windshield, lifting the driver's side first, then the passenger's side. 2. Press and hold the lock tab on the blade assembly. 3. Slide the blade assembly toward the lock tab until it releases from the wiper arm. 4. Grasp the tabbed end of the old blade and pull firmly until it comes out of its holder. 5. If the new blade has no plastic or metal reinforcement strip, move the strip from the old blade into the new one's edge slots. 6. Make sure the new blade's rubber tabs fit into each notch of the reinforcement strip. 7. Slide the new blade into the wiper arm until it is engaged along its full length. 8. Lower the wiper arm back down onto the windshield. 9. Repeat steps 2 through 8 for the other front wiper arm.

Steps for the rear wiper blade, if equipped: 10. Raise the rear wiper arm off the rear glass. 11. Slide the old blade out of the wiper arm. 12. Move the reinforcement strip to the new blade if it does not already have one. 13. Slide the new blade assembly onto the wiper arm until it locks in place. 14. Lower the rear wiper arm back down against the glass.

How you know it worked: all wiper arms sit flat against the glass with the new blades locked in place. A test run of the wipers wipes cleanly, with no streaks, chatter, or missed spots.

Replace a fuse

Safety warnings, before you open a fuse box: - Always turn the ignition switch to LOCK (0) and turn off the headlights and every accessory before checking or changing fuses. - Do not install a fuse with a higher rating than the circuit calls for. This greatly increases the risk of damaging the electrical system. - Do not ignore a fuse that blows again right away with a same-rated replacement. That means there is a real electrical problem, and the vehicle should be checked by a qualified technician.

Steps: 1. Turn the ignition switch to the LOCK (0) position. 2. Make sure the headlights and all other accessories are off. 3. Figure out which box holds the fuse: primary under-hood, on the driver's side, interior, behind the coin tray, or secondary, on the battery. 4. Remove the cover from that fuse box. 5. Find the suspect fuse using the chart on the fuse box lid or in the manual. 6. Pull a small fuse straight out using the fuse puller from the interior box. 7. For a large fuse in an under-hood box, remove it with a Phillips screwdriver instead. 8. Look through the top of the fuse at the thin wire inside. A blown fuse shows a broken or melted wire. 9. If you are not sure which one is blown, check every fuse for that circuit, not only the first one. 10. Push in a new fuse of the exact same amperage rating. 11. Turn the ignition back on and test whether the device now works. 12. Reinstall the fuse box cover.

How you know it worked: the device that stopped working, such as a light, wiper, or power window, now works normally. The new fuse does not blow again right away.

What can go wrong

  • Buying a bulb without checking its cross-reference against your old bulb's markings. A headlight sold as "H4" is often the same bulb as an HB2, but confirm this before you install it.
  • Touching a new halogen bulb's glass with bare fingers. Oil from your skin can make it overheat and shatter when lit.
  • Guessing which rear bulb is out instead of testing each function first. Test the brakes, turn signal, and reverse gear before you open any cover.
  • Prying a light assembly cover without a cloth underneath. This can scratch the paint around it.
  • Forgetting to transfer the reinforcement strip into a wiper blade that ships without one. The new blade will not sit correctly on the glass without it.
  • Letting a raised, unblocked wiper arm snap down onto bare glass. Always support or lower an arm carefully.
  • Installing a higher-rated fuse to make it stop blowing. This risks damaging the wiring or causing a fire.
  • Checking only one fuse and missing that a different one in the same circuit is the actual problem. If your device still does not work, check every fuse on that circuit.
  • Forgetting that removing the radio fuse can lock the radio. You may need your 5-digit code to unlock it again.

Quick check

  1. What is the trade name for your Fit's headlight bulb, and what other name is it commonly sold under?
  2. Name three of your Fit's rear lights that share the same 7443 bulb.
  3. How often should you check your wiper blades, and what sizes does your Fit use, front and rear?
  4. Why should you never install a fuse with a higher amperage rating than the original?
  5. What should you do if a fuse blows again right away after you replace it?

Chapter 33Keeping It Clean Inside and Out

What you will learn

  • How to wash and wax your 2008 Fit's exterior without damaging the paint or wheels
  • Why you should remove the rear antenna before a drive-through car wash
  • How to clean your seat belts safely, and why they need to fully air-dry
  • How to clean the interior and keep your floor mats from becoming a safety problem

The short version

Washing and waxing your Fit, plus a full interior clean, takes about 90 minutes the first time. Before a drive-through car wash, unscrew and remove the rear roof antenna by hand; the wash's spinning brushes can damage it. Never clean your seat belts with bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents, and always let them fully air-dry before you use the car again. Always re-anchor your floor mats into their factory anchor points once they are dry, since a loose mat can interfere with the pedals.

The full explanation

Washing and waxing the exterior

A clean, well-waxed exterior protects your Fit's paint from the sun, road salt, and everyday dirt. Wax, or a sealant, is a protective coating you apply on top of clean paint; it helps water bead up and roll off instead of sitting on the surface. Your Fit's manual specifically warns against using a drive-through car wash without first removing the rear roof antenna by hand, since the wash's spinning brushes can damage it. Reinstall the antenna once you are done.

Your Fit's wheels are aluminum alloy, meaning a lightweight metal mix, and they have a protective clear-coat finish. Avoid harsh wheel cleaners or a stiff brush on them; these can damage that clear-coat. Use a car-wash soap made for vehicles, not dish soap, since dish soap can strip away wax.

Cleaning seat belts and the interior

Your seat belts should never be cleaned with bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents. If they get dirty, clean them with a soft brush and mild soap and water instead, and let them air-dry fully before you use the car again. A damp seat belt that gets rolled back up into its retractor can stay damp for a long time, so give it time to dry completely first.

When you clean the rest of the interior, vacuum the seats, carpet, and floor. Wipe hard surfaces, like the dashboard and door panels, with a mild cleaner and a soft cloth. Take out your floor mats and clean them separately. Once everything is completely dry, put the mats back and re-anchor them into their factory anchor points. A loose mat can slide forward and interfere with the pedals, or with a seat's weight sensor, which is a real safety issue, not only a tidiness one.

Step by step

Wash and wax the exterior, and clean the interior

Safety warnings, before you start: - Do not use a drive-through car wash without first unscrewing and removing the rear roof antenna by hand. The brushes can damage it. - Do not use bleach, dye, or cleaning solvents on the seat belts. - Always let seat belts air-dry fully before using the vehicle again. - Do not clean aluminum alloy wheels with harsh chemicals or a stiff brush. It can damage their protective clear-coat.

Steps for the exterior wash and wax: 1. If you plan to use a drive-through wash, unscrew and remove the rear antenna by hand first. 2. Rinse the whole car with plain water to knock off loose dirt. 3. Wash a small section at a time with car-wash soap and a wash mitt, then rinse that section. 4. Clean the wheels separately with a mild detergent and a soft brush or sponge, avoiding harsh wheel cleaners on alloy wheels. 5. Dry the car with a clean microfiber towel before water spots form. 6. Apply wax or sealant in small sections, following the product's instructions. 7. Buff each section with a clean, dry microfiber towel. 8. Reinstall the antenna if you removed it.

Steps for the interior: 9. Remove the floor mats and shake or vacuum them separately. 10. Vacuum the seats, carpet, and floor, using an attachment to reach corners. 11. Wipe the dashboard, door panels, and other hard surfaces with a mild cleaner and a soft cloth. 12. Wipe the insides of the seat belt loops with a cloth dampened in mild soap water or isopropyl alcohol. 13. If the seat belts themselves are dirty, clean them with a soft brush and mild soap and water, then let them air-dry fully. 14. Make sure carpeting and floor mats are completely dry before you put the floor mats back. 15. Re-anchor the floor mats onto their factory anchor points. A loose mat can interfere with the pedals or a seat sensor.

How you know it worked: the exterior is clean with an even, beaded look where waxed, and the wheels are clean without dulled or damaged clear-coat. The interior looks and smells clean, and every floor mat is dry and re-anchored in place.

What can go wrong

  • Washing the car in direct hot sun. This makes soap and water dry into spots before you can rinse it off. Work in shade if you can.
  • Using a stiff brush or strong wheel cleaner on the aluminum alloy wheels. This can damage their protective clear-coat finish.
  • Skipping the antenna removal before a drive-through wash. The spinning brushes can bend or break it.
  • Cleaning seat belts with bleach or a solvent-based cleaner. This can weaken the belt webbing itself.
  • Putting damp floor mats back down, or not re-anchoring them. This can interfere with the pedals or a seat's weight sensor, which is a genuine safety concern.
  • Using dish soap instead of car-wash soap. Dish soap can strip away any wax already on the paint.

Quick check

  1. What should you do to the antenna before a drive-through car wash, and why?
  2. Name two things you should never use to clean your seat belts.
  3. Why do your wheels need a gentler cleaner and softer brush than the rest of the car?
  4. Why is re-anchoring your floor mats a safety issue, not only a tidiness one?

Part VIDiagnosing Problems

Chapter 34How to Describe a Problem

What you will learn

  • Why a clear description helps you find the real cause of a car problem
  • How to describe when a noise, vibration, smell, or leak happens
  • How to describe where you feel or notice it
  • How your speed and engine speed give useful clues
  • How color and smell narrow down a leak or an odor
  • The difference between a "Fit-specific" finding and general automotive knowledge, a difference you will see again in Chapter 37

The short version

A good description has four parts: when it happens, where you notice it, how fast you were going, and what it looks or smells like. Chapters 35 through 37 sort information this same way, so a clear description lets you use those chapters fast. Write your notes down before you call a shop or look up a code.

The full explanation

Why this matters

"My car is making a weird noise" tells a mechanic almost nothing. It also will not help you use the tables in Chapter 37 or the flowchart in Chapter 36. The more specific you are, the faster anyone, including you, can narrow down the cause.

This chapter teaches you to notice four things about any problem: when it happens, where you notice it, your speed, and what it looks or smells like. These are the same four categories this book's research used to build the lookup tables in Chapter 37.

When does it happen?

Ask yourself when the noise, shake, or smell shows up. Common answers include: at idle, meaning while the engine runs but the car is not moving; while accelerating; while turning; while braking; or over bumps.

For example, a ticking sound at idle often points to a low oil level. It can also point to a problem in the VTEC system, the mechanism that switches the intake valves between a low-lift and a high-lift setting using engine oil pressure. A clunk that happens only when you shift into Drive or Reverse points to something different. It often means a worn engine or transmission mount, the rubber-and-metal part that holds the engine or transmission to the car's frame. Two very different sounds, both "at idle." But the timing detail, a constant tick versus a one-time clunk on shifting, points to two different systems.

Where do you notice it?

Location matters too. Do you feel a vibration through the steering wheel, or through the seat and floor? Do you hear a noise from the front of the car, the middle, or the back? Owners report that a clicking noise while turning often points to a worn CV joint. A CV joint, short for constant velocity joint, is a flexible joint in the axle that lets power reach the wheel while it turns. That same clicking sound, if it does not change while you turn the wheel, more likely points to a loose exhaust heat shield instead. A heat shield is a metal panel that protects nearby parts from a hot exhaust pipe.

How fast were you going?

Speed is one of the most useful clues for a vibration. A shimmy in the steering wheel that appears below about 20 mph, then goes away above that speed, often points to a tire that is out of round. It can also point to a bent wheel, or a stuck brake part dragging. A stronger shake that grows worse between roughly 45 and 65 mph often points to a wheel or tire that is out of balance. Out of balance means its weight is not spread evenly around the wheel. Writing down the exact speed range helps you or a mechanic skip straight to the likely cause.

Engine speed matters too, separately from road speed. A whine or grind from the front of the engine that gets louder as the engine revs, not as the car speeds up, points toward the alternator. The alternator is the part that charges the battery while the engine runs, and this noise can also come from the belt that spins it.

What does it look or smell like?

For a leak, note the color and exactly where under the car you see it. Amber or dark brown usually means engine oil. Red or pink usually means automatic transmission fluid on this car. The 2008 Fit uses electric power steering, not a hydraulic system, so there is no separate power-steering fluid to confuse it with. Green, yellow-green, orange, pink, or blue, with a sweet smell, points to coolant, the fluid that keeps the engine's temperature down.

For a smell, describe it in plain words: burning rubber, burning plastic, sweet and syrupy, rotten egg, raw gasoline, or hot oil. Each points toward a different system, which Chapter 37 explains in full.

Understanding urgency

Chapter 37's tables each give an urgency level: Low, Medium, Medium-high, or High. Low means the problem is usually only annoying, not dangerous. High means you should stop driving soon, sometimes right away, because the problem could hurt you, hurt the car badly, or both. When you describe a problem to yourself or a shop, always mention any High-urgency sign. Examples include a burning smell, a grinding noise while braking, or a flashing warning light.

"Fit-specific" versus general knowledge

Some of what you will read in Chapters 35 through 37 comes from real 2007-2008 Honda Fit owners describing their own cars. This book marks those as "Owners report that..." Other information is true of almost any gasoline car and does not need a Fit-specific source, since it is standard automotive knowledge. Chapter 37 marks each row so you can tell which kind of information you are reading. Neither kind is more or less true. They are two different types of evidence.

Step by step

Use this short routine any time something feels or sounds wrong with your car.

  1. Stop and think before you drive further, especially if the problem feels sudden or serious.
  2. Note when it happens: at idle, accelerating, turning, braking, or over bumps.
  3. Note where you notice it: steering wheel, seat and floor, front, middle, or back of the car.
  4. Note your speed, or your engine speed, when it happens or gets worse.
  5. Note the color of any fluid, and exactly where under the car it appears.
  6. Note the smell, using plain words like burning, sweet, or rotten egg.
  7. Write all of this down, or say it out loud to yourself, before you look anything up.
  8. Use your notes with Chapter 35 (check engine light and codes), Chapter 36 (won't start), or Chapter 37 (lookup tables) to narrow down the cause.

What can go wrong

  • Being too vague. "It sounds funny" describes nothing. "It ticks at idle, only when the engine is cold" describes something you can look up.
  • Skipping the "when." A noise that only happens once, right after starting, is very different from a noise that happens constantly. Both matter, but they point to different causes.
  • Guessing the cause instead of describing the symptom. Say what you heard, felt, saw, or smelled first. Save your guess about the cause for after you check the tables.
  • Ignoring a High-urgency sign because the car "still drives fine." A car can still move while something inside it is failing badly. Treat a burning smell or a flashing light seriously right away.
  • Forgetting to check both speed and engine speed for a vibration or a whine. Road speed and engine speed are not the same thing, and they point to different systems.

Quick check

  1. What four things should you note about any car problem?
  2. Why does the exact speed range matter for a vibration?
  3. What is a CV joint, and what noise, at what time, points to a worn one?
  4. What is the difference between a "Fit-specific" finding and general automotive knowledge?
  5. Name one High-urgency sign you should never ignore, even if the car still seems to drive fine.

Chapter 35The Check Engine Light and OBD-II Codes

What you will learn

  • What OBD-II means and what the check engine light is actually telling you
  • Where to find the OBD-II port in your 2008 Fit
  • How reading and clearing a code works, in general terms
  • All 20 trouble codes this book found linked to this exact car, in plain English, with the first thing to check for each
  • Why 2 of those 20 codes are less certain than the other 18, and why this book says so honestly

The short version

The check engine light, formally called the Malfunction Indicator Lamp (MIL), warns you that a system tied to emissions or engine control has a problem. OBD-II, short for On-Board Diagnostics, second generation, is the standard system every 1996-and-newer US gasoline car uses to store a trouble code describing that problem. A code reader plugged into the port under your dashboard shows you the code, and this chapter's table shows you what 20 common codes mean and where to start checking.

The full explanation

What OBD-II is, and what the check engine light means

OBD-II is a federal standard. Every gasoline car sold in the US since 1996 must have it, including your 2008 Fit. The system constantly watches the engine and emissions parts. When something goes outside its normal range, the car's computer, called the ECM (engine control module), saves a code describing the problem and turns on the check engine light.

Each stored code is called a DTC, short for Diagnostic Trouble Code. A DTC does not always mean a part is broken. It means the ECM saw a reading it does not expect, which could point to a broken part, a loose connector, a low fluid level, or something else entirely. That is why this chapter's table always lists a first thing to check, not only a part to replace.

Where the OBD-II port is

The OBD-II port, also called the data link connector (DLC), is a 16-pin plug you connect a code reader to. On your 2008 Fit, it sits under the dashboard on the driver's side, at or a little above the brake and gas pedals, close to the steering column. It usually has no label, and it may sit tucked up against the underside of the dash instead of hanging down in plain view. You will likely need to crouch down and feel for it with your hand, or put your head down near the pedals to see it.

This location is not random. A federal standard called SAE J1962 sets the rules. The port must sit between the driver's end of the dashboard and a point slightly past the center of the car. It must mount to the dashboard structure and be reachable from the driver's seat without tools. That rule is why "under the dash, driver's side, near the steering column" is the right first place to look on almost any 1996-or-newer US car. A source naming the Fit by name confirms this location specifically for your car.

Reading and clearing a code, in general terms

A code reader, sometimes called a scan tool, plugs into the OBD-II port. With the ignition turned on, it shows you any stored DTCs as a letter-and-number code, such as P0300. You then look up what that code means, which this chapter's table does for the 20 most common codes on this car.

Clearing a code turns off the check engine light, but it does not fix the problem that caused the code. If the underlying cause is still there, the code, and the light, will likely come back. This book could not confirm the exact menu steps for every code reader model, since readers vary by brand. Follow the instructions that come with your own reader.

Should you keep driving with the light on?

This depends on how the light behaves. A steady check engine light means a problem exists, but usually is not an emergency by itself. A flashing check engine light is more serious. It means an active, severe misfire, a condition where a cylinder is not firing correctly, which can send unburned fuel into the hot catalytic converter and destroy it. If your check engine light is flashing, pull over and stop driving. Do not continue on to your destination first.

The 20 codes this book found for the 2008 Fit

Every code below was checked against real reports from owners of 2007-2008 Honda Fits, not a generic list of what any car's computer can report. Most are backed by two independent owner reports describing this exact code on this exact generation of car. Two codes have only one such report each, and this book flags them honestly rather than presenting them as more certain than they are.

Key to the Source column: - TSB — Backed by an actual Honda Technical Service Bulletin, the strongest evidence in this list. - 2+ — Backed by two or more independent 2007-2008 Fit owner reports. - 1 only — Backed by only one 2007-2008 Fit owner report found. Treat this as a lead, not a confirmed pattern.

Code What it means First thing to check Source
P0300 Random or multiple cylinder misfire. More than one cylinder is skipping a spark. Spark plugs first, then the ignition coil packs. Owners report that more than one coil pack can fail around the same time on this car. 2+
P0301 Cylinder 1 misfire. Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 1. If that does not fix it, check the upstream oxygen sensor next. 2+
P0302 Cylinder 2 misfire. Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 2. Owners report this code often appears alongside P0300. 2+
P0303 Cylinder 3 misfire. Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 3. If plugs and coils don't fix it, worn fuel injectors are a real possibility on this engine. 2+
P0304 Cylinder 4 misfire. Spark plug and ignition coil for cylinder 4. 2+
P0135 The heater inside the upstream oxygen sensor, the sensor that checks exhaust before the catalytic converter, is not drawing the current expected. Check the 15-amp fuse for this sensor's heater circuit first. If the fuse is fine, check the sensor's wiring before replacing the sensor. 2+
P0141 Same heater problem as P0135, but on the downstream oxygen sensor, located after the catalytic converter. Check the downstream sensor's connector and wiring first. This exact code and repair has been directly confirmed on a base-trim 2007 Fit. 2+ (base-trim confirmed)
P0420 The catalytic converter, the part that cleans exhaust gases, is not working as well as the computer expects. Rule out an active misfire or a bad upstream oxygen sensor first. Both are cheaper to fix than a converter, and both can trigger this code without the converter actually being bad. 2+
P2646 Part of the VTEC system, which uses engine oil pressure to change the intake valve lift. This code means the switch that reports oil pressure to the computer is reading a lower voltage than expected. Check engine oil level and condition first. Honda's own bulletin says this is the single most common actual cause. TSB
P2647 The same VTEC oil pressure switch as P2646, but reading a higher voltage than expected. Same as P2646: check engine oil level and condition first, before replacing the switch. TSB
P2649 The VTEC oil control solenoid, the valve that actually moves oil pressure into the VTEC system. This is a different part from the switch in P2646/P2647. Owners report a small, inexpensive O-ring or gasket at the solenoid as a common fix. This code often appears together with misfire codes on this car. Note: this code is not covered by the same Honda bulletin as P2646/P2647, unlike an earlier version of this book's notes claimed. 2+ (owner reports only)
P0335 The crankshaft position sensor, which tracks engine speed and position for timing and fuel, is sending a signal the computer cannot use. Check the sensor's electrical connector and wiring first. Owners report replacing the sensor and having the problem return right away, pointing to wiring more than the sensor itself. 2+
P0339 Same sensor and circuit as P0335, but the signal drops out only sometimes instead of failing completely. Same as P0335: check the wiring and connector first. 2+
P0500 The vehicle speed sensor, which reports how fast the car is moving to the computer and speedometer, is not sending a usable signal. Check the wiring at the transmission connector first. Owners on this car have twice found a broken wire, not the sensor itself, to be the actual cause. 2+
P0171 The fuel mixture keeps coming back leaner, meaning less fuel relative to air, than the target, and the computer is adding extra fuel to correct it. Check for a vacuum or intake leak first, such as a loose or cracked hose. If that comes up clean, worn fuel injectors are a real second possibility on this car. 2+
P0172 The opposite of P0171: the fuel mixture keeps coming back richer, meaning more fuel than the target, than expected. Check for a stuck-open EGR valve first, a part that recirculates exhaust gas into the intake. Then check fuel pressure before assuming a fuel injector problem. 2+
P2A00 The upstream air/fuel ratio sensor, Honda's more advanced version of an oxygen sensor, is reading outside its normal range. Check the sensor's connector and wiring first. Watch out for a bad-fit aftermarket sensor if this code appears right after a do-it-yourself sensor replacement. 2+
P0451 The sensor that reports pressure inside the sealed EVAP system, which is the fuel tank, canister, and lines that trap gasoline vapor instead of releasing it into the air, is sending a signal the computer does not trust. Check the sensor's electrical connector and its small vacuum hose fitting first, before replacing the sensor. 1 only
P0401 The EGR system, which feeds some exhaust gas back into the intake to lower combustion temperature, is not flowing as much as the computer expects. Clean the EGR valve and its passage first. Carbon buildup restricting the valve is the most commonly cited cause. 2+
P0113 The intake air temperature sensor is sending a reading the computer considers too high, or possibly reading as an open circuit. Check whether the code appeared right after disconnecting the battery, cleaning the throttle body, or unplugging the sensor. A code caused this way can sometimes clear itself after normal driving. 1 only

Being honest about the "1 only" codes

This book's research found only one 2007-2008 Fit-specific report for P0451 and for P0113, not the two independent reports found for every other code in this table. Both are still real, specific reports about this exact generation of car, not invented entries. But treat them as a lead worth checking, not a settled pattern the way the other 18 codes are.

What can go wrong

  • Assuming a code always means a broken part. A DTC often points to a wiring problem, a loose connector, or a low fluid level instead. Always start with the "first thing to check" column, not a parts order.
  • Ignoring a flashing check engine light. A flashing light means stop driving, not "get to it this week."
  • Clearing a code and assuming the problem is fixed. Clearing only turns off the light. If the cause is still there, the code will likely return.
  • Treating P0451 or P0113 as a fully confirmed pattern. Both only have one Fit-specific report behind them in this book's research.
  • Skipping the cheap check for P2646 or P2647. Honda's own bulletin says checking the oil level and condition comes before replacing the switch.

Quick check

  1. What does OBD-II stand for, and what does DTC stand for?
  2. Where is the OBD-II port on your 2008 Fit, and why is it in that general area on almost any car built since 1996?
  3. What does a flashing check engine light mean, and what should you do about it?
  4. Which two codes in this chapter's table are backed by an actual Honda service bulletin, and which one code was previously, incorrectly, linked to that same bulletin?
  5. Which two codes in the table have only one Fit-specific report, and how should you treat that information?

Chapter 36Won't Start, Won't Crank, or Cranks But Won't Start

What you will learn

  • The difference between "won't crank" and "cranks but won't start"
  • A 9-step flowchart to work through when your Fit will not start
  • What the immobilizer light tells you, and what to do if it blinks
  • When the problem is likely the battery, and when it is likely something else
  • When to try something yourself, and when to call for a tow

The short version

Cranking means the starter is spinning the engine over; starting means the engine actually fires and keeps running on its own. If the dashboard is completely dark, check the battery first. If the dash lights up but the engine will not crank, check your clutch pedal or shift position and the immobilizer light next. If the engine cranks normally but will not fire, think about flooding, fuel, or ignition.

The full explanation

Two different problems that feel similar

"My car won't start" can mean two very different things, and telling them apart is the fastest way to find the cause.

Won't crank means when you turn the key to START, nothing happens, or you hear only a click or a slow grinding sound. The starter, the motor that spins the engine to get it running, is not turning the engine over at a normal speed.

Cranks but won't start means the starter spins the engine at a normal speed, but the engine never fires up and keeps running on its own.

This chapter's flowchart below asks you a short question at each step, then tells you exactly where to go next depending on your answer. No step sends you down more than 3 possible paths.

The immobilizer

Your Fit has an immobilizer, an anti-theft system built into the key. The key has a chip inside it that the car's computer must recognize before the engine will start. A key-shaped light on the dashboard shows you what the immobilizer is doing. Step 3 of the flowchart below tells you what to watch for.

Step by step

The 9-step flowchart

Work through these steps in order. Each step tells you exactly which step to go to next, based on what you find.

Step 1. Turn the key to ON (the second position) without starting the engine. Do the dashboard warning lights and gauges light up normally? - If yes, go to Step 2. - If no, meaning the dashboard is completely dark, go to Step 8.

Step 2. Check your starting position. On a manual transmission, is the clutch pedal pressed all the way to the floor? On an automatic, is the shift lever fully in Park or Neutral, with the brake pedal pressed? The starter will not engage at all unless this is correct. - If this is correct and the car still will not start, go to Step 3. - If it was not correct, fix it and try again. If it now starts, you are done.

Step 3. Turn the key to ON and watch the key-shaped immobilizer light. Does it flash, or stay lit, instead of going out normally? - If yes, go to Step 4. - If no, go to Step 5.

Step 4. Try your spare key. - If the spare key starts the car, your primary key or its chip is likely damaged. Stop here and have the primary key replaced by a dealer. - If the spare key also fails to start the car, the immobilizer system itself may be at fault. Stop here and see a dealer or a qualified shop.

Step 5. Turn the key to START. Does the engine crank, meaning turn over or spin, at a normal speed? - If yes, go to Step 6. - If it makes no sound and does not move at all, go to Step 8. - If you hear a single click, or a slow grinding sound and the engine barely turns, go to Step 8.

Step 6. The engine cranks normally but does not fire. Do you smell strong raw gasoline, or did you pump the gas pedal several times before trying to start? - If yes, this may be a flooded engine, meaning too much fuel has reached it. Wait a few minutes without touching the pedal, then try again without pumping the pedal. If it still will not fire, go to Step 7. - If no, go to Step 7.

Step 7. Check the fuel gauge. Is the tank empty or very close to empty? - If yes, add fuel and try again. - If no, this points to a fuel-delivery or ignition problem. It could be the fuel pump, a fuse, the spark plugs, or the ignition coils. This needs a scan tool and a shop visit, so stop here and arrange service.

Step 8. Check the battery terminals, the metal connection points on top of the battery. Are they clean, tight, and free of white or blue corrosion? - If no, clean and tighten them, then go back to Step 1. - If yes, go to Step 9.

Step 9. Jump-starting has real risks, including sparks near battery gases. Always follow every safety warning in Chapter 16 before you connect a single cable. Once you have read those warnings, jump-start the car using the terminal order already covered in Chapter 16. Connect positive to positive on both batteries first. Then connect negative on the booster battery, then the unpainted metal ground point on your car. - Does the car start? If yes, drive for at least 20 to 30 minutes to recharge the battery, then have the battery and charging system tested soon. - If no, the starter, the alternator, or a deeper electrical fault is likely. Stop here and arrange a tow to a shop.

How you know it worked: the engine starts and keeps running on its own. The dashboard lights behave normally, and the immobilizer light goes out after its brief flash at key-ON.

What can go wrong

  • Skipping Step 2. Many "won't start" calls turn out to be a clutch pedal not fully pressed, or a shifter not fully in Park. Always check this before assuming a bigger problem.
  • Missing the immobilizer light at Step 3. A blinking key light means the chip in your key was not recognized. Trying to force a start without checking this wastes time.
  • Continuing to pump the gas pedal after a possible flooded engine. This adds more fuel and makes flooding worse, not better.
  • Jumping straight to a jump-start before checking the battery terminals. Dirty or loose terminals are checked at Step 8. That is an easy first fix that a jump-start alone would not solve.
  • Connecting jumper cables in the wrong order, or to the wrong point. Follow the exact order in Step 9 and in Chapter 16 to avoid sparks near the battery.

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between "won't crank" and "cranks but won't start"?
  2. What two starting-position checks does Step 2 ask about, and why do they matter?
  3. If the immobilizer light keeps blinking even with your spare key, what does that suggest, and what should you do?
  4. What should you do first if you suspect a flooded engine?
  5. In what order do you connect jumper cables at Step 9?

Chapter 37Noises, Vibrations, Smells, and Leaks: Lookup Tables

What you will learn

  • How to use four lookup tables to narrow down a noise, a vibration, a smell, or a leak
  • What each table's urgency rating means for how soon you should act
  • How to tell a "Fit-specific" finding from general automotive knowledge, at a glance, in each table
  • Where this book's research found a gap, meaning no Fit-specific source at all

The short version

These four tables sort common car problems by when they happen, how fast you were going, what they smell like, or what color they are. Each row also names an urgency level, from Low to High, so you know how soon to act. A "Fit" tag means real 2007-2008 Fit owners reported this pattern; a "General" tag means the row is true of gasoline cars broadly.

The full explanation

How to read these tables

Every row below has a short Source tag: - Fit — Owners report that this specific pattern shows up on real 2007-2008 Honda Fits. - General — This is true of gasoline cars broadly, and did not need, or have, a Fit-specific report.

Use Chapter 34's method before you open these tables: note when it happens, where you notice it, your speed, and any color or smell. Then find the closest matching row below.

Urgency levels mean: - Low — Usually only annoying, not dangerous. Fix it when convenient. - Medium — Worth checking soon. It can get worse or more expensive if ignored. - Medium-high — Get it checked promptly. Waiting has a real cost. - High — This can hurt you, seriously damage the car, or both. Act now.

Table 1: Noises, by when they happen

When Noise Likely cause Urgency Source
At idle Ticking or tapping from the top of the engine Low oil level, or a fault in the VTEC oil pressure switch or solenoid. VTEC is the system that uses engine oil pressure to change the intake valve lift (see Chapter 35's P2646, P2647, and P2649) Medium — check oil level today Fit
At idle Clunk when shifting into Drive or Reverse A worn engine or transmission mount, the part that holds the engine or transmission to the car's frame Medium — annoying now, but a worsening clunk can mean a fully failed mount Fit
At idle, rough or unstable Rough or hunting idle speed A vacuum leak, a dirty throttle body, or a misfire code (see Chapter 35's P0300-series codes) Medium — get it scanned for codes soon Fit
Accelerating Clicking that speeds up with engine speed but does not change with steering An exhaust heat shield, a metal panel that protects nearby parts from a hot exhaust pipe, rattling loose Low — annoying, but worth fixing before the shield falls off Fit
Accelerating, especially under load Misfire-related roughness, popping, or a flashing check engine light Spark plugs, ignition coils, or fuel injectors (see Chapter 35's misfire codes) High if the check engine light is flashing, stop driving and arrange a tow; Medium if the light is steady Fit
Turning, especially sharp turns Clicking that only happens while turning A worn outer CV joint, the flexible joint in the axle that lets power reach the wheel while it turns High — a fully failed CV joint can separate and cause loss of drive; get it inspected soon Fit
Turning, low speed, such as in a parking lot Rubbing or grinding A brake pad wear indicator touching the rotor, or a worn CV joint or steering part Medium-high — have brakes and the front end checked soon Fit
Braking High-pitched squeal Worn brake pads, whose built-in wear tab is designed to squeal on purpose, or a missing anti-squeal shim on aftermarket pads Medium-high — check pad thickness soon; worn-out pads can damage rotors Fit
Braking Grinding, metal-on-metal Brake pads worn down to the metal backing plate High — stop driving on it; rotors are likely being damaged and stopping distance is affected Fit + General
Releasing the parking brake Squeak only at that moment Usually normal friction in the parking-brake mechanism Low, if it only happens on release and brakes otherwise feel normal Fit + General
Over bumps Rattle from the dashboard or glovebox area Loose interior trim clips, often the cowl panel under the windshield or a part behind the factory radio Low — annoying, not a safety issue Fit
Over bumps Metallic clunk from the front end Worn sway-bar end links or strut top-mount bushings, small parts in the front suspension Medium — have the front suspension inspected; can worsen into a handling problem Fit
Over bumps Rattling from underneath, near the middle or rear of the car A loose or cracked exhaust heat shield Low-medium — usually only noise, but a shield that falls off can drag or become a fire risk near dry material Fit
Any time, gets louder with engine speed, not road speed Whining or grinding from the front of the engine The alternator's bearing wearing out, or the serpentine belt and its tensioner Medium-high — a failing alternator can leave you stranded with a dead battery; also check the Smells table below Fit

Table 2: Vibrations, by speed range

Speed range Vibration felt where Likely cause Urgency Source
Idle or stopped Through the seat or floor, not the steering wheel A worn engine or transmission mount, or a rough idle from a misfire or vacuum leak code Medium Fit
Low speed, under about 20 mph, goes away above that Shimmy in the steering wheel A tire out of round, a bent wheel, or a stuck brake caliper part dragging Medium General
Mid-to-highway speed, roughly 45 to 65 mph, through the steering wheel A shake that gets stronger with speed A wheel or tire out of balance, meaning its weight is not spread evenly, or a bent rim Medium-high — have wheels balanced and inspected soon; can speed up tire and bearing wear Fit + General
Highway speed, roughly 55 to 65 mph, felt through the whole car, automatic transmission only Shudder, jerk, or hesitation, not a steady vibration Owners report a split opinion: it could be the automatic transmission itself, or worn ignition coils, valve clearance, or spark plugs presenting as a shudder Medium-high — get it scanned for codes; do not assume it is only the transmission without a check, since the ignition-related cause is cheaper to fix Fit
Any speed, a growl or vibration that grows with speed, sometimes louder in one turning direction Vibration paired with a grinding or humming noise A worn wheel bearing, the part that lets the wheel spin smoothly Medium-high — a bearing that fails completely can cause a wheel to lock up or separate General
Braking, any speed Pulsation felt in the brake pedal A warped or worn brake rotor Medium — schedule a brake inspection; not usually an immediate emergency, but it will worsen General

Table 3: Smells

Smell Likely cause Urgency Source
Burning rubber, especially after highway driving or with the heater on The heater blower motor or its resistor overheating, or a drive belt slipping Medium — have it checked soon; a slipping belt can eventually fail and strand you Fit
Burning plastic or electrical The alternator or another electrical part overheating; also check for a stuck power window switch, given this car's known switch recall High — this can be a fire risk. Pull over, shut off the engine, and have it inspected before driving further Fit
Sweet, syrupy smell, especially from the heater vents or under the hood Leaking coolant, the fluid that keeps the engine's temperature down Medium-high — check coolant level; do not keep driving if the temperature gauge or light also shows overheating General + Fit
Rotten egg A catalytic converter running too rich, or, in one specific report on this car, a faint exhaust smell only with the A/C on, while idling, after hard acceleration in hot weather Medium — get it scanned for the fuel-trim, misfire, and catalyst codes covered in Chapter 35; a converter that keeps overheating from a rich mixture can be destroyed General + Fit
Raw gasoline A fuel leak, an EVAP system leak (the system that traps gasoline vapor), or an overfilled gas tank right after fueling High if it is a steady, strong smell; do not start the engine near an open flame or smoking. Medium if it is a faint smell right after topping off the tank General + Fit
Hot oil or a burning-oil smell An oil leak dripping onto a hot exhaust part Medium — check the oil level and look underneath for drips; oil pooling near hot exhaust for a long time can become a fire risk General
Clear, odorless puddle in humid or rainy weather Normal air conditioning condensation None — this is normal and not a leak to worry about General

Table 4: Leaks, by color and location

Two things about this car matter for reading this table. First, the 2008 Fit uses electric power steering, not a hydraulic pump, so there is no separate red or pink power-steering fluid puddle to worry about. Any red fluid you see is almost certainly automatic transmission fluid instead, on automatic-transmission cars. Second, on a manual transmission, the clutch and the brakes share the same fluid reservoir and fluid type. A clear-to-amber leak near the driver's side firewall could point to either system.

Location Color Likely fluid or cause Urgency Source
Under the front of the car, roughly under the engine Amber to dark brown, slightly oily Engine oil Medium-high — check the dipstick; do not run the engine low on oil General
Under the front, closer to the center or passenger side, thin and slippery Red or pink Automatic transmission fluid; this car has no hydraulic power-steering fluid to confuse it with Medium-high — have the transmission checked; low fluid can cause the highway shudder symptom in Table 2 General
Under the front, sweet-smelling Green, yellow-green, orange, pink, or blue, depending on which coolant was last used Coolant Medium-high — do not keep driving if it is also overheating; top up and have the leak found and fixed General + Fit
Under the middle of the car, near the driver's side firewall, clear to light amber, slippery, not sweet-smelling Brake fluid or clutch fluid, since they share one reservoir on a manual-transmission Fit High — brake fluid loss is a safety issue; stop driving and have it inspected right away if the pedal feels low or soft, or a brake warning light is on General + Fit
Under the middle-front area, thin, dark, smells strongly of gear oil Manual transmission fluid Medium — top up or have it checked; low fluid can cause hard shifting or gear noise General
Under the middle of the car, clear, odorless, only in hot or humid weather with the A/C running Air conditioning condensate water None — this is normal General
Under the rear of the car Any fluid color This book's research found no Fit-specific pattern for rear-of-car leaks; the fuel tank and rear brake and axle parts are the main candidates High if it smells like gasoline; Medium otherwise, pending identification General (gap: no Fit-specific source found)

What can go wrong

  • Treating any table as a final diagnosis. These tables narrow things down. A shop visit or a code check may still be needed to confirm the exact cause.
  • Ignoring a High-urgency row because the car "seems fine." Brake fluid loss, a burning-plastic smell, and a flashing check engine light are all High for a reason.
  • Mixing up red transmission fluid with power-steering fluid. This car has no hydraulic power-steering fluid, so red or pink almost always means automatic transmission fluid instead.
  • Assuming the rear-of-car leak row is settled. This book's research found no Fit-specific source for that row, and says so honestly rather than guessing.
  • Skipping Chapter 34's four questions before opening these tables. When, where, at what speed, and what color or smell, make these tables far faster to use.

  3. Why is red or pink fluid under the front of this car almost always transmission fluid, not power-steering fluid?

Chapter 38Known Weak Spots on the 2007-2008 Fit

What you will learn

  • What "owners report that" means, and how it is different from a recall.
  • Symptoms for 11 wear problems that first-generation Fit owners report.
  • A rough shop repair cost for each problem, at today's prices.
  • Whether each problem is a good DIY job, or one to leave to a shop.
  • The one problem whose repair cost this book could not confirm for the 2008 Fit.

The short version

Owners of 2007-2008 Fits report 11 wear problems that show up again and again. None of these are recalls. They are patterns spotted by real drivers, not confirmed defects from Honda or the government. Every dollar figure below is a 2024-2026 shop price, not a 2008 price, so an actual 2008 repair bill would have been lower.

The full explanation

Why this is different from Chapter 18

Chapter 18 covered recalls and TSBs. A recall is a safety fix Honda must offer for free. A TSB is a repair instruction Honda sends to dealers. Both come from Honda or the government. This chapter is different. It covers patterns that owners on Fit forums have reported seeing again and again on their own cars. This book marks each one with the phrase "owners report that." That phrase means the pattern comes from many individual drivers, not from an official investigation. A community-reported problem can still be real and common. It only means no official body has confirmed it.

A word about the prices in this chapter

Every dollar amount below comes from repair-cost websites and forum posts checked in 2026. These are today's shop prices, for cars generally 16 to 20 years old. They are not what a repair would have cost when your Fit was new, in 2007 or 2008. Prices also change by region, by shop, and by whether a part is new, aftermarket, or used. Treat every number here as a rough guide, not an exact quote.

The 11 weak spots

1. Motor mounts. Owners report that motor mounts wear out over the life of the car . A motor mount is a rubber-and-metal part that bolts the engine and transmission to the car's frame . When a mount wears out, you may feel a clunk when you shift into Drive or Reverse . You may also feel a clunk under hard acceleration . You may feel extra vibration at idle . Shop cost for one mount runs roughly $495 to $908, depending on which price page you check . New Honda mounts, called OEM parts, cost about $130 each . OEM means Original Equipment Manufacturer, the same brand Honda used when it built your car . Generic aftermarket mounts run about $30 to $40 each . Aftermarket means a part made by a company other than Honda . This job is only partly DIY-able . DIY means "do it yourself," without paying a shop . You need a way to support the engine's weight while a mount is off . You also need jack stands and a torque wrench . A first-timer should have a helper or a shop manual close by .

2. Timing chain tensioner noise. Owners report that a ticking or rattling noise comes from the front of the engine . It is most noticeable at a cold startup, and it quiets down as the engine warms up . A timing chain tensioner keeps the timing chain tight, so the engine's valves open and close at the right moment . This is the weakest-sourced item in this chapter . Researchers found only one usable online report of this exact problem on a 2008 Fit, not the two separate reports this book normally requires . Treat this as a possible pattern, not a settled fact . We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. For other small cars, this same kind of tensioner job can run from several hundred dollars to more than a thousand dollars . The job costs so much because a mechanic has to remove the front cover of the engine to reach the part . This is not a beginner job . A chain that slips out of time can bend the engine's valves . Take this repair to a shop .

3. Clutch master or slave cylinder wear (manual transmission only). Owners report that the clutch pedal can suddenly go spongy or soft . Sometimes the pedal drops to the floor with little resistance . Sometimes there is no fluid puddle under the car, because the leak happens inside the cylinder . A master cylinder and a slave cylinder work together to make the clutch disengage when you press the pedal . Shops usually replace both cylinders together . They do this because the second cylinder tends to fail soon after the first . A full job, with both cylinders and a fluid bleed, commonly runs $700 to $1,000 or more . This job is only partly DIY-able . Swapping the cylinders themselves is not hard . Bleeding air out of the hydraulic line afterward is easy to get wrong the first time .

4. Automatic transmission shudder (automatic transmission only). Owners report that the car shudders, jerks, or hesitates at about 55 to 65 miles per hour on flat ground . Forum owners disagree on the cause . Some blame the transmission itself . Others blame worn spark plugs or ignition coils instead . A torque converter is a part inside the automatic transmission that links the spinning engine to the transmission . If a worn torque converter turns out to be the cause, expect a bill around $800 to $1,600 . This is a general repair-shop price, not a number specific to the Fit . Diagnosing this shudder is not a DIY job . It needs real test time and, ideally, a scan tool that reads transmission codes . Changing the transmission fluid and filter first is a cheap, DIY-able step worth trying before paying for deeper diagnosis .

5. Air conditioning compressor and condenser problems. Owners report that two separate air conditioning complaints show up on this car . First, the condenser can get damaged by road debris . A condenser is a radiator-like part, mounted low at the front of the car, that cools the refrigerant . One 2008 Fit owner told the government this exact repair cost over $800 . Second, some owners report a rattling compressor pulley when the air conditioning turns on . Others report air conditioning that cools for a minute and then stops . Shop cost for a full compressor replacement runs roughly $1,100 to $1,800, and can run higher . This is not a DIY job . Opening an air conditioning system needs a special license . That license is called EPA Section 609 certification . You also need equipment to safely remove the old refrigerant gas . A home garage does not have this equipment .

6. Front suspension clunk or knock. Owners report that a metallic clunk or knock comes from the front of the car over bumps . Forum owners most often trace this to one of two parts . The first is a worn sway bar end link . A sway bar end link is a small linking part that connects the sway bar to the suspension . The second is a worn strut top mount . A strut top mount is the rubber bushing where the top of a strut bolts to the car's body . Owner advice is to check the end links first, before paying for a full strut job . Sway bar end links cost roughly $75 to $200 to replace on one side, or $200 to $300 to do both sides at once . A full pair of struts, with new top mounts, runs roughly $400 to $900 . End links are a real beginner DIY job . The job needs only a floor jack, jack stands, and hand tools . Struts need a spring compressor tool and real caution . A released spring can badly hurt you .

7. Dashboard or glovebox rattle. Owners report that a rattle comes from the dashboard or glovebox area over bumps . It is most often traced to a loose plastic panel under the windshield . Sometimes it is a part behind the factory radio instead . The rattle tends to get worse in cold weather . There is no set price for this job, since it depends on finding the cause first . Shops typically bill it as one diagnostic fee visit . A diagnostic fee is the charge for the time a shop spends finding a problem before fixing it . That fee runs roughly $50 to $150 at an independent shop . It can run $100 to $400 or more at a dealer . Once found, the actual fix often costs under $20 in parts, such as a broken clip . This is a good DIY job for a patient beginner . It needs only basic plastic trim-removal tools .

8. Clearcoat peeling. Owners report that the clearcoat flakes or peels on flat, horizontal surfaces after several years . The roof and hood are the surfaces owners mention most . Clearcoat is the clear, protective top layer of a car's paint . Owners, and a paint-repair professional in one forum discussion, blame thin or improperly cured factory clearcoat . Fixing one panel, like only the roof, commonly costs $500 to $1,500 or more . A full repaint is sometimes needed once the damage has spread across several panels . A full repaint can run $2,000 to $5,000, and sometimes over $10,000 . This is not a DIY job . Matching factory paint color takes real skill . Spraying an even clearcoat without runs also takes real skill and equipment .

9. Rust at the rear quarter panel and wheel well. Owners report that rust starts where the rear quarter panel meets the rear bumper cover . It also starts along a hidden seam near the rocker panel and wheel well . This spot collects road grit and moisture . Left alone, it can eat through to holes in the driver's rear wheel well . This mostly affects cars driven where roads get salted in winter . Cleaning and treating surface rust early costs roughly $150 to $900 . A real patch repair, once metal has to be cut and welded, runs roughly $800 to $1,800 . A full panel replacement can run up to about $2,500 . Catching rust early is a real DIY job . It uses a rust converter product you can buy cheaply . Welding in a new patch panel needs skill and equipment most beginners do not have .

10. Battery drain. Owners report that the battery goes dead after sitting for two or three days . Some owners report the battery draining overnight instead . Reported causes include a faulty door switch, an electrical short, or an aftermarket add-on wired into the fuse box, such as an alarm . One technical forum post says a healthy 2008 Fit should draw only about 15 to 17 milliamps with everything off . A milliamp, written mA, is a small unit of electric current . A draw above roughly 40 to 50 mA points to a real fault worth chasing . Basic diagnosis at a shop runs $50 to $100 . Tracing a harder short can run $150 to $400 or more . After that, a new battery costs $50 to $200 . A new alternator runs $200 to $600 . A wiring repair starts around $100 and climbs with complexity . The diagnostic step is DIY-able with a simple multimeter and patience . Replacing a battery or a simple switch is beginner-easy . Chasing an intermittent wiring short is much harder .

11. Weak factory radio and speakers. Owners report that the factory speaker system sounds thin . Some trims are missing rear door speakers . Some head units lack a way to plug in an aftermarket amplifier . This is a comfort complaint, not a safety defect . A professional head-unit swap commonly costs $150 to $550 all-in for a basic unit . A unit with Bluetooth or a phone-mirroring screen costs more . Replacing all four speakers plus the head unit at a shop runs roughly $600 to $1,100 . This is one of the most beginner-friendly car projects there is . It needs only a screwdriver and a trim-panel tool . A wiring adapter is a good idea too, so you do not have to cut the factory wiring .

Putting it together

Most of these 11 items are wear-and-tear, not danger . None of them stop you from driving your car safely on their own . Still, always fix a real safety issue, like a shuddering transmission or a suspension clunk, before it gets worse . The next chapter shows you how to find a fair shop for any repair on this list that you cannot, or should not, do yourself.

Quick check

  1. What does "owners report that" mean, and how is it different from a recall?
  2. Name three of the 11 weak spots and their main symptom.
  3. Which weak spot's repair cost could this book not confirm for the 2008 Fit, and why not?
  4. Name one weak spot that is a good DIY job for a beginner, and one that is not.
  5. Why are the dollar amounts in this chapter higher than what a 2008 Fit owner actually paid back in 2008?

Chapter 39When to Go to a Shop, and How to Not Get Ripped Off

What you will learn

  • The difference between an independent shop and a dealership, and why their prices differ.
  • Typical hourly labor rates for each, at today's prices.
  • Three situations where paying a dealer's higher rate still makes sense.
  • How to read every line of a repair estimate before you agree to pay.
  • Simple habits that protect you from a surprise bill.

The short version

Independent shops usually charge less per hour than dealerships. A dealer is still the right choice in a few specific cases. Every repair estimate has the same five parts: a diagnostic fee, labor, parts, shop supplies, and tax. Read every line before you agree to a repair, and compare the final bill to that estimate before you pay.

The full explanation

Independent shop versus dealership: labor rates

A labor rate is the hourly price a shop charges for a mechanic's time . It is separate from the cost of parts . An independent shop is a repair shop not owned by a car maker . Independent shops commonly charge $80 to $130 per hour . Dealerships commonly charge $120 to $180 per hour . A separate national study found that nearly half of all US repair shops price their labor somewhere between $120 and $159 per hour . Dealerships tend to charge more for a few reasons . Their mechanics go through the car maker's own training programs . They must buy diagnostic tools the maker requires . They also carry higher costs from running a franchise and a larger building .

For a routine job you already understand, like brakes or a motor mount, an independent shop can often do equal-quality work for meaningfully less money . Look for a shop with mechanics who hold ASE certification, a widely recognized test for mechanics . A dealer becomes the better choice in three cases . First, the repair needs a special tool only the maker sells . Second, the repair is still covered by a warranty, a recall, or a TSB; see Chapter 18 for your car's own recall list . Third, the needed part is sold only through the dealer's own parts network .

How to read a repair estimate

Ask for a written estimate before any work starts . Do not settle for a spoken number only . A complete estimate has five parts .

Diagnostic fee. This is the charge for the time a shop spends figuring out what is wrong . It comes before any repair is agreed to . It commonly covers 60 to 90 minutes of labor . Many shops apply this fee toward your repair bill if you have the work done there . Ask this question before you say yes to the diagnosis .

Labor. This line shows the job name, a number of hours, and the shop's hourly rate . Hours are often written in tenths, so 3.5 means three and a half hours . You can check the math yourself by multiplying the hours by the rate .

Parts. Each parts line should say exactly what is being replaced . It should also say what kind of part it is . A part can be new OEM, meaning Original Equipment Manufacturer, the same brand Honda used . A part can be new aftermarket, made by a different company . A part can be remanufactured, meaning a used part rebuilt to work like new . A part can also be plain used . A cheaper price on this line often means a lower-tier part, which is worth asking about .

Shop supplies. This line covers small extra costs, like rags, cleaning fluid, and small fasteners . It is normal to see this charge . It is usually about 5 to 10 percent of the labor total . A vague fee, with no clear dollar amount or percentage, is worth questioning .

Tax. Sales tax applies to parts in most states . Whether labor also gets taxed depends on your state . This line will vary by where you live .

Habits that protect you

Get every estimate in writing before work starts . Check that the paperwork lists your own car's VIN and mileage, not another car's . A VIN is your car's own 17-character serial number, covered in Chapter 18 . When the final bill arrives, compare it line by line against the original estimate . A bill that grew without a phone call explaining why is a warning sign .

Tying it back to Chapter 38

Chapter 38 told you which of the 11 weak spots a beginner can safely try at home. When a repair on that list is marked "no," or needs tools you do not have, use this chapter's habits before you pay. Ask for a written estimate. Read every line. Compare the final bill against it. Doing this each time protects you far more than trying to memorize a "fair price" for every possible repair.

Quick check

  1. What is the usual hourly labor-rate range at an independent shop, and at a dealership?
  2. Name three situations where paying a dealer's higher rate makes sense.
  3. What are the five parts of a complete repair estimate?
  4. What does OEM mean, and how is it different from aftermarket and remanufactured?
  5. What two things should you always compare before paying a repair bill?

Part VIIRepairs You Can Do at Home

Chapter 40Brake job, full

What you will learn

  • The difference between a basic pad or shoe swap and a full brake job
  • How to check a front brake rotor and replace it along with the pads
  • How to check a rear brake drum and replace or resurface it
  • The extra tools a full brake job needs beyond a basic pad or shoe job
  • What can go wrong on these harder steps, and how to catch it

The short version

Chapter 27 already showed you how to swap front brake pads and rear brake shoes on their own. A full brake job goes one step further: you also check the front rotor and the rear drum, and replace either one if it is worn out. You do this work with the same jack stands, the same care, and mostly the same tools as Chapter 27, plus a few extras for the rotor and the drum.

The full explanation

A rotor is the flat metal disc that spins with your front wheel. The caliper squeezes your brake pads against it to slow the car. A drum is the metal cylinder that spins with your rear wheel. Your brake shoes press outward against the inside of the drum to slow the car.

Pads and shoes wear out on their own schedule, and so do rotors and drums. A rotor or drum can wear thin, get scored with deep grooves, or crack, even while the pads or shoes next to it still have life left. Both the rotor and the drum carry a wear-limit number stamped into the metal. If your measurement goes past that number, or the metal is cracked or deeply grooved, you replace the part instead of reusing it.

This chapter does not repeat the pad and shoe steps from Chapter 27. It focuses only on what a full brake job adds: taking the rotor or drum off, checking it, and putting a new or resurfaced one back on.

Step by step

Front brakes: pads and rotors together

Do this job with the safety rules from Chapter 27 in mind. Always wear eye protection, since brake dust and rust flakes can hurt your eyes. Do not breathe brake dust, and never use compressed air or a dry brush on brake parts; use brake-parts cleaner instead. Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. Work on one wheel at a time and leave the other wheel on the ground, so you always have a reference for how the parts go back together. Never let the caliper hang by its brake hose; support it so the hose carries no weight. Never resurface or reuse a rotor that is cracked, deeply grooved, or thinner than its stamped minimum thickness; replace it instead. Always pump the brake pedal several times before you drive, until it feels firm again.

  1. Loosen the front wheel nuts, then raise and support the car on jack stands, and remove the wheel.
  2. Remove the caliper slide-pin bolts and lift the caliper off its bracket. Hang the caliper so it does not pull on the brake hose.
  3. Remove the two caliper mounting-bracket bolts and lift the bracket off the steering knuckle, the metal part the wheel bearing sits in.
  4. Check the rotor's edge for a minimum-thickness number stamped into the metal, and write it down before you remove the rotor.
  5. If the rotor is stuck to the hub, tap it near its center with a rubber mallet. Never hit the braking surface.
  6. Pull the old rotor off the hub studs.
  7. Clean rust and debris off the hub face with a wire brush, then wipe it clean.
  8. Apply a thin coat of anti-seize compound to the hub face only. Keep it off the braking surface.
  9. Slide the new rotor onto the hub studs.
  10. Clean the caliper bracket and slide pins with a wire brush and brake-parts cleaner.
  11. Reinstall the caliper bracket. Owners report torquing its mounting bolts to 80 lbf-ft (108 N-m), but this number is not independently confirmed for this car.
  12. Use a C-clamp against an old pad, or a block of wood, to push the caliper piston back into its bore.
  13. Coat the new pads' metal backing and the slide pins with a thin layer of brake grease, then place the pads into the bracket.
  14. Slide the caliper back over the new pads and reinstall the slide-pin bolts.
  15. Reinstall the wheel, snug the nuts by hand, then lower the car and torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf-ft (108 N-m) in a crisscross pattern.
  16. Repeat steps 1 through 15 on the other front wheel.
  17. Pump the brake pedal several times with the engine off, until it feels firm. Then check the brake fluid reservoir and top it off if needed.
  18. Drive slowly at first and test the brakes gently in an empty area before you return to normal driving.

Rear brakes: drums

Chapter 27 already walked you through the rear brake shoe swap on this car, including the return spring, which is the hardest single step in that job. This section only adds the drum check. Do it in the same session as the shoe swap, since the drum has to come off to reach the shoes anyway.

Always wear eye protection when you inspect or clean a drum. Never reinstall a drum that is cracked, deeply scored, or worn past its stamped maximum diameter; replace it instead. Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands.

  1. Follow the first three steps of Chapter 27's rear brake job: raise the car, remove the wheel, and pull the drum off the hub.
  2. Look for a maximum-diameter number cast or stamped into the drum's outer face.
  3. Measure the drum's inside diameter with a micrometer or caliper, checking several points around the circle.
  4. If any measurement goes past the stamped maximum diameter, or the surface has deep grooves or cracks, plan to replace both rear drums as a pair.
  5. If the drum is within its limit but glazed or lightly scored, a machine shop can resurface it instead of replacing it. Ask the shop whether resurfacing costs less than a new drum.
  6. If you are replacing the drum, do not transfer anything from the old drum itself. Only the shoes, springs, and adjuster carry over.
  7. Finish the rest of Chapter 27's rear brake shoe steps, using the new or resurfaced drum in place of the old one.

What can go wrong

Getting anti-seize compound or grease on the rotor's braking surface, or on the pad linings, causes squealing and cuts your stopping power. Reusing a rotor or drum that is warped, cracked, or past its wear limit is the most common mistake in a full brake job. Always check the stamped number before you decide to reuse a part. Pushing a caliper piston back without first loosening the brake fluid reservoir cap can push fluid out or damage a seal. Letting a caliper hang by its hose can stretch or kink the hose. Replacing only one side's drum, instead of both as a pair, can cause the car to pull or brake unevenly. And skipping the "pump the pedal first" step, on either job, can mean the pedal goes soft or all the way to the floor on your first drive.

Quick check

  1. What two extra parts does a full brake job add on top of Chapter 27's basic pad and shoe swap?
  2. How do you know a rotor or drum has to be replaced instead of reused?
  3. Why should you never let a caliper hang by its brake hose?
  4. Why should you check both rear drums, instead of only the one side that looks worn?
  5. What should you do with the brake pedal before your first drive after the job, and why?

Chapter 41Suspension: struts, shocks, sway bar links, bushings

What you will learn

  • Why the front struts are a shop job, but the rear shocks are not
  • What a shop will do when it replaces your front struts
  • How to replace your rear shocks yourself, step by step
  • How to replace your front sway bar end links and rear stabilizer bushing
  • The difference between an end-link swap and a full sway bar replacement
  • What can go wrong on each of these jobs

The short version

Your Fit's suspension holds the car up, controls its bounce, and keeps the tires planted on bumps. Some suspension parts are easy weekend jobs, and some are not. The front struts need a spring compressor and carry a real injury risk, so this book treats that one as a shop job. The rear shocks, the front sway bar end links, and the rear stabilizer bushing are all simpler jobs you can do yourself with basic tools and jack stands.

The full explanation

A strut and a shock absorber, often called a "shock" for short, do the same basic job: they slow down your suspension's bounce after a bump. A sway bar, also called a stabilizer bar, is a metal bar that connects the suspension on the car's left side to its right side. It reduces how much the body leans in turns. A sway bar end link is a short linkage part that connects the end of the sway bar to the suspension. A bushing is a rubber or rubber-and-metal piece that cushions a joint and lets it flex slightly.

Your Fit's front suspension uses a MacPherson strut, a design where a coil spring wraps directly around the strut itself. That spring stores a large amount of energy while compressed. A tool called a spring compressor squeezes the spring so a mechanic can safely take the strut apart. If a compressor slips or is used the wrong way, it can release that stored energy suddenly and cause serious injury or death. This book could not confirm the exact top-nut torque number for this car's front strut from two independent sources. That gap is one more reason this book sends the front strut job to a shop, instead of guessing at that number.

Your Fit's rear suspension is different. The rear coil spring sits on its own in the rear axle beam, not wrapped around the shock. That means the rear shock job needs no spring compressor at all, which makes it a meaningfully easier job than the front strut.

This chapter also covers two smaller jobs that people often lump together under "sway bar links" and "bushings": swapping the front end links, and swapping the rear stabilizer bushing. Replacing the sway bar itself, the long bar rather than its end links, is a much bigger job on this car. It requires dropping the front subframe and the steering gearbox. This book treats a full sway bar swap as a shop job, and does not give full steps for it here.

What the shop will do: front struts

Even if a shop does this job, know what to ask about, before you read the summary below. Always treat a coil spring on a strut as if it stores enough energy to cause serious injury if it lets go suddenly and out of control. Never let anyone attempt this job with a homemade spring compressor, such as rope, chain, or a bar clamp; a real job uses a purpose-built strut spring compressor. Always ask the shop to check, and if needed perform, a wheel alignment after the strut job.

A shop replacing your front struts will typically:
