  • Raise the car and remove the front wheel on each side.
  • Disconnect the strut from the steering knuckle, then unbolt the brake hose bracket and any ABS sensor wiring clip from the strut.
  • Remove the three top strut-mount nuts from inside the engine bay and lift the strut and spring out as one assembly.
  • On a workbench, compress the spring with a proper spring compressor, then remove the top-mount nut while holding the shaft still with a hex wrench. Take the strut apart and rebuild it with the new part.
  • Reinstall the new strut assembly in reverse order and torque every fastener to the specification in the shop's own service information.
  • Perform a wheel alignment afterward. New struts can change ride height slightly. That can shift camber, how far the top of a tire tilts in or out, or toe, how far the tires point toward or away from each other.

Step by step

Rear shocks (both sides)

Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. Use a floor jack only to support the axle beam while you work, not as your only support. Always wear eye protection when working under the car. Never let the shock swing loose and strike the fuel tank, brake lines, or wiring; keep control of its movement as you remove or install it. Always take care not to scratch the car's body while maneuvering the shock in and out of its mounting spot.

  1. Raise the rear of the car, support it on jack stands, and remove both rear wheels.
  2. Remove the wire guide grommet from both sides of the rear axle beam, the metal beam that the rear wheels attach to.
  3. Position a floor jack under the lower spring seat on the axle beam. Raise it slightly, only enough for the suspension to begin compressing.
  4. Remove the damper mounting bolt that connects the axle beam to the bottom of the shock.
  5. Remove the access panel from the cargo area's side trim to reach the shock's top mount.
  6. Hold the shock shaft still with a hex wrench, and remove the self-locking top nut.
  7. Remove the top mounting washer and rubber mount.
  8. Compress the shock by hand and lift it out of the car, taking care not to scratch the body.
  9. Repeat steps 1 through 8 on the other side, if you are replacing both shocks.
  10. Fit the rubber mount onto the new shock's top, and position the shock between the body and the axle beam.
  11. Raise the floor jack slowly until the bottom mounting hole lines up. Loosely fit the new bottom mounting bolt. Honda's own procedure calls for a new bolt here, not the old one.
  12. Raise the axle beam with the jack until the car's rear barely lifts off the jack stands, then torque the bottom mounting bolt to 40 lbf-ft (54 N-m).
  13. Fit the top rubber mount, washer, and a new self-locking nut onto the shock shaft. Honda's own procedure calls for a new nut here too.
  14. Hold the shaft still with the hex wrench and tighten the top nut. We could not confirm the exact torque number for this nut on this pass; check Appendix C or your new shock's instructions for it.
  15. Reinstall the access panel, the wire guide grommets, and both rear wheels.
  16. Lower the car and torque the wheel nuts to 80 lbf-ft (108 N-m) in a crisscross pattern.

Front sway bar end links (both sides)

Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. Always wear eye protection, especially when you use penetrating oil or a cutting tool on rusted fasteners. Never use a cutting wheel near brake lines, fuel lines, or wiring; check what is nearby before you cut a stuck fastener.

  1. Raise the front of the car and support it on jack stands, with both front wheels off the ground.
  2. Spray the end-link nuts with penetrating oil and let it sit for a few minutes.
  3. Hold the end-link stud still with a hex key while loosening the nut with a wrench.
  4. If the stud spins instead of the nut, grip the stud's shoulder with vice grips.
  5. If a nut is fully seized, cut it off carefully with a cutting wheel rather than forcing it loose.
  6. Remove the old end link from both its top connection and its bottom connection to the sway bar.
  7. Fit the new end link, and start both nuts by hand.
  8. Check the new part's instructions for the suspension position it wants during tightening: unloaded, with the wheel off the ground, or loaded to normal ride height.
  9. Position the suspension the way the instructions call for, then tighten both nuts snug.
  10. Repeat steps 2 through 9 on the other side.
  11. Lower the car and check that both end links move freely as the suspension travels.

Rear stabilizer bushing

Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. Always cover nearby electrical connectors before using silicone spray, since silicone residue can interfere with switches and sensors.

  1. Raise the rear of the car and support it on jack stands.
  2. Remove the old stabilizer bushing from the axle beam.
  3. Apply silicone spray to the new bushing to ease its installation.
  4. Line up the new bushing's end face with the center of its mounting hole, and press it into the axle beam.
  5. Make sure the stabilizer bar seats fully into the bushing's groove, and does not stick out past the end of the axle beam.

What can go wrong

Skipping the alignment check after a strut job can lead to uneven tire wear later. Reusing a worn or cracked top-mount bearing instead of replacing it along with the strut is a common shortcut that causes new noises. On the rear shocks, not supporting the axle beam with a floor jack before removing the bottom bolt can let the beam drop suddenly. Reusing the old self-locking top nut or bottom bolt, instead of the new ones Honda's procedure calls for, is another common mistake. On the sway bar end links, forcing a seized nut without penetrating oil first can snap the stud. Tightening the links with the suspension hanging free, instead of at normal ride height, can pre-load the rubber wrong. On the rear bushing, getting silicone spray on nearby electrical connectors can cause switch problems later. Finally, do not confuse a noisy or loose end link with a problem in the sway bar's own mounting bushings on the subframe. That bigger issue means the full bar has to come out, which is the shop-recommended job, not the end-link swap in this chapter.

Quick check

  1. Why does this book send the front strut job to a shop but not the rear shock job?
  2. What is a spring compressor for, and why is it dangerous if used the wrong way?
  3. Name two parts Honda's own procedure says to replace with new ones every time during a rear shock job, rather than reusing the old ones.
  4. What is the difference between replacing a sway bar end link and replacing the sway bar itself?
  5. Why should you check silicone spray placement carefully during the rear stabilizer bushing job?

Chapter 42Cooling system: thermostat, radiator, water pump

What you will learn

  • What the thermostat, radiator, and water pump each do to keep your engine cool
  • How to replace the thermostat
  • How to replace the radiator
  • How to replace the water pump, and why it is easier on this car than on many others
  • The safety rules for working on a hot cooling system, and what can go wrong

The short version

Your engine's cooling system moves coolant, a special liquid, through the engine to carry heat away. The thermostat controls when that coolant starts flowing to the radiator. The radiator gets rid of the heat. The water pump pushes the coolant around the whole loop. All three jobs need the engine fully cool first. A hot system can spray scalding coolant when opened.

The full explanation

The thermostat is a small valve in the cooling system. It stays shut while the engine is cold, so the engine warms up faster. Once the engine reaches its normal operating temperature, the thermostat opens, and coolant can flow to the radiator. On your Fit, the thermostat sits inside a housing that Honda's own service information calls the "water outlet."

The radiator is a large, flat, finned unit at the front of the car. It takes hot coolant from the engine and lets outside air pass through it, cooling the coolant before it flows back to the engine. If your Fit has an automatic transmission, the radiator also carries a set of hose connections that cool the automatic transmission fluid. You need a radiator that matches your transmission type.

The water pump is the part that physically pushes coolant through the whole system. On many cars, the water pump is driven by the timing chain or timing belt deep inside the engine, which makes it a hard job. On your Fit, the water pump pulley is driven by the same external drive belt the Maintenance Minder already checks. You can reach it from the right front wheel well. That makes the water pump a meaningfully easier job on this car than on many others.

For all three jobs, check Appendix B for the correct coolant type and the amount your Fit needs after a refill. Coolant is toxic. Keep spills away from pets and children, since it can taste sweet to animals.

Step by step

Replace the thermostat

Always let the engine cool completely before you open the cooling system; a hot system can spray scalding coolant. Never open the radiator cap or the thermostat housing while the engine is warm or running. Always wear eye protection when you drain or refill coolant. Never let coolant sit on your skin for long, and never leave a spill where a pet or child could reach it. Never run the engine until you have refilled the system and bled the air out of it.

  1. Make sure the engine is completely cool before you start.
  2. Remove the air cleaner assembly to reach the thermostat housing area.
  3. Drain the engine coolant into a clean drain pan.
  4. Disconnect the upper radiator hose and the water bypass hose from the housing.
  5. Unplug the coolant temperature sensor's connector. Then remove the housing from the cylinder head.
  6. Lift the old thermostat out of the housing, and note which side faces the engine.
  7. Remove all old gasket material from the housing's mating surfaces, its bolts, and the bolt holes, then clean and dry every surface.
  8. Fit a new sealing ring to the coolant temperature sensor, and set the sensor aside for now.
  9. Apply Honda liquid gasket, part number 08717-0004, 08718-0003, 08718-0004, or 08718-0009, to the housing's mating surface, following the bead pattern on the tube.
  10. Install the housing quickly. Some Honda liquid gaskets start to skin over in as little as 4 to 5 minutes.
  11. Plug the coolant temperature sensor's connector back in. Then reconnect the upper radiator hose and the water bypass hose.
  12. Reinstall the air cleaner assembly.
  13. Wait at least 30 minutes before you refill the system with coolant.
  14. Do not run the engine for at least 3 hours after you install the housing, so the gasket can cure.
  15. Refill the radiator with the correct coolant and bleed the air out, following your car's own fill procedure.
  16. Clean up any spilled coolant right away.
  17. Start the engine, let it reach normal operating temperature, and check for leaks around the housing.

Replace the radiator

Always let the engine cool completely before you drain coolant or disconnect hoses. Never open the radiator cap while the engine is warm or running. Always wear eye protection when draining or refilling coolant. Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands. Always keep your hands clear of the electric radiator fan; it can switch on even with the engine off, if the coolant is still hot.

  1. Make sure the engine is completely cool before you start.
  2. Raise the car and support it on jack stands, if you need to reach the lower hose and drain plug.
  3. Drain the engine coolant into a clean drain pan.
  4. Unplug the coolant temperature sensor connector near the radiator. Unclip its harness clamp.
  5. If your car has air conditioning, unplug the A/C compressor clutch connector. Move its harness clamps out of the way.
  6. Disconnect the lower radiator hose.
  7. On an automatic transmission car, disconnect the automatic transmission fluid cooler hoses, and plug both the hose and the line to keep dirt out.
  8. Lower the car if it is raised, and remove the coolant reservoir.
  9. Unplug the radiator fan motor connector, and the air conditioning condenser fan motor connector if equipped. Unclip their harness clamps.
  10. Disconnect the upper radiator hose. Then remove the radiator's upper mounting brackets.
  11. Lift the radiator up and out. If your car has air conditioning, remove the condenser fan shroud first.
  12. Remove the fan shroud, radiator cap, coolant temperature sensor, and drain plug from the old radiator. Reuse the ones that are still good, or transfer them by your new radiator's instructions.
  13. Fit new O-rings and assemble the fan shroud, cap, sensor, and drain plug onto the new radiator.
  14. Set the new radiator in place, making sure its lower rubber cushions seat firmly.
  15. Reinstall the upper mounting brackets, then reconnect the upper radiator hose.
  16. Plug in the fan motor connectors and their harness clamps. Then reinstall the coolant reservoir.
  17. Raise the car again if needed, and reconnect the lower radiator hose.
  18. On an automatic, remove the plugs and reconnect the transmission fluid cooler hoses.
  19. Plug in the coolant temperature sensor connector, and the air conditioning compressor clutch connector if equipped. Reattach their harness clamps.
  20. Lower the car. Refill the radiator with the correct coolant, and bleed the air out per your fill procedure.
  21. Clean up any spilled coolant right away.
  22. Start the engine, let it reach normal operating temperature, and check every connection for leaks.

Replace the water pump

Always let the engine cool completely before you drain coolant. Never open the cooling system while the engine is warm or running. Always wear eye protection when draining or refilling coolant. Never work under a car held up only by a jack; always use jack stands.

  1. Make sure the engine is completely cool before you start.
  2. Drain the engine coolant into a clean drain pan.
  3. Raise the car and support it on jack stands, then remove the right front wheel.
  4. Remove the splash shield, the plastic panel that covers the underside of the wheel well.
  5. Loosen the water pump pulley's mounting bolts, then remove the drive belt.
  6. Remove the water pump pulley's mounting bolts and the pulley itself.
  7. Remove the water pump's mounting bolts, and lift the old pump away from the engine block.
  8. Clean the old gasket material off the mounting surface.
  9. Fit the new pump with a new gasket or O-ring, following its instructions.
  10. Install the pump's mounting bolts and torque them per the new pump's instructions. We could not confirm an exact Honda torque number for this pass; check Appendix C for any later confirmation.
  11. Install the water pump pulley, and loosely fit its mounting bolts.
  12. Install the drive belt, then tighten the pulley's mounting bolts fully.
  13. Install the splash shield and the right front wheel, then lower the car.
  14. Refill the radiator with the correct coolant, and bleed the air out per your fill procedure.
  15. Clean up any spilled coolant right away.
  16. Start the engine, let it reach normal operating temperature, and check around the pump for leaks.

What can go wrong

Opening any part of the cooling system while the engine is still warm can spray scalding coolant on your skin. On the thermostat job, missing the liquid gasket's short install window lets it skin over before the housing is bolted down. Installing the thermostat backwards can keep the engine from warming up correctly, so check the old part's orientation before you throw it away. On the radiator job, forgetting to plug a disconnected transmission fluid cooler line lets dirt into the transmission's cooling circuit. Skipping the bleed-air step afterward can cause a false overheating reading on a later drive. On the water pump, do not mistake a small, normal amount of weeping at the pump's bleed holes for proof the old pump had failed. A healthy pump can show a trace of this. Judge a water pump by whether its pulley turns freely or by an active leak, not by a trace of weeping alone.

Quick check

  1. What job does each of the thermostat, the radiator, and the water pump do in the cooling system?
  2. Why must the engine be completely cool before you start any of these three jobs?
  3. What makes the water pump on this car easier to reach than on many other cars?
  4. What extra part must you disconnect and plug on an automatic transmission car during a radiator swap?
  5. Why should you not run the engine right after installing a new thermostat housing?

Chapter 43Starter, alternator, ignition switch

What you will learn

  • What the starter, the alternator, and the ignition switch each do
  • How to replace the starter yourself, step by step
  • What a shop will do to replace the alternator, and why this book sends that job to a shop
  • What a shop will do to replace the ignition switch, and why it is really two different jobs
  • What can go wrong on each job, including battery-cable safety

The short version

The starter spins your engine to life when you turn the key or press the start button. The alternator charges your battery and powers your electrical system while the engine runs. The ignition switch is the part the key turns to control power to the starter and the rest of the car. The starter is a doable weekend job. The alternator and the ignition switch both go to a shop in this book, for two different reasons explained below.

The full explanation

On your Fit, the starter sits low on the driver's side of the engine. This book could not find a plain factory guide for removing and installing the starter on this car. The steps below come from real owners of this exact car instead. Reaching the starter's top bolt is widely reported as the hardest part of the job.

The alternator on your Fit sits in a hard spot. You must remove the intake manifold, the part that feeds air into the engine, just to reach it. That makes this a bigger job than a simple bolt-off swap. It also raises the chance of a vacuum leak or a bad gasket if it is not done with care. This book sends the alternator job to a shop for that reason.

The ignition switch is really two different jobs hiding under one name. One job replaces only the electrical switch behind the steering column covers. That is a moderate job for a confident home mechanic with basic tools. The other job replaces the whole lock cylinder, the mechanical part the key barrel turns inside. That job needs drilling out security shear bolts. These are bolts made to snap off flush once tightened, so a thief cannot unbolt the lock. On many Hondas, the job also needs reprogramming the immobilizer system to accept a new key. The immobilizer is the security system that only lets the engine start with a key it recognizes. Chapter 7 covers its dashboard light. A "hard to turn" key can be caused by either problem. Choosing the wrong repair wastes time and money. This book sends the whole ignition switch job to a shop so it can be diagnosed correctly first.

This car has no ignition-switch recall on record. This is wear-and-tear repair work, not a safety recall.

Step by step

Replace the starter

Always take off the negative battery terminal first, then the positive terminal, before you start this job. Always wear eye protection. Never let a wrench touch a positive battery or starter terminal and any grounded metal at the same time. This can cause a dangerous short and a burn. Never work under a car held up only by a jack. Always use jack stands.

  1. Disconnect the negative battery terminal, then the positive terminal.
  2. Remove the two 10 mm bolts holding the wiring harness to the intake manifold.
  3. Remove the 10 mm bolt for the dipstick tube bracket.
  4. Remove the 10 mm bolt on the starter that holds a nearby hose bracket.
  5. Detach the radiator hose holder from the starter bracket.
  6. Disconnect the electrical connectors from the starter's "S" (signal) and "B" (battery) terminals.
  7. Remove the starter's mounting bolts. Use a long extension and a swivel joint on the hard-to-reach top bolt.
  8. Guide the starter out from below the car, or through the engine bay, whichever your access allows.
  9. Compare the new starter to the old one to confirm it matches before you install it.
  10. Guide the new starter into place, and install its mounting bolts. We could not confirm an exact Honda torque number for this pass; follow your new starter's own instructions.
  11. Reconnect the electrical connectors to the "S" and "B" terminals.
  12. Reattach the radiator hose holder to the starter bracket.
  13. Reinstall the dipstick tube bracket bolt and the two wiring harness bolts at the intake manifold.
  14. Reconnect the positive battery terminal, then the negative terminal.
  15. Start the car to confirm the new starter cranks the engine smoothly.

What the shop will do: alternator

Even if a shop does this job, know what to ask about first. Always disconnect the battery before this job. The alternator's output terminal stays electrically live even with the key off. Always wear eye protection if you help with any part of this job. Never skip a post-repair check of the charging voltage before you call the job done.

  • Disconnect the battery, following the proper terminal disconnection order.
  • Remove the drive belt, then remove the intake manifold to reach the alternator.
  • Disconnect the alternator's electrical connector and its positive cable, then unbolt and remove the alternator.
  • Install the new alternator, and torque its mounting bolts to 17 lbf-ft (24 N-m).
  • Reconnect the alternator's connector and positive cable. Make sure the cable's ring terminal has its crimped side facing away from the alternator, and torque the cable's terminal bolt to 6 lbf-ft (8 N-m).
  • Reinstall the intake manifold, using a new gasket if the old one is damaged, reinstall the drive belt, and reconnect the battery.
  • Start the engine and confirm the charging system light goes out, and the battery charges normally.

What the shop will do: ignition switch

Even if a shop does this job, know what to ask about first. Always disconnect the battery before any steering-column work. The airbag system's wiring runs close to the column. Never let anyone bypass the immobilizer system instead of properly reprogramming a new key. A bypassed immobilizer is a theft and safety risk. Always confirm with the shop that the airbag wiring near the column was not disturbed. If it was moved, confirm it was put back correctly.

  • Diagnose whether the problem is the electrical switch, meaning the key turns but the car does not respond correctly. Or it could be the mechanical lock cylinder, meaning the key will not turn or turns roughly.
  • If it is the electrical switch only: remove the plastic covers around the steering column, unplug and unbolt the old switch, and install the new one.
  • If it is the lock cylinder or the full ignition lock housing: remove the steering column covers, and drill out the security shear bolts holding the housing. Remove the old housing and install a new assembly. This part of the job is reported at around 2 hours of labor.
  • If a new lock cylinder or an immobilizer-linked part is installed, program the new key or keys to the immobilizer system so the engine will start.
  • Test that the key turns smoothly through LOCK, ACCESSORY, ON, and START, and that the car starts reliably afterward.

What can go wrong

On the starter job, connecting the battery terminals in the wrong order is the most common mistake. Always take the negative terminal off first, and put it back on last. Forcing the starter's top bolt without a swivel joint can strip the bolt or its bracket. Mixing up the "S" and "B" connectors can keep the new starter from working right. On the alternator job, hooking up the positive cable with its ring terminal facing the wrong way is a common error. Skipping a post-job charging-voltage check is another. Either mistake can hide a problem until the battery goes flat later. On the ignition switch, paying for the wrong sub-repair wastes money. This can mean fixing the electrical switch instead of the lock cylinder, or the other way around, if the shop has not found the real problem first. Forgetting to reprogram the immobilizer for a new key can leave the car unable to start, even after a correct mechanical repair.

Quick check

  1. What does each of the starter, the alternator, and the ignition switch do?
  2. Why does reaching the starter's top mounting bolt matter so much on this job?
  3. Why does this book send the alternator job to a shop instead of giving full DIY steps?
  4. What are the two different jobs that can both be called "replacing the ignition switch"?
  5. Why is disconnecting the battery in the correct order important before working on any of these three parts?

Chapter 44Exhaust and O2 sensors

What you will learn

  • What an oxygen sensor does, and why your Fit has two of them
  • How to replace an upstream or downstream oxygen sensor
  • How to replace a rusted or broken section of exhaust pipe or muffler
  • The warning signs that a job is too big for your driveway
  • What to check before you call either repair finished

The short version

Your Fit's exhaust system carries hot gas away from the engine. Two small sensors along the way check the oxygen level in that gas. You can replace either sensor, or a rusted section of pipe or muffler, using basic tools. Always let the exhaust cool first, since these parts stay dangerously hot long after you shut off the engine.

The full explanation

What an oxygen sensor does

An oxygen sensor is a small part that threads into your exhaust pipe. It measures how much oxygen is left in the exhaust gas. Your Fit's engine has one exhaust bank, and two of these sensors sit along it.

The upstream sensor sits closer to the engine. It is also called the primary sensor, or the air-fuel-ratio sensor. It threads into the exhaust manifold, the metal pipe that collects gas straight from the engine's cylinders. The downstream sensor sits farther back, on the catalytic converter. A catalytic converter is a part that cleans pollutants out of the exhaust gas. The downstream sensor watches how well that converter is working.

These two sensors connect to check-engine codes covered in Chapter 35. Code P0135 flags a problem with the upstream sensor's heater. Code P0141 flags the same kind of problem on the downstream sensor. A third code, P0420, means the computer thinks your catalytic converter is not cleaning the exhaust well enough. That code can also show up because of a bad upstream sensor or an engine misfire. Always rule those two causes out before you blame the converter itself.

Reaching the downstream sensor's electrical connector is harder than reaching the upstream one. On this car, you likely need to remove the center console from inside the car first. That is why this book rates the upstream sensor job easier than the downstream one, even though the two sensors look similar.

Exhaust pipes and mufflers

Your exhaust system is a chain of pipe sections and a muffler. These parts join at flanges, which are flat joints held together with bolts. Rubber hangers hold the whole system up under the car. Rust and road salt attack these flanges and hangers over the years. Eventually a section rusts through, or a joint seizes shut. This book could not find a Fit-specific, bolt-by-bolt guide for this exact car's exhaust system. The steps below use standard technique for this type of car and system. They are not a Honda-confirmed script written for the 2008 Fit specifically.

Step by step

Replacing an oxygen (O2) sensor

What you need: - An oxygen-sensor socket (a socket with a slot cut for the sensor's wire), or a wrench that fits - Penetrating oil, for a rusted sensor - Anti-seize compound made for oxygen sensors — a paste that keeps metal parts from rusting together - Jack and 2 jack stands, if you work from underneath - Basic interior trim tools, only if you replace the downstream sensor and need to remove the center console - A new sensor made for the exact position you are replacing. Upstream and downstream sensors are different parts. This book could not confirm a part number this pass. Ask a Honda parts counter, or check Appendix F.

Safety warnings: - Always let the exhaust system cool completely before you touch it. It stays dangerously hot long after the engine shuts off. - Always wear eye protection, especially when you use penetrating oil on a rusted sensor. - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. - Do not overtighten a new sensor. Overtightening can crack the sensor body.

Steps: 1. Make sure the exhaust system is completely cool before you start. 2. Decide whether you are replacing the upstream sensor or the downstream sensor. 3. Raise the car and support it on jack stands, if you need to work from underneath. 4. Disconnect the sensor's electrical connector. For the downstream sensor, you may need to remove the center console first. 5. Spray penetrating oil on the sensor's threads if it looks rusted. Let it sit a few minutes. 6. Use your oxygen-sensor socket or wrench to loosen and remove the old sensor. 7. Compare the new sensor's connector and threads to the old one, to confirm they match. 8. Thread the new sensor in by hand first, so you do not cross the threads. 9. Tighten it with the socket or wrench until snug. Do not overtighten it. 10. Reconnect the sensor's electrical connector. 11. Reinstall the center console, if you removed it for the downstream sensor. 12. Lower the car, if you raised it. 13. If you have a scan tool, clear any stored check-engine codes tied to the sensor. Then take a test drive.

How you know it worked: The check-engine light stays off for the code you were chasing, after a normal test drive. If you have a scan tool, the sensor's reading changes as the engine runs, instead of staying flat.

Replacing an exhaust section or muffler

What you need: - Jack and 2 jack stands - Penetrating oil - A wrench and socket set for the flange bolts or clamps - A cutting tool, such as a rotary tool or a hacksaw, for a seized joint - A rubber mallet, to help free a rusted flange - New gaskets (thin seals that stop exhaust leaks) and new self-locking nuts (nuts with a built-in insert that resists loosening on its own) for every flange joint you open. Honda's own guidance for this car calls for fresh ones, not reused ones. - New rubber hangers, for any that are cracked or torn - The replacement pipe, muffler, or section itself. This book could not confirm a part number this pass. Check with a Honda parts counter, a muffler shop, or Appendix F.

Safety warnings: - Always let the exhaust system cool completely before you touch it. - Always wear eye protection, especially when you use penetrating oil or a cutting tool on rusted fasteners. - Do not work under a car held up only by a jack. Use jack stands. - Do not cut near the fuel tank, fuel lines, or brake lines. Know what sits above and around a spot before you cut it. - Always support a heavy section, like the muffler, before you loosen its last hanger. It should not swing down and hit you or the ground.

Steps: 1. Make sure the exhaust system is completely cool before you start. 2. Raise the car and support it on jack stands. 3. Spray penetrating oil on every fastener and hanger rubber you plan to disturb. Let it sit. 4. Support the section you are removing with a jack, a strap, or a helper, before you loosen its last fastener. 5. Separate the flange joint at each end of the section. Use a rubber mallet if it is rusted tight. 6. If a clamp or bolt is fully seized, cut it carefully rather than force it and damage the pipe around it. 7. Remove the old section, working it free of its rubber hangers. 8. Compare the new section to the old one, to confirm it matches, before you install it. 9. Fit new rubber hangers if the old ones are cracked or torn. 10. Position the new section and hang it loosely on its rubber mounts before you tighten anything. 11. Fit new gaskets at each flange joint you opened. 12. Install new self-locking nuts at those flange joints. 13. Tighten all the flange fasteners, with the system hanging naturally, not twisted or under strain. 14. Lower the car and start the engine. Listen and look for any exhaust leak or rattle.

How you know it worked: There is no hissing, ticking, or rattling from the exhaust while the engine idles. There is no visible leak at any joint. The exhaust hangs evenly, without touching the body or the ground.

What can go wrong

Oxygen sensor mistakes

  • Confusing the upstream and downstream sensor, and ordering the wrong part.
  • Overtightening the new sensor and cracking its body.
  • Forcing a rusted sensor without penetrating oil, and snapping it off inside the exhaust manifold or converter.
  • Not clearing the old fault code. The check-engine light can stay on even after a correct repair, until enough clean drive cycles pass.

Exhaust and muffler mistakes

  • Reusing old gaskets or nuts at a flange joint instead of new ones, which can cause a leak later.
  • Fully tightening one flange before the whole section is loosely hung, which can twist and stress the joints.
  • Cutting a stuck fastener without checking what sits directly behind or above it first.
  • Not supporting a heavy section before you remove its last hanger.

Quick check

  1. What does an oxygen sensor measure, and where are your Fit's two sensors located?
  2. Why should you rule out a bad upstream sensor or a misfire before you blame the catalytic converter for a P0420 code?
  3. Name two safety warnings you must follow before working on either sensor or the exhaust pipe.
  4. What should you do differently with an exhaust flange joint you open, compared to reusing the old parts?
  5. How do you know an oxygen sensor swap or an exhaust repair actually worked?

Chapter 45Interior fixes: door handles, window regulators, seat repair

What you will learn

  • How to replace a door handle
  • How to replace a window regulator, the mechanism that raises and lowers your window
  • Why one detail in the window regulator job is not confirmed for your exact car, and what to do about it
  • How to repair a torn or damaged seat cover
  • What can go wrong in each of these three jobs

The short version

You can replace a door handle, a window regulator, or a torn seat cover with basic tools and some patience. Each job means taking a door panel or a seat apart, so work carefully and keep track of every clip and bolt. For the window regulator, one detail below comes from a different Honda Fit than yours, and this book says so plainly instead of guessing.

The full explanation

Door handles

A door handle connects to a metal rod inside the door. Pulling the handle pulls the rod, which opens the latch. The steps below come from a real repair guide for this car's rear door outer handle. The front door handle uses a very similar design. The door panel comes off, the rod disconnects, and the handle pries out with a trim tool. A trim tool is a flat plastic tool made to remove panels without scratching them. Treat the exact bolt count as confirmed for the rear door, and only reasonably similar, not confirmed, for the front.

Window regulators, and an honest gap in this book's research

A window regulator is the mechanism inside your door that raises and lowers the glass. It usually has a small electric motor attached.

Here is where this book has to be honest with you. The most detailed, bolt-by-bolt window regulator guide this project could find was written for a 2011 Honda Fit. That is a later, different-generation Fit than yours, built between 2009 and 2013. Your 2008 Fit is a different car under the skin, even though it shares a name and looks similar. This book will not treat a detail from that different car as a confirmed fact about yours.

Here is the good news. A real 2007 Fit owner reports replacing a window regulator in their own driveway. That confirms the job itself works on your exact generation of car. Only one detail stays unconfirmed: the exact number of bolts holding the regulator in the door. The 2011-Fit guide reports roughly two bolts near the top and three near the bottom. Nobody has confirmed that exact count on a 2008 Fit. Count your own car's bolts as you go, rather than assuming this number in advance.

Seat repair

A torn seat cover is a general repair, not something unique to your Fit. No car-specific guide exists for patching a Fit seat, because the technique is the same on almost any car. The steps below use standard upholstery technique — the skill of repairing fabric, vinyl, or leather covers.

Step by step

Replacing a door handle

What you need: - A door or trim panel removal tool kit (plastic pry tools), so you do not scratch the door or break plastic clips - A clip remover tool, for the rod fastener - A basic screwdriver and socket set - Protective tape, to protect the paint near the handle while you pry - A new door handle. Confirm front vs. rear and driver vs. passenger side before you order one. This book could not confirm a part number this pass. Ask a Honda parts counter, or check Appendix F. - A replacement plastic access-cover cap, in case the old one cracks or tears during removal

Safety warnings: - Always wear gloves. Sharp metal edges and clips inside the door can cut your hands. - Do not pry directly against painted door metal without protective tape. A slipped trim tool can scratch the paint. - Always raise the window glass fully before you work inside the door, so it cannot drop while the panel is off.

Steps: 1. Remove the door panel to reach the inside of the door. 2. Raise the window glass fully before you continue. 3. If the door has power locks, disconnect the power door lock actuator connector (the small motor that locks and unlocks the door) and detach its harness clip. 4. Remove the rear plug cap and the plastic access cover, keeping them intact if you can. 5. Remove the maintenance caps that cover the handle's fasteners. 6. Detach the rod fastener with a clip remover, then disconnect the outer handle rod from the handle. 7. Remove the handle's mounting bolts. 8. Apply protective tape to the door around the handle, to protect the paint. 9. Pry the outer handle out with your trim tool, to release its hook, then remove it. 10. Compare the new handle to the old one, to confirm it matches, before you install it. 11. Fit the new handle into its opening and press it in until the hook engages. 12. Reinstall the mounting bolts. 13. Reconnect the outer handle rod and refit its clip fastener. 14. Reinstall the maintenance caps, the plastic access cover, and the rear plug cap. 15. If your door has power locks, reconnect the power door lock actuator connector and its harness clip. 16. Reinstall the door panel. 17. Test the handle from outside. If your door has power locks, test that function too, before you call the job done.

How you know it worked: The door opens smoothly from outside with normal effort. The handle does not feel loose or gritty. Any power lock function on that door still works correctly.

Replacing a window regulator

What you need: - A door or trim panel removal tool kit (plastic pry tools) - A 10 mm socket wrench or nut driver. The exact bolt size and count are not independently confirmed for your car — see the note above. Confirm yours before you buy hardware. - A basic screwdriver set - A new window regulator, with or without a motor, depending on what failed, for the exact door and side you are fixing. This book could not confirm a part number this pass. Ask a Honda parts counter, or check Appendix F.

Safety warnings: - Always wear gloves. Broken window glass and sharp door-frame edges can cut your hands. - Do not remove the window glass while it is only partly supported. Make sure it cannot slide down and shatter while the door panel is off. - Always disconnect the window regulator motor's electrical connector before you handle the regulator. This avoids an accidental pinch if the motor still has power.

Steps: 1. Remove the door panel and the plastic watershield behind it. A watershield is a plastic sheet that keeps water out of the car's interior. 2. Move the window glass until its mounting fasteners are accessible. 3. Remove the glass's mounting fasteners, and carefully pull the glass out through the window slot. Support it the whole time. 4. Remove the bolts holding the old regulator to the door. A guide written for a later, different Fit generation reports roughly two bolts near the top and three near the bottom. Count your own car's bolts as you go, rather than assuming this number. 5. Disconnect the electrical connector for the window regulator motor. 6. Carry the old regulator out of the door. 7. Compare the new regulator to the old one, to confirm it matches, before you install it. 8. Place the new regulator inside the door and reconnect the motor's electrical connector. 9. Line up the regulator to its original position. 10. Reinstall the mounting bolts, tightening them evenly. 11. Reinstall the window glass into its mounting fasteners. 12. Reinstall the watershield and the door panel. 13. Test the window's full up-and-down travel, and its auto up or down function if it has one, before you call the job done.

How you know it worked: The window moves smoothly through its full range, with no grinding, binding, or unusual noise. It stops properly at both the fully up and fully down positions.

Repairing a torn or damaged seat

What you need: - A ratchet or wrench for the seat mounting bolts, if you need to remove the seat for better access - Scissors, a heat gun on a low setting or a hair dryer, and a stiff-bristle brush, for vinyl or leather repair - A sewing kit with heavy-duty upholstery thread, for a small fabric tear - Clean rags and isopropyl alcohol, to clean the area before any repair - A vinyl or leather repair kit (color-matched filler and grain paper), for a torn vinyl or leather seat - Matching seat fabric, or a patch kit, for a torn cloth seat - Foam replacement or extra padding, if the seat's foam is also crushed or torn underneath the cover

Safety warnings: - Always disconnect the seat's electrical connectors before you remove a seat from the car. This includes wiring for a power seat, a seat heater, or a seat-belt pretensioner (a device that tightens the belt suddenly in a crash). - Do not cut into or disturb any wiring or part you cannot identify near the seat frame. Some seats route side-impact airbag wiring through them. - Always let a repaired area cure fully, per your repair kit's instructions, before you sit on it.

Steps: 1. Clean the damaged area with a rag and isopropyl alcohol, and let it dry. 2. Decide whether you can do the repair with the seat in the car, or need to remove the seat for full access. 3. If you remove the seat, disconnect its electrical connectors first, then remove its mounting bolts and lift it out with a helper. 4. For a small fabric tear, stitch the edges together from the underside where you can, using heavy-duty thread. 5. Apply a fabric patch behind the tear for strength. 6. For a vinyl or leather tear, trim any loose or frayed edges. 7. Follow your repair kit's instructions to fill the tear with the color-matched compound. 8. Use the heat gun on a low setting, held well away from the surface, if your kit calls for heat-setting. 9. This step helps the vinyl or leather filler cure. 10. Press the grain paper from your kit onto the wet filler, to recreate the surface texture. 11. Peel it away once it cures, per the kit's instructions. 12. Blend the repaired area's color if needed, using your kit's color-matching guidance. 13. If you removed the seat, reinstall it and reconnect its electrical connectors and mounting bolts. 14. Let the repair cure fully before normal use, per your kit's stated time.

How you know it worked: The repaired area holds together under normal sitting and getting in and out. It does not visibly gap or peel once fully cured. Any seat electrical function still works normally if you removed the seat.

What can go wrong

Door handle mistakes

  • Prying without protective tape, and scratching the paint around the handle.
  • Losing or breaking a small plastic access cover or maintenance cap during removal.
  • Forgetting to raise the window fully before you remove the door panel, risking a dropped or damaged glass.
  • Not fully engaging the new handle's hook before you tighten the mounting bolts, leaving it loose.

Window regulator mistakes

  • Letting the glass slide down and shatter while it is unsupported, with the door panel off.
  • Reconnecting the motor while it still has power, risking a pinched finger if it activates unexpectedly.
  • Not reinstalling the watershield correctly, letting water into the door and, eventually, into the car's interior.
  • Assuming the bolt count from a different Fit generation's guide is correct, without checking your own door first.

Seat repair mistakes

  • Skipping the cleaning step, so the repair compound does not bond well to a dirty or oily surface.
  • Using too much heat too close to the vinyl, scorching it instead of curing the filler.
  • Disturbing seat wiring without labeling the connectors first, then reconnecting something incorrectly.
  • Sitting on the repair before it has fully cured.

Quick check

  1. What does a window regulator do, and where is it located?
  2. Why can't this book confirm the exact bolt count for your window regulator job?
  3. What evidence confirms the window regulator job itself is doable on a 2008 Fit, even without a confirmed bolt count?
  4. Name two safety steps you should follow before removing a car seat.
  5. What tells you a door handle repair or a seat repair actually worked?

Chapter 46Body: dents, rust, paint touch-up, headlight restoration

What you will learn

  • How to pull out a small, shallow dent
  • How to treat surface rust before it becomes a hole
  • How to touch up a paint chip or scratch
  • How to restore cloudy or yellowed headlight lenses
  • When a body repair is too big for your driveway, and needs a shop instead

The short version

You can fix a small dent, treat early rust, touch up a paint chip, and clear up hazy headlights with basic tools and some patience. Rust is the one job with a hard line. Treat it early yourself, but once rust eats all the way through the metal, that spot needs a shop, not a driveway fix. Headlight haze is different. It comes from sunlight, not rust, and almost anyone can fix it in under an hour.

The full explanation

Dents, rust, and paint: one small job that grows

This book covers three small cosmetic body jobs together, because you often do them one after another on the same spot. First a dent, then any rust the dent exposed, then a paint touch-up to finish it.

Chapter 38 already flagged a specific rust spot on first-generation Fits like yours. It shows up where the rear quarter panel meets the rear bumper cover. It also shows up along a seam where a plastic cover hides the join between the quarter panel, the rocker panel, and the underside of the car. That pocket collects road grit and moisture, and it is the first place rust tends to appear. This is more of a concern if you live somewhere that salts the roads in winter.

Surface rust is rust that sits only on top of the metal, not through it. Treating it is a beginner job: you clean it, treat it with a rust converter (a chemical that changes rust into a paintable surface), then prime and paint over it. A hole in the metal is a different story. Fixing a rust hole means cutting out the bad metal and welding in a new patch, which is not a beginner task. Covering a rust hole with filler alone might look fine for a while, but it does not stop the rust spreading underneath. A repair shop estimate for early surface or bubble rust treatment on this kind of spot typically runs $150 to $900. A real hole patch or panel replacement typically runs $800 to $2,500.

For paint, your touch-up color has to match your exact car. Your doorjamb certification label carries your car's paint code. This book could not independently confirm the exact paint code numbers for every 2008 Fit color. Treat any code you find online as a lead to check, not a confirmed fact. Always check it against your own doorjamb label first.

Headlight haze

Modern headlight covers are plastic, not glass, and plastic clouds over time from years of sunlight. Ultraviolet light, the part of sunlight that also causes sunburn, slowly breaks down the plastic's clear outer layer. This happens to nearly every car with plastic headlight covers, not only the Fit. So this job uses a general technique, rather than a Fit-specific guide.

Step by step

Fixing a small dent

What you need: - A plunger, or a dedicated dent-pulling tool, for a small, shallow dent in a flat panel

Safety warnings: - Always wear gloves and eye protection when you sand, wire-brush, or use chemical rust products, later in this same job. - Do not sand through to bare metal over a large area without protecting it with primer right away. Bare metal left exposed starts rusting again quickly.

Steps: 1. Clean the dented area with soap and water, then dry it. 2. For a shallow dent on a flat panel, use a plunger with some water on the rim, to create suction. 3. Pull outward in short, firm pulls. 4. For a dent a plunger cannot pull, a dedicated dent-puller kit with glue tabs and a slide hammer can help. 5. This kind of kit can fix many minor dents without damaging the paint. 6. Check the repaired area's paint for cracking. A cracked clear coat over a pulled dent may still need a touch-up.

Treating surface rust

What you need: - 220 to 600 grit sandpaper, to feather paint edges (sand them into a smooth, gradual taper instead of a hard ridge) and prep the rust spot - A wire brush, or a drill-mounted rust-removal wheel - Masking tape and paper, to protect the surrounding paint - A rust converter or inhibitor product, to treat the rust before you paint over it - Rust-inhibiting primer

Safety warnings: - Always wear gloves and eye protection when you sand, wire-brush, or use a chemical rust converter. - Do not use rust converter or primer in a closed, unventilated space. Work outdoors, or with good airflow. - Always let each layer — converter, primer, paint, and clear coat — dry or cure fully before you add the next one.

Steps: 1. Clean the rusted area with soap and water, then dry it fully. 2. Sand or wire-brush the rust down to a sound, unrusted surface. Feather the edges into the good paint around it. 3. Wipe away all the dust, then apply rust converter or inhibitor, following its instructions. 4. Once the converter has cured, apply rust-inhibiting primer over the treated area. 5. If the rust has already progressed to a hole in the metal, stop here. 6. Treat a rust hole as a job for a shop instead. See the note above.

Touching up paint

What you need: - Masking tape and paper - Fine sandpaper, 600 grit or finer - A small foam applicator or fine artist's brush - Touch-up paint matched to your car's paint code, found on your doorjamb certification label. Verify any code you find online against your own label first. - Clear coat, matched to your paint type, for the final layer over the touch-up paint

Safety warnings: - Always wear gloves and eye protection during sanding. - Do not skip letting each coat dry fully before you add the next one.

Steps: 1. Mask off the area around the chip, scratch, or repaired dent or rust spot with tape and paper. 2. If there is a rough edge, lightly sand the immediate area with fine sandpaper, then wipe it clean. 3. Apply touch-up paint in thin coats with a fine brush or applicator. Let each coat dry before the next, rather than applying one thick coat. 4. Once the paint has cured per its label, apply clear coat in thin coats over the repaired area. 5. Remove the masking tape and paper once everything has cured. 6. Wax the area once the repair is fully cured, blending it into the surrounding panel.

How you know it worked: The dent is flat enough that you cannot see it easily from a normal viewing distance. There is no more active rust bleeding through the paint at the treated spot. The touch-up paint is close enough in color and gloss that it does not stand out up close.

Restoring cloudy or yellowed headlights

What you need: - A headlight restoration kit. Most kits include sandpaper of several grits, a polishing compound, and applicator pads. - Masking tape, to protect the surrounding paint - A clean microfiber cloth - A spray bottle with water, to keep the sandpaper wet, if your kit calls for wet-sanding - A UV-protectant sealant, if your kit includes one

Safety warnings: - Do not attempt this job in direct, hot sunlight. The plastic, and any chemical products you use, can behave unpredictably in high heat. - Always mask off the paint around each headlight before you sand or polish, to avoid scratching it. - Do not use harsh solvents not meant for automotive plastic. They can cloud or crack the lens further.

Steps: 1. Wash both headlights with soap and water, then dry them. 2. Mask off the paint and trim around each headlight with tape. 3. Starting with your kit's coarsest sandpaper, wet-sand the lens in one direction, keeping the surface wet the whole time. 4. Rinse the lens and check your progress. The surface will look hazy white during sanding — this is normal and expected. 5. Move to the next-finer grit in your kit, and repeat the wet-sanding, rinsing between grits. 6. Continue through every grit your kit provides, finishing on the finest one. 7. Rinse and dry the lens fully. 8. Apply the polishing compound with an applicator pad, working it in small circles until the lens looks clear. 9. Wipe off any leftover polish residue with a clean microfiber cloth. 10. If your kit includes a UV-protectant sealant, apply it per the kit's instructions, to help slow future yellowing. 11. Remove the masking tape. 12. Repeat steps 2 through 11 on the other headlight.

How you know it worked: Both headlight lenses look clear instead of cloudy or yellowed. A nighttime check shows noticeably brighter, more evenly spread light on the road or a wall, compared to before.

What can go wrong

Dent, rust, and paint mistakes

  • Painting directly over untreated rust. The rust keeps spreading invisibly under the new paint.
  • Applying touch-up paint in one thick coat, which causes it to run or look lumpy.
  • Skipping the clear coat step, leaving the touch-up looking dull or mismatched in gloss.
  • Trying to patch a rust hole with filler alone, instead of cutting out the rusted metal and welding in new metal. Filler alone does not fix the underlying problem.

Headlight restoration mistakes

  • Skipping a grit step in the sanding sequence, leaving fine scratches the polish cannot remove.
  • Letting the sandpaper run dry, which can gouge the plastic instead of smoothing it.
  • Forgetting to mask the surrounding paint, and scratching it with sandpaper or polish.
  • Skipping the UV-protectant sealant step, if your kit includes one, and having the haze return sooner than expected.

Quick check

  1. Where does Chapter 38 say rust tends to start first on a first-generation Fit like yours?
  2. What is the difference between surface rust and a rust hole, and why does that difference matter for a DIY repair?
  3. Where do you find your car's exact paint code, and why should you double-check any code you find online?
  4. What causes headlight lenses to turn cloudy or yellow over time?
  5. How do you know a headlight restoration job actually worked?

Part VIIISouping It Up

Chapter 47Modding Philosophy — Goals, Budget, and the Law

What you will learn

  • Why you should set a clear goal before you buy any part
  • What federal law says about your car's emissions equipment
  • What a CARB Executive Order (EO) is, and how to look one up
  • How modifying your Fit can affect your insurance and your warranty
  • What this book will never tell you to do

The short version

Before you spend money, decide what you want your Fit to do better, and set a budget you will actually keep. Federal law lets you modify your car, but it does not let you remove or defeat any part built to control exhaust emissions. Tell your insurance company about every modification you make, because an untold modification can leave you paying the full bill yourself.

The full explanation

Set a goal before you set a budget

Modding means changing your car from how the factory built it, usually to improve how it handles, stops, sounds, or looks. Before you buy a single part, decide what you actually want. A car built for autocross needs different parts than a car built to look sharp on the street. Autocross is a timed event held in a parking lot or on an airport lot. A car built for daily driving to school needs different parts than either one.

Write your goal down in one sentence. Then set a budget for the whole project, not only the first part. Mods add up fast. A coilover kit, an adjustable suspension upgrade covered fully in Chapter 50, is only useful if you can also afford an alignment afterward. Spend on baseline items first, covered in Chapter 48, before you spend on power or looks.

The law: what "tampering" means

The Clean Air Act is the federal law that sets emissions rules for every car sold in the United States, including your Fit. It makes two separate things illegal. First, it is illegal for anyone to manufacture, sell, or install a part built mainly to bypass, defeat, or disable a device that controls your car's exhaust emissions. That kind of part is called a defeat device, and this rule targets whoever makes, sells, or installs it, not only the driver.

Second, a separate part of the same law applies directly to you, the owner. It makes it illegal to knowingly remove or disable any emissions device on your own car after you bought it. This second rule is the one that applies to you if you do the work yourself.

Both rules apply everywhere in the United States, not only in states with strict smog laws. The federal government, through the EPA (Environmental Protection Agency), enforces them nationwide. CARB, the California Air Resources Board, adds its own state-level rules on top of the federal ones, mainly for cars titled or registered in California. Chapter 17 explains how to check your own state's inspection and emissions rules.

Here is the good news: EPA does not chase down every driver who installs an aftermarket intake or exhaust, meaning a part made by a company other than Honda. EPA uses what it calls "enforcement discretion," and it focuses on parts that actually raise emissions. EPA generally leaves a part alone if you can show a "reasonable basis" that it will not hurt emissions, proven one of three ways. The part can be identical in design and function to the one it replaces. The modified car can still pass the same emissions tests it was certified with. Or the part can carry a CARB Executive Order that covers your exact vehicle.

The penalties for breaking these rules are real money, not a scare tactic. As of the date on EPA's own fact sheet, a manufacturer or dealer can be fined up to $48,192 per violating vehicle or part. Any other person, meaning you, can be fined up to $4,819 per violating vehicle or part. Faking or disabling emissions-related monitoring equipment, like sensors your check-engine light relies on, is a separate criminal violation on top of that fine.

CARB Executive Orders: the exemption a shop can check

A CARB Executive Order (EO) is a certificate California issues for one specific aftermarket part, after CARB's own engineers confirm the part does not raise emissions. An EO exempts that exact part, on that exact vehicle, from California's own anti-tampering law. A smog-check station or a CARB inspector can look up the EO number to confirm it is real.

Here is the part every reader of this book must remember. One EO does not automatically cover every car the part might physically bolt onto. An EO is tied to a specific engine family and a specific model year. An EO written for the second-generation 2009-2014 Fit, chassis code GE8, does not automatically cover your 2007-2008 Fit, chassis code GD3, even if the part looks identical on the shelf. Always confirm the EO you are reading names your exact model year before you trust it. Chapter 50 walks through this exact year-confusion trap using real suspension parts, so you can see how it plays out with actual products.

You can look up a real EO yourself at CARB's public tool, https://ww2.arb.ca.gov/aftermarket-parts-lookup. It offers five search categories, and "Performance & Add-on Parts" is the one you want for an intake, header, or exhaust. That category page leads into CARB's aftermarket-parts search tool. Outside descriptions say you can search it by EO number, part number, or manufacturer, though this book could not fully confirm every field of that tool works as described.

An EO also matters outside California. EPA itself lists "a CARB EO covering the same part" and vehicle as one of the three accepted ways to pass its own federal reasonable-basis test. A correctly matched EO is useful evidence anywhere in the country, not only in California.

Warranties and insurance: what new modders forget

EPA's own fact sheet states plainly that tampering "can void manufacturer warranties and insurance agreements." That single sentence connects the legal rules above to two very practical costs.

A standard car insurance policy is priced around your car's factory configuration. A turbocharger is an engine power upgrade, covered fully in Chapter 54. Any change not made at the factory, whether a suspension part, a turbocharger, custom paint, or a stereo system, counts as a modification for insurance purposes. Insurance companies generally will not automatically pay full value for modified parts. To get that coverage, you typically need an added policy endorsement, meaning an add-on to your existing policy, sometimes called "custom parts and equipment coverage." That coverage only works on top of comprehensive and collision coverage you already carry.

If you add parts and never tell your insurance company, the company may refuse to cover those parts. You then become responsible for repairing or replacing them yourself, even though you pay for insurance every month. Call your insurer before you install anything, not after something breaks.

Not every modification raises your rate. Performance and appearance parts, like a bigger engine output or expensive aftermarket wheels, usually raise cost. They raise the car's value, and they often cost more to repair than factory parts. Safety and security additions, like an alarm, a backup camera, or parking sensors, can sometimes earn a discount. This is not guaranteed, though, since some of that added technology is itself expensive to repair.

What this book will never tell you to do

This book never tells you to skip a safety step, and it never tells you to remove or disable emissions equipment for more performance. When a mod could hurt you or someone else, this book says so before it tells you how to do it. If a part looks too good to be true, like a free power gain with no downside, check its real fitment and its real legal status before you buy it. Chapter 50 teaches you exactly how to do that check yourself.

Quick check

  1. What is a "defeat device," and who can the federal defeat-device rule punish?
  2. Name the three ways EPA says an aftermarket part can pass its "reasonable basis" test.
  3. Why must you confirm a CARB EO names your exact model year before you trust it?
  4. What can happen if you install a modification and never tell your insurance company?
  5. Give one example of a modification that can sometimes lower your insurance cost, and explain why it does not always work that way.

Chapter 48Stage 0 — Get Your Baseline Right First

What you will learn

  • What "Stage 0" means, and why it comes before any performance part
  • Why fresh fluids matter before you add stress to your Fit
  • Why good tires and good brakes matter more than any bolt-on part
  • Why this book cannot hand you an alignment procedure, and what to do instead

The short version

Before you buy a single performance part, make sure your Fit's basic maintenance is already current. Stage 0 means fresh oil, tires in good shape, and brakes that already work correctly. Stage 0 costs less than most single mods, and it protects every mod you add after it.

The full explanation

What "Stage 0" means

Most modding guides number their steps: Stage 1, Stage 2, and so on, from mild to wild. Stage 0 is the step before all of those. It means bringing your Fit's existing maintenance up to date before you spend a dollar on power, handling, or looks. A stiffer suspension on old, worn shocks will not behave the way a review video shows. A grippier tire on a car with a slipping alignment will wear out fast and unevenly.

This chapter is short by design. Every Stage 0 item already has a full procedure card written in an earlier chapter of this book. This chapter tells you which chapter to open and why that item matters before you mod, instead of repeating steps you can already find there.

Fresh fluids

The first Stage 0 item is fresh engine oil and a fresh filter. Old oil has already picked up dirt and lost some of its ability to protect your engine. A car that will work harder, longer, or hotter than normal deserves oil that is not already tired. Chapter 24 covers the full oil-change procedure, including the tools, the drain-bolt torque spec, and every safety warning. Open Chapter 24 and follow its steps rather than guessing at your own order.

Good tires

The second Stage 0 item is good tires: correct, even air pressure, and no uneven wear from a rotation you skipped. This is a check, not a rebuild. Chapter 26 already covers how to check and set your tire pressure, and how to rotate your tires so they wear evenly. Read Chapter 26 and confirm both are already current before you spend money on new wheels or tires, which Chapter 49 covers next.

Stage 0 is not about buying new tires yet. It is about confirming the tires you already have are properly maintained, so any new tire or wheel choice starts from a clean baseline instead of hiding an existing problem.

Good brakes

The third Stage 0 item is good brakes. This means pads or shoes and fluid that are already in solid condition before you add track driving or spirited street driving to the mix. Your Fit's front brakes use pads and rotors, and its rear brakes use shoes inside a drum, which is a different design with different parts and a different repair. Chapter 27 covers inspecting the front pads through the wheel, replacing the front pads, replacing the rear shoes, and bleeding the brake fluid. Work through Chapter 27's checks before you plan any brake upgrade in Chapter 51.

Alignment: the one gap in this book

Here is where this book has to be honest with you instead of guessing. Alignment is the process of adjusting your wheels' angles, camber, caster, and toe, so your car drives straight, handles predictably, and wears its tires evenly. This book does not include a step-by-step alignment procedure. We could not confirm a home alignment procedure for the 2008 Fit in this book's research. Check your owner's manual, and plan on a shop visit instead.

There is a practical reason for this gap. Alignment normally needs an alignment rack, a specialized shop machine that measures your wheel angles precisely and then adjusts them. This is not a typical home-garage job the way an oil change or a brake-pad swap is. For Stage 0, the right move is to have a shop check your alignment, especially if the car has ever been in a curb strike or a minor accident. Once you get to Chapter 50, you will also want a fresh alignment any time you change springs, coilovers, or camber parts.

A simple Stage 0 checklist

  • Fresh engine oil and filter, per Chapter 24.
  • Correct, even tire pressure and a current tire rotation, per Chapter 26.
  • Healthy front brake pads, healthy rear brake shoes, and fresh brake fluid, per Chapter 27.
  • An alignment check at a shop, especially after any curb strike, accident, or suspension work.

Quick check

  1. What does "Stage 0" mean, and why does it come before power or handling mods?
  2. Which chapter should you open for the exact oil-change steps at Stage 0?
  3. Name the two tire-related checks that count toward Stage 0, and which chapter covers them.
  4. Why does "good brakes" mean checking two different repair types on your Fit, not only one?
  5. Why does this book send you to a shop for alignment instead of giving you home steps?

Chapter 49Tires and Wheels — Sizes, Offset, and Weight

What you will learn

  • What the numbers in a tire size and a wheel offset actually mean
  • Which wheel and tire sizes owners report fitting a GD3 without rubbing
  • Why a bigger wheel is not automatically a better wheel
  • What autocross class a stock Fit runs in, and how tire choice connects to it

The short version

Fitting a tire and wheel to your Fit depends on diameter, width, and offset working together, not any single number. Owners of first-generation Fits report that a 195/50R15 tire on a 15x6.5-to-7-inch wheel fits without rubbing. Bigger wheels and wider tires get progressively tighter, and they also add weight your engine has to turn, which can work against your handling goal instead of helping it.

The full explanation

Reading a tire size, and what "offset" means

A tire size like 195/50R15 has three parts. The 195 is the tire's width in millimeters. The 50 is the aspect ratio, the tire's sidewall height as a percentage of that width. The R15 means it is a radial tire built for a 15-inch wheel diameter.

Offset is a separate number, measured in millimeters, that describes how far the wheel's mounting face sits from its true centerline. A higher offset number pulls the tire in toward the engine and suspension. A lower offset number pushes the tire out toward the fender. Offset, tire width, and wheel diameter all work together to decide whether a tire rubs. A tire rubs when it touches the fender, a suspension part, or a body panel while turning or going over a bump.

Sizes owners report fitting without rubbing

Owners report that 195/50R15 on a 15x6.5-to-7-inch wheel, at +40 to +42mm offset, is the combination that fits a stock-height GD3 without rubbing. A 205/45R16 tire on a 16x7-inch wheel is described as more marginal. It only works at a minimum offset of +42mm, and some owners report rear rubbing, especially with passengers in the back seat.

Owners also report on a 205/50R15 tire, one size wider than the 195/50R15 baseline. The consensus from those reports is that a 205/50R15 fits without severe rubbing. It runs tighter than the baseline size, though, and it may need the fender lip rolled back or a lower ride height to stay fully clear at full steering lock.

One owner who autocrossed a GD3 for four to five seasons reports a staggered setup. Up front sit 225/45R15 tires on 15x7.5-inch wheels, and the rear runs narrower 205/50R15 tires. A staggered setup means the front and rear tires are different sizes on purpose. This particular combination is reported by only one source, so treat it as one experienced owner's setup, not a confirmed community consensus.

Weight: why bigger is not always better

A wheel and tire are part of your car's unsprung weight, the mass that your springs do not support, since it sits below them and moves with the road surface. Heavier unsprung weight generally makes a suspension react slower and feel less precise, even if the tire itself grips well. This is one reason the autocross setup above pairs a wider front tire with a narrower, lighter rear wheel. The wider front tire adds grip up front, without adding weight to the rear too.

Every size owners report above stays inside the 15-inch wheel diameter family. This matters because your Fit's Sport trim already used a 15-inch factory wheel. Staying at 15 inches keeps your speedometer and anti-lock brake system reading closer to correct than jumping straight to a 16-inch or 17-inch wheel would.

Autocross tire sizes and classing

Autocross is a timed event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked with cones, usually in a parking lot. A stock, unmodified 2008 Fit runs in H Street (HS) class under SCCA's national Solo rules, since the rulebook's "Fit" entry does not carry a model-year restriction. Once you add aftermarket wheels, tires, and other Street Touring-eligible parts, your Fit typically moves into Street Touring FWD (STF) class instead. STF allows a wider range of parts than H Street does.

Combining every source found for this chapter, two sizes come up most often for GD3 autocross: 195/50R15 and 205/50R15, both usually run on 15x7-to-7.5-inch wheels. Chapter 58 covers autocross classing and event prep in full detail; this chapter only connects your tire choice to that bigger picture.

A buying tip before you spend

Every size above is community-reported, meaning real owners shared it online, not a guarantee written by Honda or a tire manufacturer. Your own car's ride height, past accident history, and even factory tolerances can shift what actually clears. Before you buy a full set, ask the seller or a shop whether they have fit that exact size to a GD3 before. Check clearance on your own car at full steering lock before you commit.

Quick check

  1. What do the three parts of a tire size like 195/50R15 tell you?
  2. What is "offset," and why does changing it affect whether a tire rubs on the fender?
  3. Which size do owners report as the safest baseline fit for a GD3, and which size is described as marginal?
  4. Why can a bigger, heavier wheel work against your handling goal instead of helping it?
  5. What SCCA class does a stock 2008 Fit run in, and what class do many modified GD3s move into?

Chapter 50Suspension — Springs, Coilovers, Sway Bars, and Alignment

What you will learn

  • What a spring, a coilover, and a sway bar each actually do
  • 9 real suspension products confirmed to fit your GD3, and how each was checked
  • 7 real products that looked right but were rejected, and exactly why
  • How to run that same fitment check yourself before you ever buy a part
  • Street and track alignment targets, and why they are not the same numbers

The short version

A lowering spring drops your ride height and changes how stiff your suspension feels, and a coilover is a fully adjustable spring-and-shock unit. A sway bar reduces how much your car leans in a corner. This chapter lists 9 real products confirmed, from each product's own listing text, to fit your 2007-2008 GD3 Fit. It also lists 7 look-alike products this book rejected, because they were actually built for the different 2009-2014 GE8 Fit. Learning to spot that difference yourself is one of the most useful skills a Fit modder can build.

The full explanation

What these parts actually do

A spring is the coiled metal part that holds your car's weight up and compresses when the wheel hits a bump. A shorter, stiffer aftermarket spring usually lowers your ride height and reduces how much the car leans and dives. A coilover combines a spring around a shock absorber, called a damper, into one adjustable unit. Most coilovers let you set ride height independent of how hard the spring is preloaded, and many also let you adjust damping, meaning how quickly the shock compresses and rebounds.

A sway bar, also called an anti-roll bar, is a metal bar that connects the left and right suspension. It twists slightly in a corner to resist body lean. A thicker sway bar generally means a stiffer, less-leaning car.

Three more terms matter for the alignment section later in this chapter. Camber is how much a wheel tilts inward or outward at the top, viewed from the front of the car, measured in degrees. Caster is how much the steering axis tilts, viewed from the side. Toe is whether the wheels point slightly toward each other or away from each other, viewed from above.

The single most important rule in this chapter

Chapter 47 warned you that a part built for the 2009-2014 Fit, chassis code GE8, does not automatically fit your 2007-2008 Fit, chassis code GD3, even when it looks identical. This chapter puts that warning into practice with real parts, real listings, and real mistakes this book's own research caught.

Here is how you run the same check yourself. Read the product listing's own text, not only its headline. Look for the words "GD3," "2007-2008," or "07-08," or for two separate fitment lines, one for 2007 and one for 2008. A generic claim like "fits Honda Fit" is not enough on its own, since that same wording can quietly cover the GE8 too. Watch for three specific traps. A trusted brand can sell a near-identical part for the GE8 under a similar name. A part number can look right while sitting on a different generation's page. And a Japanese-market production-date window can fail to line up with the US 2007-2008 model years, even when the chassis code looks correct.

9 real products confirmed for the GD3

Every product below states, in its own listing text, that it fits the 2007-2008 Fit or the GD3 chassis code by name. Confirm this yourself again at the time you buy, since listings change.

Product Type GD3 confirmation in the listing Reported spec
BC Racing BR Series Coilover "2007-2008 Honda Fit FWD (GD3)," two separate year lines 5 kg/mm front and rear, ride height and damping adjustable
TEIN BASIC (DSB36-LUAS2) Coilover "07+ GD3 Honda Fit FF" Twin-tube, 22mm piston front and rear, ride height adjustable
Skunk2 Pro-C (541-05-6990) Coilover "06-08 Honda Fit" / "2006-2008 Honda Fit" 12-way adjustable, 8 kg/mm front, 6 kg/mm progressive rear, race-oriented
Fortune Auto 510 Series Coilover "Honda Fit (GD3) USDM 2007-2008," repeated on the page Customer-selectable spring rates, 4k-16k kg/mm range
F2 Suspension Type 2 Coilover "Honda Fit GD (2006-2008)" Fitment confirmed; specific spring rates not confirmed this pass
TEIN S.Tech Lowering spring "2007-2008 Honda Fit FWD (GD3)," two separate year lines Front 2.8 kg/mm, rear 6 kg/mm, drop about 1.8 in front / 1.2 in rear
H&R Sport Spring (51830) Lowering spring Fitment table names 2007 and 2008 Fit Base, and Sport, separately Drop about 1.6 in front / 1.3 in rear
Eibach Pro-Kit (4069.140) Lowering spring Listing's own fitment list names only "2007 Honda Fit" and "2008 Honda Fit," no 2009+ line Drop 1.6 in front / 1.3 in rear
Fortune Auto 500 Series Coilover "Honda Fit (GD3) USDM 2007-2008," same title pattern as the 510 Series Entry-level version of the 510 Series above

That is 9 real spring and coilover products, across 6 different brands, each confirmed by its own listing text to fit the GD3. Notice that the H&R spring's own fitment table names your base trim directly, not only the Sport trim.

7 rejected products — the year-confusion trap, caught in the act

While researching the list above, this book also found 7 similar-looking products that failed the same check. Every one is recorded here so you can recognize the pattern, not only avoid these specific parts.

# Category Product Why it was rejected
