1 Lowering spring Eibach Pro-Kit, part 4081.140 Listing says "2009-2013" / "09-12 Honda Fit," a GE8-only part with a different part number than the correct GD3 part above
2 Coilover TEIN Street Basis Z Listing title and URL say "2009-2014 Honda Fit FWD (GE8)"
3 Coilover TEIN Street Advance Z Listing tags itself "(GE8)," even though "2008" also appears in the title text
4 Camber bolt Eibach Pro-Alignment 5.81260K Listing says "Honda Fit (2009-2014)" (GE8)
5 Brake pad EBC Yellowstuff Listing says "Honda Fit (09-15)" (GE8/GK); no listing naming "2007-2008" or "GD3" was found
6 Camber bolt SPC EZCam 81260, on a "09-14" page Same physical part number as a confirmed GD3 fact below, but this specific page is tagged for the GE8, so it is not cited as a GD3 source
7 Coilover TEIN FLEX Z Listing does say "GD3," but its own Japanese production-date window, 2003.10 to 2007.09, ends before the US 2008 model year begins

Row 3 is worth reading twice. A listing can say "2008" in its title and still not be for your car. That happens when the same listing also tags itself with a trim or region code, like "RS," that Honda never sold as a US 2008 base Fit. Row 7 shows an even sneakier version of the same trap: the chassis code is right, but the production-date window quietly excludes your car's actual build year. Read every date and code on a listing, not only the ones that jump out at you.

This chapter researched three categories: springs and coilovers, camber bolts, and brake pads. Across all three, 7 total candidates were found and rejected for not actually fitting the GD3, judged by their own listing text.

The same part, three different listings

One more case study drives the lesson home. The retailer Heeltoe Automotive sells the identical SPC 81260 camber bolt on three separate product pages. One page is tagged "07-08 Honda Fit" for your GD3. Another is tagged "09-14 Honda Fit" for the GE8, and a third is tagged "15-20 Honda Fit" for the newer GK generation. A buyer who searches only by the bare part number, 81260, could click into the wrong page and never notice. Always confirm you are on the generation-matched page, not only the correct part number.

Sway bars for the rear

The Progress Technology 22mm rear sway bar, part 62.1060, is listed for "07-08 Honda Fit," and its own body copy adds "Compatible with GD Honda Fit." The Ultra Racing 16mm rear sway bar is listed for "Honda Fit (GD) 2001-2008," a thinner alternative to the Progress bar above.

One listing deserves a caution instead of a recommendation. A Whiteline sway bar link is sold under a web address that says "rear sway bar link." The same page's own title and body text, read directly, say "Front Sway Bar Link" throughout instead. Because the page disagrees with itself about which end of the car this part is for, this book does not use it as a confirmed rear-bar part. As a bonus, a Cusco 24mm bar listed for "Honda Fit-Jazz GD1/GD3" was also found this pass. It is explicitly a front sway bar, not a rear one, so skip it if you are specifically shopping for a rear bar.

Camber and alignment adjustment parts

The SPC EZCam 14mm camber bolt, part 81260, gives roughly plus or minus 1.75 degrees of front camber adjustment when both bolts on a strut are replaced. It installs by swapping out one or both of your strut's existing pinch bolts for this eccentric, off-center bolt. Buy it from the page tagged "07-08 Honda Fit," not the "09-14" or "15-20" pages selling the identical part number.

Honed Developments, a shop focused specifically on the GD3, sells a strut-top part as part of its published GD3 suspension recipe. This part increases both front caster and front camber beyond what a camber bolt alone provides, though no numeric adjustment figure for it was confirmed this pass. Your Fit's rear suspension is a torsion-beam axle, a solid rear beam with no built-in camber or toe adjustment from the factory. Honed Developments also sells a rear alignment shim kit, listed for "GD & GK Jazz/Fit," that adds camber and toe adjustment where none exists from the factory.

Alignment specs: street versus track

These two alignment targets come from different sources and different goals, so this book labels each one clearly rather than blending them into a single number.

One GD3-focused suspension recipe publishes a track or performance setup. Up front: camber -3.5 to -4.5 degrees, caster +2 to +2.5 degrees, and toe 0 degrees. In the rear: camber -2 to -2.5 degrees, and toe +2 to 0 (in). This target came from a single source, so treat it as a strong starting point for a dedicated track car, not a universal rule.

GD3 owners report a gentler street or daily-driving setup instead: roughly -1.25 to -2.0 degrees of front camber, which balances comfortable driving with acceptable tire wear. Toe is kept as close to 0 as the front and rear allow. Owners report that rotating your tires regularly helps manage the extra wear that negative camber causes on a street-driven car. Notice how much less aggressive this is than the track numbers above; a track alignment on a daily driver chews through tires fast.

One number this book could not confirm is your Fit's original factory alignment spec. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit's factory alignment; check your owner's manual or a dealer. A source found this pass gave factory numbers alongside a tire fitment and pressure spec. That pressure spec conflicts with this book's own verified owner's-manual data elsewhere in this book. This book's research could not confirm which generation or market that source actually describes. Rather than guess, this book flags the conflict and sends you to your own door-jamb sticker and factory manual instead.

Quick check

  1. Name the three alignment angles, camber, caster, and toe, and say what each one describes.
  2. What is the difference between a lowering spring and a coilover?
  3. List two ways a suspension listing can quietly reveal it is actually built for the GE8, not your GD3.
  4. Why does the same SPC camber bolt appear on three separate product pages, and why does that matter to you as a buyer?
  5. How do the track alignment numbers in this chapter differ from the street numbers, and why does that difference exist?

Chapter 51Brakes for Mods — Pads, Fluid, Lines, Rotors, and Cooling

What you will learn

  • Two brake pad compounds built for street use and track use, both confirmed for your GD3
  • How to read a brake fluid boiling point, and why a bigger DOT number is not always the winner
  • Three stainless steel brake line kits confirmed for your GD3
  • Two upgrade areas, rotors and brake cooling, that this chapter could not confirm real GD3 products for

The short version

A track-oriented brake pad, a high-boiling-point brake fluid, and stainless steel brake lines all work together to give you a firmer, more consistent pedal under repeated hard braking. This chapter presents real manufacturer data on two pad compounds and two fluid types, all confirmed to fit or apply to your GD3. Fresh, correct fluid matters as much as any pad choice, since fluid that boils turns your brake pedal soft right when you need it most.

The full explanation

Two pad compounds, one confirmed fitment list

Hawk Performance sells two pad compounds worth knowing for a modified Fit. Hawk's own fitment database lists its HPS ("High Performance Street") front pad, part HB418F.646, for both the 2007 and 2008 Honda Fit, Base and Sport, by name. HPS is a street-oriented compound: lower noise and dust for daily driving, positioned below Hawk's track pad for outright heat capacity.

Hawk's HP+ ("High Performance Plus") front pad, part HB418N.646, appears on the identical fitment list, again naming both 2007 and 2008, Base and Sport, by name. HP+ is Hawk's higher-temperature track and autocross compound, generally preferred for repeated hard sessions, at the cost of more noise, more dust, and a longer bed-in period on the street. Both parts are confirmed for your GD3 by Hawk's own manufacturer fitment list naming your car and your base trim directly, not by a generic aftermarket claim.

Brake fluid: DOT numbers and boiling points

Brake fluid transfers the force from your foot on the pedal to the pads or shoes at each wheel, through a sealed hydraulic system. Brake fluid absorbs a small amount of water over time, and hot brakes can heat that fluid enough to boil it. Fluid that boils turns to gas bubbles, which compress instead of pushing the pads, and your pedal goes soft or sinks to the floor.

A DOT rating is a US safety standard that sets a minimum boiling point for a brake fluid. Dry boiling point is the boiling point of fresh, unused fluid. Wet boiling point is the boiling point after the fluid has absorbed a set amount of water, closer to how fluid behaves after real-world use.

Fluid Dry boiling point Wet boiling point
FMVSS 116 DOT 4, regulatory minimum 446°F (230°C) 311°F (155°C)
FMVSS 116 DOT 5.1 (non-silicone), regulatory minimum 500°F (260°C) 356°F (180°C)
Motul DOT 5.1, actual product data 522°F (272°C) 365°F (185°C)
Motul RBF 600 Factory Line, a racing DOT 4 fluid 594°F (312°C) 399°F (204°C)

Read that table carefully, because it teaches an important lesson. The racing-grade DOT 4 fluid at the bottom has a higher boiling point than the plain DOT 5.1 fluid above it. This is true even though 5.1 is technically the "higher" number. A DOT rating is a minimum standard, not a guarantee that a higher-numbered fluid always beats a lower-numbered one. When you shop for a track-capable fluid, compare actual boiling points on the data sheet, not only the DOT number printed on the bottle.

This chapter could not confirm which brake fluid your Fit's owner's manual specifies from the factory. We could not confirm this specific fact in this chapter's own research; Chapter 27 covers your factory-specified fluid type in full. Use this chapter's boiling-point table to compare upgrade options once you know your factory baseline from Chapter 27.

Stainless steel brake lines

A stainless steel brake line replaces the factory rubber brake hose with a braided steel outer layer over a sealed inner tube. Rubber hoses can swell slightly under hard, repeated braking, which can make the pedal feel soft even with good pads and fluid; a stainless line resists that swelling.

Three stainless line kits were confirmed for the GD3 this pass. A StopTech kit, part 950.40017, is listed as a front kit for "Honda Fit 2007-2008." The listing found this pass marks it discontinued at that retailer, so treat it as a lead to chase down, not a guaranteed current purchase. A Fastline Performance kit carries the chassis code directly in its own part number, FLP-BLKIT-GD3, in addition to a "2007-08 Honda Fit" title. A Techna-Fit kit, part HN-2600, is listed for "2007-2008 Honda Fit" by two independent retailers.

Notice that the Fastline part number is the strongest fitment confirmation of the three. The manufacturer built the chassis code into the part number itself, not only into marketing text on a web page.

Rotors and brake cooling: an honest gap

Every product in this chapter so far came with real, checked fitment text. Rotors and dedicated brake-cooling parts, like brake ducts that route outside air to a hot rotor, did not turn up a confirmed GD3-specific product in this chapter's own research. We could not confirm specific rotor or brake-cooling products for the 2008 Fit in this pass. Check current listings yourself, and apply the same GD3-versus-GE8 check from Chapter 50 to anything you find.

A general point still applies even without a specific product to name. Any rotor you buy should be sized and drilled for your Fit's specific caliper and hub. Any brake-cooling duct should be checked for physical clearance against your specific bumper and fender, the same way a wheel or tire needs a clearance check in Chapter 49.

Quick check

  1. Which two Hawk pad compounds are confirmed for your GD3, and which one suits daily street driving better?
  2. What is the difference between a dry boiling point and a wet boiling point?
  3. Why can a DOT 4 fluid sometimes have a higher boiling point than a DOT 5.1 fluid?
  4. Name the three confirmed stainless brake line kits, and which one has the strongest fitment proof, and why.
  5. Why did this chapter not name a specific rotor or brake-cooling product for your GD3?

Chapter 52Intake, Header, and Exhaust

What you will learn

  • What an intake, a header, and an exhaust system each do on your Fit's engine.
  • The real dyno numbers researchers found for each part on this exact car.
  • Why a manufacturer's own claim is weaker proof than two separate independent tests.
  • Why no clean, isolated dyno number exists for an exhaust system alone.
  • How these parts perform together, instead of one at a time.

The short version

An intake, a header, and an exhaust each free up a small amount of power on your L15A1 engine . Every real dyno number found for these parts on this car is modest, in the single digits . No clean, isolated dyno test for an exhaust system alone was found for this car .

The full explanation

Words you need first

A dynamometer, or dyno, is a machine that measures how much power an engine makes . Some dynos measure power at the wheels . That number is written as whp . Wheel numbers are usually lower than crankshaft numbers . Power gets lost on the way to the wheels . A cold air intake replaces the factory air box and tubing . It feeds air into your engine . A header replaces the factory exhaust manifold . That is the metal piece that collects exhaust gas right off the engine . A cat-back exhaust replaces the pipe and muffler after the catalytic converter . The catalytic converter is the part that cleans exhaust gas . An axle-back exhaust replaces only the section behind the rear axle . It is a smaller piece than a full cat-back . A test pipe, sometimes called a catless downpipe, is a straight pipe . It replaces the catalytic converter itself .

Intake gains

Researchers found dyno numbers for three intake products on the L15A1 . Injen's SP1511 cold air intake is built specifically for the 2007-2008 Fit . Injen advertises a gain of up to 7 hp, plus up to 8 lb-ft of extra torque . That same figure appears on a second retailer's page . Both pages trace back to the same original Injen claim . They are not two separately run tests . This book reports the number as Injen's own claim, not as two independent tests .

A different, independent video test used a Dynapack dyno on a car identified as a GD3 . An HPS Performance cold air intake gained a reported 5.9 whp at 5,685 rpm . It also gained 7.6 lb-ft of torque at 3,655 rpm . An HPS Performance short-ram intake pulls air from inside the engine bay instead of outside . It gained a reported 7.1 whp at 5,645 rpm and 6.4 lb-ft at 5,590 rpm . Three different intake products, tested two different ways, all landed in the same narrow range . Every reported gain is roughly 5 to 8 hp, and roughly 6 to 8 lb-ft . No source found claims a double-digit horsepower gain from an intake alone .

Header gains

Only one number-based dyno claim for a header alone was found . It came from a test on an otherwise completely stock GD3 Fit . A fitfreak.net forum thread reported a gain of "5+HP" . Owners report that figure; it was not independently re-tested by this research . That single number sits below this book's normal bar of two separate sources . Treat it as a rough hint, not a settled fact . A second header claim of "+9 hp" turned up during research . It named a header built for the 2009-2014 Fit . That is a different car, with a different engine, so it does not count here . Header products built specifically for the 2007-2008 GD3 Fit are still sold today . No published dyno number is attached to that current product's own listing .

Exhaust gains, and the honest gap

Stop and remember Chapter 47 before you read this section. A test pipe removes your car's catalytic converter, the part that cleans exhaust gas. Removing it is exactly the kind of change the Clean Air Act calls a defeat device. Federal law makes that illegal on a car driven on public roads, with real fines attached. A test pipe belongs on a car built only for closed-course track or competition use. It does not belong on a car you also drive to school or work, unless a specific part carries a CARB EO that covers your exact car.

Community discussion describes a test pipe alone as "usually" adding about 3 to 5 whp . One owner reported a header, a test pipe, and a cat-back exhaust installed together . That combination raised the car from 92 whp to 100 whp . That is an 8 whp gain for all three parts combined, not the test pipe alone .

Here is the honest gap in this research. No clean, isolated dyno number for a cat-back or axle-back exhaust alone was found for this exact car . Every full-exhaust dyno result found was reported together with a header, a test pipe, or an engine tune . There is no way to know what the exhaust piece alone contributed . Rather than guess at a number, this book leaves it unconfirmed . Real cat-back and axle-back exhaust products built for the 2007-2008 Fit do exist today . A'PEXi and HKS both sell one . Neither brand's own product page publishes a specific dyno figure . Both use only general marketing language instead .

What happens when you combine all of them

Two community reports show what these parts can do together . Neither report isolates any single part's own share of the gain . One 2008 Fit owner started with a stock rating near 107 hp . He reported reaching about 115 whp . He got there by adding an intake, a header, a catless downpipe, a full exhaust, and an engine tune together . A second build reported 115.46 whp from a header, a downpipe, an axle-back muffler, and a tune . A third build, with an intake, a header, and a downpipe, reached 101 whp with a different tune . These are real reports from real owners . Treat them as "several parts together," not proof of what any one part adds by itself .

Putting it together

An intake, a header, and an exhaust are each real, modest upgrades on the L15A1 . None of them is a magic power boost . Every isolated number found for these parts is small, in the single digits . The header claim rests on one source, not two, so treat it carefully . No isolated exhaust-only number exists at all . This book will not invent one . If you want a real, noticeable gain, expect it to come from combining several parts, plus a tune, not any single part alone .

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between horsepower measured at the wheels and at the crankshaft?
  2. Why does this book treat the Injen intake's "7 hp" claim as one piece of evidence, even though it appears on two web pages?
  3. What single number could this book not confirm for the exhaust system, and why not?
  4. Why was a "+9 hp" header claim left out of this chapter?
  5. If a reported combo build gains power from an intake, a header, and a tune together, what can you NOT conclude from that number?

Chapter 53Engine Management

What you will learn

  • What an ECU is, and what "tuning" or "reflashing" it actually means.
  • The single most surprising fact in this whole book about aftermarket support: which Fit generation Hondata actually supports.
  • What Hondata sells for your exact car, and what it costs.
  • What does NOT exist for your car, so you do not waste money chasing it.
  • Why a header dyno number "for a Honda Fit" you find online might not even be for your car.

The short version

A company called Hondata sells a full engine-tuning system built specifically for your 2007-2008 Fit, right now, today . Hondata has never sold anything like it for the newer 2009-2014 Fit, which is a different car with a different engine . If you assumed the newer Fit has better tuning support than yours, that assumption is backwards .

The full explanation

Words you need first

An ECU, short for Engine Control Unit, is the small computer that runs your engine . It decides things like fuel amount and spark timing . "Tuning" an ECU means changing those settings, usually to make more power or run differently . A "reflash" rewrites the software inside the factory ECU itself, through the car's OBD-II port . OBD-II is the standard data port under your dashboard, used on all cars built after 1996 . A reflash tool does not need to physically cut any wires . A "piggyback" is a separate small box that sits between the ECU and its sensors . It changes some signals without touching the factory ECU's own software . A "standalone" system fully replaces the factory ECU with a new one . CARB is the California Air Resources Board, the state agency that sets emissions rules . A CARB EO, or Executive Order, is a specific approval number from that agency . It lets an aftermarket part be sold as legal for street use in every state, not only outside California .

The headline finding

Here is the single most important finding in this chapter, so read it twice . Hondata's FlashPro system is a real, full-reflash tuning product . Hondata's own product page lists it for "Vehicle: Fit, Year: 2007-2008, Market: US/Canada" . That is your exact car . This is not a guess or an inference . It is a direct statement from the manufacturer's own current catalog .

Now for the surprising part . A direct check of Hondata's own product catalog, filtered specifically to the Fit, shows only two products . Both of them are for the 2007-2008 car, your car . Zero products exist in that same catalog for the 2009-2014 Fit . Hondata's own customer forum has a thread from a 2009-2014 Fit owner asking "Still nothing for the L15A7?" . The L15A7 is the engine in the 2009-2014 Fit . A reader might expect the newer car to have more aftermarket support, since it is newer . For Hondata specifically, the exact opposite is true . Never assume the newer Fit has better tuning support without checking for yourself .

What Hondata sells for your car

Hondata sells two different FlashPro versions for the 2007-2008 Fit . The full version, product code FP-FIT-US, costs $795 . It unlocks complete custom tuning and full datalogging . Datalogging means the system records engine data over time, so a tuner can study it later . It comes with 20 hours of on-board datalogging memory . The second version, product code FP-FIT-US-CARB, costs $595 . It offers datalogging plus a limited menu of preset tuning options, not fully open custom tuning . Hondata states this CARB version carries CARB EO number D-742-1, which keeps it legal for street use in every state, including California .

Here is the real tradeoff . The $595 CARB version keeps you street-legal everywhere, but limits you to Hondata's own preset calibrations . The $795 full version unlocks real custom tuning instead . That kind of tuning is what you need to get the most out of a header or other bolt-ons . Hondata does not represent the full version as CARB-legal for street use, though . Choose based on whether legality or full tuning matters more to you .

Both versions connect through your car's OBD-II port . Neither one requires cutting into your factory ECU . Hondata's own specification page lists the exact factory ECU part numbers this system supports . That range is RME-A11 through RME-A89 for US cars, and RME-C11 through RME-C89 for Canadian cars . An independent retailer's own product page reproduces that same exact part-number range, word for word . That second, separate confirmation makes this an unusually solid fact .

What does NOT exist for your car

Hondata also makes older products called K-Pro and s300 . Neither one applies to any Honda Fit, in either generation . K-Pro is built only for Honda's K-series engines, used in cars like the Civic Si and Acura RSX . Your Fit does not have a K-series engine . The s300 line is built for much older, 1992-1999 OBD1-era Hondas . Your Fit is a newer OBD2 car . Very old-school Honda ECU-chipping tools, such as Uberdata, Neptune, or Crome, are also built only for those same 1992-1995 OBD1 Hondas . None of them can work on your car's ECU .

A plug-and-play standalone system, like an AEM Infinity harness, is a different kind of product than FlashPro . AEM sells this kind of harness for some older Honda engines . No dedicated, named harness for the Fit or the L15A1 was found in this research . That does not prove one does not exist somewhere . It only means none was found . This is recorded as unverified, not as a confirmed "no" .

A real piggyback option, with real limits

At least one owner has used a piggyback controller, an Apexi VAFC, on a GD3 Fit . A piggyback, remember, sits between the ECU and its sensors rather than replacing the ECU . Owners report that the VAFC could adjust the air-fuel mixture . Owners also report that trying to use it to affect VTEC engagement triggered the check engine light . VTEC is Honda's variable valve-timing system . This is a single community report, so treat the specific limitation as reported, not fully confirmed .

A trap this research caught

While researching this chapter, a claim about a "+9 hp" header surfaced online . Tracing it back showed the header itself was built for the "2009-2012 Honda Fit," the newer car . The forum discussion about it also sat in a section for that newer car, not yours . This is exactly the kind of mix-up this book is built to catch . A number you find online for "a Honda Fit" might actually be for the newer, different car, not the one you own . Always check the model year before trusting a number .

Putting it together

Real, current, full-reflash ECU tuning exists for your exact car, sold today by a mainstream company, for $595 or $795 . This is not a niche or discontinued method . Hondata sells the same kind of product family for many other current Honda and Acura models . The newer 2009-2014 Fit, by contrast, has no equivalent Hondata product at all . Do not assume the newer car has better tuning support only because it is newer . For this brand and this exact question, your 2007-2008 car is the one Hondata actually built for .

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between an ECU reflash and a piggyback controller?
  2. What does Hondata's own current product catalog show for the 2009-2014 Fit's engine, the L15A7?
  3. What is the price difference between Hondata's two FlashPro versions for your car, and what do you give up with the cheaper one?
  4. Why does neither Hondata's K-Pro nor its s300 line apply to any Honda Fit?
  5. Why was a "+9 hp" header claim rejected from this chapter, even though it mentioned "a Honda Fit"?

Chapter 54Power Adders

What you will learn

  • What a turbocharger and a supercharger are, and how they differ from a header or an exhaust.
  • The three real, GD3-specific turbo or supercharger kits researchers found.
  • Why all three of those kits are hard, or impossible, to buy new today.
  • What real reliability problems owners and builders report.
  • What else you would likely need to change if you add real boost: clutch, fuel system, and more.

The short version

Exactly three real, brand-named turbo or supercharger kits were ever built specifically for your GD3 Fit's L15A1 engine . All three are hard to buy new today; two are fully discontinued, and the third is small-batch and often out of stock . Adding real boost is reported to also demand a stronger clutch and a bigger fuel system, not only the turbo or supercharger kit itself .

The full explanation

Words you need first

A turbocharger is a small turbine that uses your engine's own exhaust gas to force extra air into the engine . A supercharger does the same job, but it is spun by a belt connected to the engine, not by exhaust gas . Both are ways to force more air in, so the engine can burn more fuel and make more power . This forced air is called boost, and it is measured in psi, or pounds per square inch . An intercooler is a small radiator that cools the forced air down before it enters the engine . Cooler air is denser and safer for the engine . A CARB EO is a California approval number that lets a part be sold as legal for street use in every state . A clutch is the part that connects your engine to the transmission on a manual car . A flywheel is a heavy spinning disc the clutch grabs onto .

The three real kits

KraftWerks made a bolt-on supercharger kit built specifically for the manual-transmission 2007-2008 US Fit . It was also sold under the related brand name Jackson Racing . The kit used a Rotrex C15-60 supercharger, plus Hondata engine management and an intercooler, included in the box . It was never built for the automatic-transmission Fit . Neither KraftWerks' own current shop nor Jackson Racing's own current shop lists a Fit kit today . This kit is discontinued . Used kits can sometimes be found on sites like eBay instead .

Two different power figures were reported for this kit, from two different points in its life . An early write-up, at the kit's first announcement, reported 121 whp for the "base kit" . A later write-up described a "High-Boost" version, reporting 164 whp and 132 lb-ft of torque at 10 psi of boost . Both numbers are reported by enthusiast media, not independently re-tested by this research .

This kit's retail price was reported at $4,495 plus shipping, from a single source . Reliability reports mostly cover the same Rotrex supercharger design used across other Honda models, not the Fit specifically . Owners of those other cars report belt and tensioner problems . One report describes a tensioner bolt snapping and the belt coming off during highway driving . Another describes belt edge wear from rubbing on the pulley . KraftWerks itself states it added an automatic belt tensioner specifically for the Fit kit, and reports no belt issues on it . That is the manufacturer's own claim, though, not an independent owner report .

HKS made a bolt-on turbo kit named specifically for "2007 and 2008 model year Honda Fits" with the 1.5L engine . This is confirmed directly by California's own CARB Executive Order record, D-186-27 . The kit used a Garrett T25 turbocharger, a front-mount intercooler, and HKS's own F-Con IS engine management, which plugs into the OBD-II port . Maximum boost was set at 6.0 psi per that same official record . HKS described the kit as "legal in all 50 states," based on that CARB approval . HKS reported roughly a 30 percent gain in peak horsepower . That kit is also reported discontinued today, with only used or old-stock units left to find .

Unequal Fabrication, a small aftermarket parts maker, currently lists a "Honda Fit GD3 (07-08) Turbo Kit" at $2,300 . The listing describes a bolt-on kit that keeps your air conditioning, heat, and fog lights working . The seller states this kit can reach "170hp on the stock internals," given a healthy clutch . That is a manufacturer claim, not an independent test . Separate tuning software, such as the Hondata FlashPro covered in Chapter 53, is required and not included in the price . The listing states the kit is "FOR OFF ROAD USE" . At the time of this research, the kit was shown as "Out of Stock," built to order with a variable wait time .

A warning about generic listings

Unbranded turbo kits also show up for sale on sites like eBay, labeled for the "GD3" or "L15A" . One such listing claims a huge 485 horsepower gain . That number is far above every other figure found in this research and is not backed up anywhere . This book does not use that number, and you should not trust it either . A GD3-specific community thread, from experienced builders, gives a more realistic picture instead . They describe the stock L15A1 as able to hold about 8 psi of boost safely . They suggest a realistic target of roughly 160 to 200 whp on stock internals . One named builder blew his engine at 220 whp on a custom turbo setup . He then rebuilt it with a larger bore to reach about 260 whp . This is a single community thread, so treat it as a reported estimate, not a lab-tested fact .

One well-written article about a turbocharged Fit was found and read in full, then set aside . The car in that article used the L15A7 engine, which is the newer 2009-2014 Fit's engine, not yours . It was excluded from this chapter entirely, so no number from it appears anywhere above .

What else you would likely need

Owners report that the stock clutch can slip once real boost is added . Exedy sells Sport Clutch kits built specifically for the 2007-2008 Fit in two strength levels . The Stage 1 kit is rated for 128 lb-ft of torque to the wheels, and costs about $454 . The Stage 2 kit is rated for 160 lb-ft . For a daily-driven, non-boosted car, though, community advice says you should not need to upgrade the stock clutch at all . Some race-style clutches are reported to feel "too grabby" for comfortable daily driving . Save the clutch upgrade for if, and only if, you actually add boost .

Fuel system upgrades are also reported as necessary once you add real power . For the KraftWerks supercharger kit specifically, owners report upgrading to larger 410cc fuel injectors at 10 psi of boost . Changing injector size always requires a matching ECU retune, to avoid running dangerously lean . For custom turbo builds, owners report that aftermarket fuel pumps, such as a Walbro or Deatschwerks pump, are commonly used instead of the stock pump .

No source found in this research shows the stock axles failing specifically because of added horsepower . This is left honestly unverified, rather than guessed at .

Putting it together

Three real turbo or supercharger kits were built for your exact car, and all three are hard to buy new today . Reported power gains for these kits range from about 121 whp up to about 260 whp on a heavily built engine . Reliability problems are real and reported, mostly around belts, tensioners, and the need for a stronger clutch and fuel system . Treat any huge, unsupported power claim you find online, like the 485 horsepower eBay listing, with real suspicion .

Quick check

  1. What is the difference between how a turbocharger and a supercharger force air into the engine?
  2. Name the three real, GD3-specific turbo or supercharger kits found in this research.
  3. Why is a huge power claim on a generic eBay turbo-kit listing not trustworthy?
  4. What two other systems do owners report needing to upgrade once they add real boost?
  5. Why was a well-written turbo-Fit build article excluded from this chapter entirely?

Chapter 55Engine Swaps

What you will learn

  • What an engine swap is, and why it is a much bigger job than a bolt-on part.
  • The one documented engine family that has actually been swapped into a GD3 Fit.
  • The real obstacles builders report: mounts, wiring, the ECU, the transmission, and more.
  • Why this is one of the thinnest, smallest topics in this whole book.
  • What was searched for and not found, so you know the honest limits of this research.

The short version

An engine swap means removing your car's factory engine and installing a different one . Exactly one engine family has a real, documented swap into a GD3 Fit: Honda's K-series engine . This is a genuinely small, niche corner of the Fit hobby; one community estimate puts the total number of K-swapped GD3 Fits worldwide at fewer than 50 .

The full explanation

Words you need first

An engine swap replaces a car's original engine with a different one, often from another model . Honda's K-series is a family of engines used in cars like the Civic Si and the Acura TSX . A wiring harness is the bundle of wires that connects an engine and its ECU to the rest of the car . An immobilizer is an anti-theft system that stops the engine from starting without the correct key . A vehicle speed sensor, or VSS, tells the ECU how fast the car is moving . A limited slip differential, or LSD, is a transmission part that helps both drive wheels get power during hard cornering .

A legal reminder before you start

An engine swap is a much bigger emissions question than any bolt-on part in this book. Chapter 47 already covered the basic rule: federal law bans removing or defeating emissions equipment, and many states, especially California, apply extra rules specifically to swapped engines. This book's research did not confirm the exact swap-approval steps for this exact car, so this book will not guess at them. Check your own state's rules on engine swaps before you start, the same way Chapter 17 already told you to check your state's inspection and emissions rules.

The one documented swap family

Researchers found real, detailed build documentation for exactly one swapped-in engine family: Honda's K-series . Two specific K-series engines have documented builds: the K24A2, pulled from a wrecked Acura TSX, and the K20Z3, pulled from a Civic Si . No documented, completed swap of any other engine family was found for a GD3 chassis . That includes other Honda engine families, non-Honda engines, and electric motor conversions .

Two independent, named builders documented their own K-series swaps . One builder runs a website, pbomers.com, showing his own K24A2 swap into a GD3 Fit in detail . He says he has a running K24-swapped Fit . He also points to his brother's YouTube channel for video coverage of four completed swaps between the two of them . A second builder posted a 2014 forum thread about a K20Z3 swap into a 2007 GD Fit . He used an engine, ECU, and wiring harness pulled from a Civic Si . A different forum member replied that he had "just went through this" the same way, about a month earlier . That second, independent reply shows this is a known method within the small community, not a one-off guess .

The real obstacles

Builders report real problems in several different car systems . On motor mounts: without stiffer aftermarket mounts, or reinforcing the stock ones, the engine is reported to rock and rub inside the tight engine bay . Hasport is the one mount brand specifically named by builders . On body clearance: the front support structure often has to be cut to fit the larger K24 intake manifold . A smaller K-series intake needs less cutting . The stock radiator and its fans can also foul the taller intake manifold, so some builders relocate the radiator and switch to slimmer fans .

Wiring is reported as one of the hardest parts of this swap . One builder said he contacted more than half a dozen harness companies and was ignored by all of them . His solution was to hand-build a harness, using an Acura TSX ECU, since the TSX shares similar wiring architecture with the Fit . The car's immobilizer has to be disabled or re-synced to the new ECU and key, at a Honda dealership . Builders also report that only certain donor transmissions work correctly . Honda changed its vehicle speed sensor design at some point . An older-design sensor will not read correctly on a newer ECU . Builders report using axles from a 2002-2004 Acura RSX, instead of the stock Fit axles, to make everything fit together . Because the Fit uses electric power steering, an idler arm borrowed from a Honda Civic Si is also reported as necessary . Even the exhaust header is reported as a genuinely tough part to source for this swap .

Cost and rarity

No total build-cost figure was found for a full K-series swap into a GD3 . This is left honestly unverified, rather than estimated or guessed at . One real, single component price point was found instead: a commercial Hasport-brand engine mount kit for this exact swap sells for $249 to $285, depending on the retailer . That is only a small fraction of what a complete swap would actually cost . On rarity, one experienced forum member estimated that fewer than 50 K-swapped GD3 Fits exist worldwide . That is a rough personal guess, not an official count . A second, separate long-time forum member said he could not remember any currently active members with a K-swapped Fit . Both statements point the same direction: this swap is genuinely rare .

A real parts market, even so

Even though this swap is rare, a real commercial parts market exists to support it . Three separate retailers each independently sell the same Hasport-brand engine mount kit, specifically titled for the "07-08 Honda Fit" K-series swap . That does not confirm any one builder's total cost, but it does show real, ongoing commercial demand, from more than one seller .

Claims checked and set aside

One claim, that a professional shop called King Motorsports built a K24-swapped Fit for the SEMA car show, was searched for directly and could not be confirmed . Only an ordinary parts listing for a mount kit was found, no build article or show record . This claim is not used anywhere in this book .

A forum thread asking about swapping in a B-series or D-series Honda engine, a different engine family than K-series, was found . No completed, documented build of that kind into a GD3 chassis was found, though . This is recorded honestly as "few to no documented examples," not as a confirmed impossibility .

An electric motor, or EV, conversion was also searched for . One forum thread shows a GD3 owner asking questions about an EV conversion, which suggests at least planning-stage interest . No completed, documented GD3-specific EV conversion build was found . General EV conversion kits exist for other cars, but none found named the Fit specifically .

Putting it together

This is one of the smallest, most niche topics in this entire book . Exactly one engine family, Honda's K-series, has real documented swaps into a GD3 Fit . The obstacles are real and touch nearly every system in the car: mounts, wiring, the ECU, the transmission, the axles, even the steering . No total cost figure exists, and the swap is genuinely rare, at well under 50 known worldwide by one rough estimate . If you are considering this, expect a long, expensive, mostly self-taught project, not a weekend job .

Quick check

  1. What is the one documented engine family that has been swapped into a GD3 Fit?
  2. Name three different systems in the car that builders report as real obstacles for this swap.
  3. Why does this book leave the total swap cost as unverified, instead of estimating it?
  4. About how many K-swapped GD3 Fits does one community estimate suggest exist worldwide?
  5. Why was a claim about a professional shop building one for a car show left out of this chapter?

Chapter 56Weight reduction and aerodynamics

What you will learn

  • Which parts owners commonly remove from a base 2008 Fit to save weight
  • How much weight the spare tire, its cover, and the jack system add up to
  • How much weight the air conditioning system adds
  • Why SCCA's Street category limits what you can remove
  • Why aerodynamic parts are a thin topic for this specific car

The short version

Removing the spare tire, its cover, and the jack and tools together saves you roughly 30 to 35 pounds. Removing the air conditioning system saves about 23.46 pounds more. Aerodynamic parts like spoilers and splitters exist for this car, but they rarely help much at Fit speeds, and SCCA's Street category will not let you use them anyway.

The full explanation

Why weight matters

A lighter car turns, stops, and accelerates better with the same engine. Removing weight is one of the cheapest ways to make a stock Fit quicker at autocross or a track day. Autocross is a timed driving event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked with cones. SCCA stands for the Sports Car Club of America. It is a group that runs autocross and racing events and writes the rulebooks for them.

The spare tire system

In this book, the "spare tire system" means the spare tire itself, its cover, and the jack and tools that go with it.

Community-reported figures found for a first-generation Fit like yours put the spare tire itself at about 21 pounds (9,525 g). The fabric cover some Fits carry over the spare weighs about 7.76 pounds (3,520 g). The jack, wrench, tow hook, and mounting bracket together weigh about 4.91 pounds (2,227 g). Add those three together and you get roughly 30 to 35 pounds. These numbers come from a single online community source, so treat them as a good estimate, not an exact figure.

Removing the spare tire system is easy and reversible. You can put it back before you drive home. But driving without a spare means you have no backup if you get a flat tire on the way to or from the event. Plan around that risk.

The air conditioning system

Owners report that removing the entire air conditioning system — the condenser, compressor, refrigerant lines, brackets, and belt pulley — saves about 23.46 pounds (10,645 g). This job also frees up a small amount of engine power, since the compressor no longer drags on the engine's belt. This is a bigger job than pulling the spare tire, and it is usually a track-only or Street-Prepared-category modification, not something you do for a single event. Always have a licensed shop recover the refrigerant with the correct equipment before any A/C line is opened. Venting refrigerant straight into the air is illegal under federal law and harms the environment, so this is not a driveway job.

Sound deadening and rear seats

Owners report removing sound-deadening material — the tar-like padding under the carpet that quiets road noise — for further weight savings. One community source lists about 11 pounds of sound deadening found so far, but the source itself calls this tally incomplete. Sound deadening is a modest weight item on this car compared to the seats or the air conditioning system.

Rear seat weight is a different story. We could not confirm this for the 2008 Fit. Check your owner's manual. A number exists for the newer, second-generation Fit's rear seat, but that is a different car, so this book will not print it as if it applied to yours. The Fit's fold-flat Magic Seat design — Honda's name for rear seats that fold down flat to open up cargo space — makes removing the rear seat an easy, reversible job. That is likely why owners still do it, even without a confirmed weight figure.

The realistic number, and the extreme number

One community source describes a full "race trim" build that removes about 534.67 pounds (242,525 g) total. That number assumes gutting most of the interior, fitting lighter wheels and tires, a lightweight flywheel and battery, and a dedicated racing seat. That is not a realistic goal for a typical weekend autocrosser.

A more honest number keeps the car street-legal and Street-category-legal. Combining the spare tire system and the air conditioning system saves about 55 to 60 pounds. SCCA's Street category rule requires the car to run "as specified by the manufacturer with only standard equipment." That means a Street-classed Fit cannot legally remove its rear seats, sound deadening, or air conditioning at all. Owners who want to remove more have to move up into Street Prepared, Street Modified, Prepared, or Modified category first.

Aerodynamics: an honest, thin topic

Aerodynamic parts use the shape of moving air to push a car down onto the road, which can help it grip better in fast corners. For the base 2008 Fit, this is honestly a thin topic, for three reasons.

First, only a few parts are made specifically for this exact chassis. Rear spoilers are the most common part sold for a GD3 Fit, and most of them are small, styling-focused pieces rather than parts built to add real downforce. Front and side splitters — panels that redirect air under the front of the car — are also sold specifically for the GD3 chassis, by at least two retailers. Canards, small wing-like fins added to the front bumper, are the thinnest category. No canard was found molded specifically for this car's bumper shape; the parts sold are generic pieces trimmed to fit.

Second, SCCA's Street category will not let you use most of these parts anyway. The rulebook does not list splitters, canards, or non-factory wings as allowed Street-category modifications. You would need to move up to Street Prepared, Street Modified, Prepared, or Modified category before you could legally add them.

Third, aerodynamic parts work best at higher speeds, because the extra grip they create grows with the square of your speed. A Fit at typical street or track-day speeds is well below the speed range where a splitter or wing meaningfully outweighs the extra weight and drag it adds. Treat a rear spoiler as a styling choice, and a splitter as a niche option for a higher-category or track-only build, not a priority modification for this car.

A general checklist, beyond the specific numbers

Beyond the confirmed figures above, owners of small economy hatchbacks commonly remove other items too. These include the factory first-aid or warning-triangle kit, any aftermarket subwoofer or amplifier, and floor mats. They also empty the trunk of anything not needed that day. These items do not have specific weight figures behind them in this book; they are general community practice, not measured facts.

Quick check

  1. Roughly how much weight does removing the spare tire system save, and what risk do you take on by removing it before an event?
  2. About how much does the air conditioning system weigh if fully removed?
  3. Why can a Street-classed Fit not legally remove its rear seats or air conditioning?
  4. Name the three reasons aerodynamics is described as a thin topic for this car.
  5. What is a canard, and why are GD3-specific canards hard to find?

Chapter 57Seats, harnesses, roll bars

What you will learn

  • What SCCA autocross rules require for seat belts and helmets in a stock Fit
  • Why a stock Fit does not need a roll bar or harness for autocross
  • What NASA requires for seat belts and roll bars at a track day
  • Why NASA's roll bar rule depends on your car's body style, not your speed
  • What happens once you move up to wheel-to-wheel racing

The short version

A stock, closed-body Fit does not need a roll bar or an aftermarket harness for autocross or a typical track day. Both SCCA and NASA only require a roll bar once a car is either heavily modified or has an open top, like a convertible. Neither rulebook ties the roll bar requirement to how fast you drive.

The full explanation

Two organizations, two kinds of event

This chapter covers rules from two organizations. SCCA, the Sports Car Club of America, runs autocross events, called Solo. NASA, the National Auto Sport Association, runs High Performance Driving Events, known as HPDE, along with Time Trial and full wheel-to-wheel racing. An HPDE is a track day where you drive your own car on a real racetrack. An instructor rides along at first, to help you build skill rather than to race other cars.

A roll bar is a metal hoop or frame built into the car to protect your head in a rollover crash. A roll cage is a fuller, more complete version of the same idea, with multiple bars protecting more of the cabin. A harness is a set of racing seat belts with more attachment points than a factory belt, usually four, five, six, or seven points instead of the factory's three.

SCCA autocross: what a stock Fit needs

For autocross, SCCA's current rulebook requires a lap belt in every car. A lap belt is the strap that crosses your hips; a shoulder belt is the strap that crosses your chest. Using a shoulder belt or a harness is strongly recommended, but it is not required in a closed car like your Fit. Your Fit's factory-installed belt system already meets this rule.

Here is a rule worth remembering carefully: SCCA only allows a non-factory harness in an open car. An open car means a convertible, or a car with its targa top or T-tops removed. Even then, the car also needs a roll structure that meets the rulebook's Appendix C standard. The top of that structure must sit at or above the top of your helmet. Your Fit is a closed hardtop with no factory open-top version, so this rule does not open a path to installing a racing harness in it. The rulebook does not let you swap a racing harness into a closed car at all.

Some autocrossers install a harness bar, a bar that lets you mount alternate belts, without a full roll cage. A harness bar is only a belt-mounting fixture. It is not a certified roll structure by itself. So installing a harness bar alone does not satisfy the roll-structure requirement above, and does not open the door to using a harness in a closed car either.

Helmets are a firmer rule. Every competitor, in every class, needs a helmet meeting one of a specific list of Snell, SFI, or FIA safety standards. Snell, SFI, and FIA are organizations that test and certify helmets for motorsport use, and none of them offer an exception based on speed or class. The chin strap must be fastened securely. Most cars with a normal-size windshield, like your Fit, do not need a full-face helmet, but open-wheel cars and karts do.

SCCA autocross: when a roll bar becomes mandatory

A roll bar or cage is not required for a closed car running in SCCA's Street, Street Touring, Street Prepared, or Street Modified categories. These are the categories a base, lightly-modified Fit would run in; see Chapter 58 for exactly which class. A roll bar only becomes mandatory in specific, heavily-modified classes: A Modified, B Modified, C Modified, F Modified, open cars in the Prepared category, D Modified, and E Modified. These "Modified" and "Prepared" classes are SCCA's most heavily-built, purpose-made-for-racing categories, well beyond a car with bolt-on street parts. Building a car into one of these classes is a major step beyond the stock or lightly-modified Fit this book focuses on.

If a roll bar is added to a car in one of the more modified categories, SCCA's Appendix C sets rules for it. The top of the bar cannot sit below the top of the driver's helmet. The rulebook also suggests it extend at least 3.0 inches (76.2 mm) above the helmet. It must use seamless steel tubing, not a bolted-together post design, and must attach to the car's structure at two points, forming a hoop shape.

NASA HPDE and Time Trial: what a stock Fit needs

For an HPDE or Time Trial day, NASA requires modern, good-condition factory seat belts, or a DOT-approved restraint system. DOT stands for the U.S. Department of Transportation, which sets safety standards for belts sold for street cars. A lap belt alone, with no shoulder strap, is not allowed. Your Fit's stock three-point belt already meets this rule, with no changes needed.

NASA also requires a helmet meeting Snell SA2010 or newer, or an equivalent standard, for both the driver and any passenger. Eye protection like goggles or a face shield is recommended, but is not a hard requirement the way the helmet is.

The roll bar rule that surprises a lot of people

Here is the most important, and most misunderstood, rule in this chapter. NASA's roll bar requirement for HPDE and Time Trial depends entirely on whether your car is open or closed. It does not depend on how fast you drive, or which run group you are in. NASA's rulebook states that open cars, meaning convertibles or open T-tops, should have a roll bar installed. Nothing in NASA's rules ties a roll bar requirement to a driver's lap time or top speed. Your base 2008 Fit is a closed hardtop with no factory open-top version. It does not need a roll bar for any NASA HPDE or Time Trial run group, no matter how fast you get.

Many drivers assume that going faster, or moving into a higher run group, eventually triggers a roll bar requirement. That assumption is wrong for both organizations covered in this chapter. The real trigger is always event type and car body style, never a specific speed or lap time.

There is one thing that does change your equipment needs on a closed car: adding a hard-shell, fixed-back racing seat. NASA's rulebook says stock belts should not be worn with that kind of seat, so a car with one needs a harness meeting a specific certification standard instead. This does not apply to your Fit's stock seats. It only matters if you later install an aftermarket racing seat.

Wheel-to-wheel racing: the one place equipment is always mandatory

Once you move beyond HPDE and Time Trial into wheel-to-wheel Competition — racing where more than one car is on the track at once, with finishing positions — the rules change completely. A full roll cage is required in every car, with no exception for car type or speed. The cage must meet detailed construction standards. It needs a continuous main hoop close to the roof, and a diagonal brace. Padding must cover anywhere your head or limbs could hit it. The cage also needs a minimum of six mounting points to the car's floor or rocker panels. A 5-, 6-, or 7-point harness, certified to a specific SFI or FIA standard, is also required, and that certification carries a hard expiration date with no exceptions.

Building a Fit to this level is a large step past autocross or an HPDE day, and beyond the scope of a first project car. If you ever consider it, treat this chapter as only a starting point, and work from the full current rulebook.

Quick check

  1. Does a stock, closed 2008 Fit need a roll bar for SCCA autocross? Why or why not?
  2. What is the real trigger for NASA's roll bar requirement at an HPDE or Time Trial day — speed, lap time, or something else?
  3. Under SCCA's rules, when is an aftermarket harness legal, and why can you not install one in your Fit as it comes from the factory?
  4. What is the difference between a roll bar and a roll cage?
  5. What piece of aftermarket equipment can require a harness on a closed car, even without a roll bar rule forcing it?

Part IXGetting Ready for the Track

Chapter 58Autocross

What you will learn

  • What autocross is and how it works
  • What SCCA class a stock 2008 base Fit runs in
  • What kinds of changes move your Fit into a faster class
  • Whether an older Fit is still allowed to compete
  • What to know before your first event

The short version

Autocross is a timed event where you drive one car at a time through a course marked out with cones, trying to beat the clock without hitting anything. A stock 2008 base Fit currently runs in SCCA's H Street class. Adding suspension, brake, or wheel and tire upgrades moves it up into a faster class, and adding race tires and engine tuning moves it up again.

The full explanation

What autocross actually is

SCCA, the Sports Car Club of America, calls its autocross program "Solo." In Solo, you drive one car at a time through a course laid out with cones on a large paved lot, usually a parking lot or an unused runway. You are timed, not racing side by side against another driver, so your only competition is the clock and everyone else's best run of the day. Cars are sorted into classes so that similar cars compete against each other, which is why knowing your class matters.

Your Fit's class: H Street

The current SCCA National Solo rulebook lists the Fit in H Street, written as HS. This classing was checked in two separate rulebook editions and is identical in both, so it has not changed recently. Street is SCCA's entry-level category. The rulebook itself calls it "the lowest barrier of entry." It allows only limited changes: mainly shocks, anti-roll bars — suspension parts that resist body lean in turns — and replacement tires. Beyond those changes, a Street-classed Fit is expected to stay close to how it left the factory.

Your 2008 model-year Fit is still fully eligible to compete. SCCA's own age rule allows a car to run in National events through the end of the 30th calendar year after its model year. For a 2008 car, that rule stays in effect through 2038, so age is not a concern for many years to come. Regional-only Solo events do not use this same age limit at all.

Moving up a class: Street Touring and Street Prepared

If you add the kinds of parts Street category does not allow, your Fit moves into a different class instead of staying in H Street. The next class up is E Street Touring, written EST. SCCA describes Street Touring as sitting between Street and the more heavily modified Street Prepared category. Moving into EST means adding aftermarket suspension parts, like shocks, springs, or sway bars. It can also mean a different differential — the part that lets your two drive wheels spin at different speeds through a turn — bolt-on engine parts, or a bigger brake kit. Wider or different-sized wheels and tires than Street category allows are permitted too. A car in this class is still expected to stay street-legal. It must keep its emissions equipment and OBD-II system — the car's onboard diagnostic computer, which tracks emissions and engine problems — working. Its factory anti-lock brakes must also keep working, though they may be turned off electronically.

Going further still moves your Fit into F Street Prepared, written FSP. Street Prepared builds on everything Street Touring allows, then adds a further step that is not really meant for driving on public roads. This includes race-compatible tires and permanent bodywork changes, such as cutting or flaring fenders for tire clearance. It also includes front splitters, rear spoilers, and an aftermarket ECU — an aftermarket computer that controls engine tuning — for engine tuning. Emissions equipment can be removed, and the intake and exhaust become unrestricted. A Street Prepared car is also allowed further weight-reduction changes, like removing the air conditioning system, and more suspension upgrades than Street Touring allows.

Put together, the modification ladder for your Fit looks like this. Street or H Street keeps the car stock, aside from shocks, sway bars, and tires. Street Touring or EST adds bolt-on suspension, brake, and wheel upgrades while staying street-legal. Street Prepared or FSP adds race tires, tuning, and body changes, and is no longer necessarily street-legal.

Getting ready for your first event

Before your first event, confirm which class you plan to run. If your Fit is stock, or close to it, that class is H Street. Read Chapter 57 for what your Fit needs, and does not need, in terms of seat belts, harnesses, and helmets. Read Chapter 60 for a full checklist of what to bring and do the night before, the morning of, and between runs. Knowing your class ahead of time means you register correctly and compete against the right group of cars from your very first run.

Quick check

  1. What class does a stock 2008 base Fit run in under SCCA's current rulebook?
  2. What is the difference between Street, Street Touring, and Street Prepared, in terms of what each one allows you to change?
  3. Through what year does SCCA's age rule keep a 2008 model-year car eligible for National events?
  4. Name two changes that would move your Fit from H Street into E Street Touring.
  5. Where in this book should you look for the safety-gear rules and the full event-day checklist?

Chapter 59HPDE / track days

What you will learn

  • What HPDE means and how it differs from racing
  • What NASA's tech inspection checks on the car itself
  • What safety and general-condition items tech inspection checks
  • What the actual paper tech form looks like
  • The most common reasons cars fail tech inspection

The short version

HPDE stands for High Performance Driving Event, a track day where you drive your own car on a real track to build skill, not to race other drivers. Before you can drive, your car has to pass a tech inspection covering its mechanical condition and its safety equipment. A well-maintained, stock Fit with good tires and brakes should pass without trouble.

The full explanation

What tech inspection checks on the car itself

NASA's Club Codes and Regulations, the rulebook covering NASA's HPDE program, lists specific mechanical items every car must pass. Wheels and tires must show no cracked or damaged wheels and no exposed cords or bubbles on the tire, with every lug nut present and tight. Steering and suspension get checked for tightness, since very little or no play is allowed in the wheel bearings, suspension, or steering. The engine bay must show no significant fluid leaks from the engine, radiator, or hoses, and hoses must be in good condition with no cracks. The radiator needs an overflow container holding at least one liter. Any oil breather or vent line must either return to the engine, or route to a catch tank of at least one liter. Brakes must work well enough to stop the car in a reasonable, safe distance, with adequate pedal pressure. Fluid must sit at or above the minimum level, and brake lines must be in good condition. The car's general appearance also gets checked against what the rulebook calls the "50/50 rule": it should look undamaged and straight at 50 mph from 50 feet away.

What tech inspection checks beyond the mechanicals

Tech inspection also covers safety items that are not part of the car's mechanical systems. Seatbelts and harnesses must be in good, undamaged, factory configuration, or use an approved aftermarket harness if the car has an aftermarket hard-shell seat. Roll bars are required on all open cars, but not on a closed car like your Fit. The battery must be securely fastened — a bungee cord or rubber cord alone is not acceptable — and its positive terminal must be covered with a non-conductive material. The fuel cap must seal well enough that fuel does not spill under hard driving. No exposed, live wiring is allowed inside the cabin, and seats must be securely fastened to the car's structure. Every loose object must come out of the passenger compartment before you go on track. At least one working rear-view mirror and at least two working brake lights, visible from 300 feet, are both required. Tow eyes — front and rear hooks a tow truck can safely pull from — are strongly recommended, though not always required.

On the process itself, you review the Technical Inspection Form before the event, then either have the car pre-teched at an authorized shop or teched at the track. Once the form is signed, you place it on the dash or windshield so a grid marshal can collect it and apply a tech sticker.

The real tech form, item by item

A real NASA HPDE/Time Trial Technical Inspection Form organizes its checklist into five boxes. Wheels and tires covers tire condition, all lug nuts present and tight, and no hubcaps or beauty rings left on. Steering and suspension covers front and rear wheel-bearing play and steering play. Engine covers fluid leaks from oil, fuel, or water, the radiator overflow and hose condition, and a battery secured with no bungee cords and covered terminals. Brakes covers pedal pressure, fluid level, line condition, and brake lights. Safety Equipment covers roll bars on open cars, seat belts or a harness, a Snell SA2005-or-newer helmet, and a covered battery if it sits inside the cockpit. Miscellaneous covers a secured gas cap, no exposed wires, and seats bolted down tight.

The form also reminds you to put contrasting-color numbers, at least 10 inches tall, on both sides of the car. It tells you to remove all loose objects, including the spare tire and floor mats. Finally, place your tech sticker at the top center of the windshield. The most common first-timer tech-inspection failures are worn brake pads, a loose battery, corded or cracked tires, leaking fluids, loose wheel bearings, and a missing tow hook. Inspect your car several days before the event, not the morning of, so you have time to fix or order parts for anything you find.

Quick check

  1. What does HPDE stand for, and how is it different from wheel-to-wheel racing?
  2. Name three mechanical items NASA's tech inspection checks under the car's hood.
  3. Does a stock, closed Fit need a roll bar to pass tech inspection? Why or why not?
  4. What are the five checklist boxes on a real NASA tech inspection form?
  5. What are the most common reasons a first-timer's car fails tech inspection, and how can you avoid them?

Chapter 60Track-day prep checklist

What you will learn

  • What to do the night before a track day
  • What to check and bring the morning of the event
  • What to check between track sessions
  • Why finishing car prep early matters more than doing it at the last minute

The short version

Finish your car prep several days before the event, not the night before, and pack your gear bag completely so the morning is simple. On the morning of, check your cold tire pressure and dress for the day's required clothing. Between sessions, recheck tire pressure and wheel torque, and take time to eat, drink, and rest.

The full explanation

The night before

Finish any car repairs or upgrades several days ahead of the event, not the night before. A last-minute repair that goes wrong, like a broken bolt or a surprise problem, is one of the most common ways a driver arrives at the track exhausted and unprepared. The night before, pack your car and gear bag completely, so the morning of the event stays simple. Pack your helmet and gloves, your completed or shop-signed tech inspection form, a pen, and a tire pressure gauge. Also pack a small tool bag with a jack, a wheel-lock key if your car uses one, and zip ties. Also pack a filled gas container if the track does not sell fuel, and a cooler with water or a sports drink and food. Bring cash for tow-truck tips or tolls, and a toll transponder if you will drive toll roads to get there. Many event organizers hold check-in the evening before the event. Attending it avoids a morning-of line for waivers, the tech form, car numbers, and your event wristband or tech sticker.

The morning of

Check your tire pressures while the tires are still cold, right at the start of the day. Tire pressure rises once the tires warm up on track. Note your cold baseline before you leave, so you can adjust it correctly later. Confirm the car has no new fluid leaks and no dashboard warning lights before you leave home. Dress for the event's required clothing rather than showing up in shorts and sandals. Many organizations, including NASA, require long sleeves and pants, and closed-toe shoes. Bring sunscreen, comfortable weather-appropriate clothing, snacks, and plenty of water. A good rule of thumb is roughly one quart of fluid for every 50 pounds of body weight over a full track day. Heat and nervous energy both increase how much fluid you lose. Arrive with enough time before the mandatory drivers' meeting to unload, get through registration and tech, and not feel rushed.

Between sessions

After each session, recheck tire pressure, and tire temperature too if you have a temperature gun on hand. Track surface temperature and how hard you drive both change your tire pressure through the day. Recheck your wheel and lug torque periodically, since track driving can work fasteners loose faster than everyday street driving. Top off engine oil if your car is a known oil-consumer, or at least check the level, using oil and a funnel packed for exactly this purpose. Use the break between sessions to rehydrate, eat something, and rest in the shade, rather than only working on the car. Staying hydrated and fed directly affects how well, and how safely, you drive later in the day. Many organized programs hold an instructor debrief or driver meeting between sessions. Taking a few notes on what changed between sessions is a widely repeated habit. A braking point, an odd noise, or a new tire pressure are all worth writing down, for tracking progress and problems across the day.

Quick check

  1. Why should you finish car repairs several days before the event instead of the night before?
  2. Name three things you should pack in your gear bag the night before.
  3. Why do you check tire pressure while the tires are still cold in the morning?
  4. What should you check on your wheels between sessions, and why does track driving make this more important than usual?
  5. Besides working on the car, what should you do during the break between sessions?

Chapter 61Driver skills

What you will learn

  • Where to look with your eyes while driving on track
  • Why smooth steering and pedal inputs make you faster, not just safer
  • What trail braking means and why drivers use it
  • How these skills connect to what you already do behind the wheel

The short version

Look far ahead, toward the path you want, not at a hazard or the track's edge. Smooth, gentle steering and pedal inputs keep more grip in your tires than sudden, jerky ones do. Trail braking means letting off the brake gradually as you steer into a corner, instead of coming off it all at once beforehand.

The full explanation

Where to look

A well-known professional driving coach's free instructional material teaches vision as one of the very first skills to learn, ahead of lines or braking technique. Train your eyes to look far down the track, not just at the pavement right in front of your car. Point your eyes toward the path you want the car to take. Avoid fixing your gaze on a hazard or the track's outer edge, since your eyes tend to pull the car toward wherever they are aimed. Turning your head to see into a corner, instead of keeping it pointed straight ahead, helps you spot that path earlier. Treat this as a constant scanning habit. Always pick up your next reference point further down the track, rather than glancing once and then forgetting about it. Your overall awareness of the drive shapes how well you react. That means what you see, what you feel through the seat as the car grips or slides, and what you hear from the engine and tires.

Why smoothness matters

The same source teaches keeping your hands near the 9-and-3 o'clock position on the wheel, like the hands of a clock, through most corners on most tracks. A car that needs less wheel movement tends to be smoother and more predictable to drive. Keeping your hand movements small is directly tied to how smooth, and ultimately how fast, you can drive.

Here is why smoothness matters so much: weight transfer. Weight transfer is the way a car's weight shifts from one end, or one side, to the other as you brake, speed up, or turn. Braking shifts weight toward the front tires. Speeding up shifts it toward the rear. Turning shifts it toward the outside tires. A sudden, jerky input causes a bigger, more sudden weight shift than the same input made gradually. A tire's grip does not grow at the same rate as the extra load it carries. That is why a car actually loses more overall grip from a jerky input than a smooth one. That is the real reason smooth driving is fast driving, not only safer driving.

Trail braking, explained simply

Trail braking means gradually letting off the brake pedal with your foot as you steer into a corner, instead of coming completely off the brake before you begin turning. This trade swaps some of the brake's stopping force for turning force, right as the corner asks for it. Braking keeps extra weight, and therefore extra grip, on your front tires right when you are also asking them to change the car's direction. Coming off the brake gradually, instead of all at once, helps keep the front tires closer to their best grip as you turn in. Many quick drivers pay less attention to their braking point, and more attention to exactly when they finish coming off the brake. A smooth, late release keeps the car balanced, and keeps your eyes up on the corner instead of down at your feet.

Putting it together

These three skills work as a set, not on their own. Looking far ahead gives you the time you need to make your steering and pedal inputs smooth, instead of rushed. Smooth inputs keep your tires gripping their best, which is exactly what trail braking is trying to use as you enter a corner. None of these skills require a faster car. They only take practice, and they work just as well in a stock Fit as in any other car.

Quick check

  1. Where should your eyes look while driving on track, and why does this matter?
  2. What is weight transfer, and how does it connect braking, speeding up, and turning to your tires' grip?
  3. Why is a smooth input often faster than a jerky one, not just safer?
  4. What is trail braking, in your own words?
  5. Why do many faster drivers pay more attention to how they finish releasing the brake than to where they start braking?

Chapter 62After the track

What you will learn

  • What to do to your car right after you finish your last session
  • Why you should avoid the parking brake right after hard driving
  • What to check before you drive home
  • What to check once you get home, for a fuller post-event inspection

The short version

Take a slow cool-down lap at the end of your last session, instead of braking hard and parking right away. Once the car has cooled, recheck your tire pressure, tread, and brake pedal feel before you drive home. At home, check for fresh leaks, fluid levels, pad wear, tire wear, and lug torque before you put the car back into everyday use.

The full explanation

Right after your last session

Drive one slower, gentle lap at the end of your last session, instead of braking hard and parking the car right away. This cool-down lap lets air move through the brakes, engine, and transmission, bringing their temperatures down gradually rather than all at once. Avoid using the parking brake, also called the hand brake, right after a hot session. A very hot brake rotor, clamped tight by the parking brake, can warp or damage the pad or rotor as it cools. Let the car cool with the parking brake off instead.

Before you drive home

Once the car and its tires have cooled, recheck your tire pressure and tread condition before you leave for home. Pressures measured right after a hot session read higher than your correct driving pressure, so wait until the tires cool before trusting the number. A hard session can also reveal cuts, exposed cords, or uneven wear that were not there that morning. Check your brakes too: the pedal should still feel firm, not soft or noticeably longer in travel than it felt that morning. Brake fluid can boil or fade under sustained hard track use, and a spongy or long pedal is a sign that has happened.

The fuller inspection, once you get home

Look under the car and around the engine bay, on clean, dry ground, for any fresh drips of oil, coolant, or brake fluid that were not there before the event. Check that your engine oil, coolant, and brake fluid are all still at the correct levels. A hard day of high-RPM driving and heavy braking uses up and stresses fluids faster than ordinary street driving does. Check the visible brake pad thickness at each wheel, and look for fresh cracks, heavy discoloration, or fluid residue near the calipers. Pad wear is one of the fastest-consumed items on a track day, so this check matters even if the car seemed fine on the drive home. Inspect all four tires for uneven wear, cuts, or a change in the tread surface. Re-torque every wheel's lug nuts once the car and wheels are fully cool. Track driving's repeated heating and cooling, combined with harder cornering loads, can work fasteners loose faster than normal driving does. Finally, walk around the car and listen for anything that came loose — in the engine bay, under the car, or inside. Check trim panels, undertray fasteners, and exhaust hangers before you put the car back into everyday use.

Quick check

  1. Why should you drive a slower cool-down lap instead of stopping and parking right away?
  2. Why should you avoid using the parking brake right after a hard session?
  3. Why does tire pressure right after a session not tell you the correct driving pressure?
  4. Name three fluids you should check the level of once you get home.
